  findings: In a framed field experiment with Turkish small-scale fishers, four treatments were compared: baseline (open-access), face-to-face communication, monetary penalty (punishment for exceeding quota), and purchase of fishing rights (market-based over-quota payment). All groups played a linear PGG extraction game for 15 rounds (pre-treatment), then 14 rounds under one of the four treatments. The monetary penalty (punishment) significantly reduced extraction and moderately increased group earnings compared to baseline, but the effect was smaller than that of communication, which yielded the highest earnings and lowest inequality. The purchase of fishing rights (identical cost to penalty) did not significantly reduce extraction or increase earnings. Thus, punishment can improve efficiency, but its effect is sensitive to framing and is less than that of informal communication.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that introducing a monetary penalty (punishment) in a linear PGG extraction game with small groups (n=5) and low MPCR (0.4) increases group efficiency (earnings) relative to open-access, but the effect is moderate. The effect is smaller than that of enabling face-to-face communication. The framing of the punishment matters: a market-based 'purchase of rights' with identical cost is less effective than a penalty. For prediction, this suggests that punishment will generally increase efficiency over baseline, but the magnitude depends on the punishment's framing and the presence of communication. Control efficiency in this context is already above the selfish prediction, so the incremental gain from punishment is moderate. The results are most applicable to small groups, continuous extraction, no chat in baseline/punishment, and low but certain punishment.

- source: Ambrus, A., & Greiner, B. (2019). Individual, Dictator, and Democratic punishment in public good games with perfect and imperfect observability. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper experimentally compares three punishment institutions (individual, democratic, dictator) in repeated 5-person public goods games, under both perfect and imperfect monitoring. Democratic punishment, where a majority vote is required to punish, results in significantly higher average net profits (efficiency) and higher contributions than individual or dictator punishment. This is true in both monitoring conditions, though the efficiency advantage is somewhat reduced under noise. The improvement is due to a sharp reduction in anti-social punishment (punishing cooperators), while pro-social punishment (punishing defectors) is maintained. Dictator punishment does not outperform individual punishment in terms of efficiency. The study provides direct, quantitative evidence on the effect of punishment institution and monitoring noise on group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong, directly relevant evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated public goods games. It shows that the institutional design of punishment (democratic vs. individual vs. dictator) and the observability of contributions (perfect vs. noisy) are key moderators. Democratic punishment substantially increases efficiency compared to individual punishment, especially by reducing anti-social punishment. However, the efficiency gain is somewhat sensitive to monitoring noise. The paper provides exact values for average net profits under each condition, allowing for quantitative calibration of prediction models. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, observability, etc.) are all clearly specified, making the results highly transferable to similar prediction tasks.

- source: Rockenbach, B., & Wolff, I. (2016). Designing Institutions for Social Dilemmas. *German Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that when subjects are allowed to design institutions for a repeated public goods game, they initially favor punishment or redistribution mechanisms, but these are associated with lower efficiency (group payoff relative to the social optimum) compared to rules that emphasize communication (framing, appeals) or participative elements (endogenous rule change). Over the course of the experiment, the use of punishment declines, and efficiency increases, suggesting that punishment is not an efficient solution in this context. Communication and participative rule features are positively associated with efficiency. The study provides direct evidence that punishment, as typically implemented by endogenous designers, tends to reduce efficiency, mainly due to its costs, and that designers learn to move away from it.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard repeated public goods game with endogenous institution design, enabling punishment mechanisms tends to reduce efficiency relative to the control (no-punishment) baseline, especially when punishment is costly and not peer-administered. The effect is robust across multiple rounds and rule sets, with efficiency increasing as designers move away from punishment toward communication and participative mechanisms. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, all else equal, enabling punishment (especially costly, centralized, or automatic forms) is likely to result in lower efficiency than the control, unless the punishment is highly refined or replaced by redistribution mechanisms with lower waste. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment in similar PGG environments.

- source: Waichman, I., & Stenzel, L. (2019). When Punishment Strikes Late: The Effect of a Delay in Punishment and Punishment Feedback on Cooperation and Efficiency. *Journal of Neuroscience Psychology and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that peer punishment increases both cooperation and efficiency in a linear public good game compared to a no-punishment baseline. This effect holds even when punishment is delayed by five periods, as long as participants receive salient feedback linking punishment to their past contributions. If this feedback is absent (i.e., participants are punished after a delay and are not reminded of their past behavior), punishment still increases cooperation but does not improve efficiency over the no-punishment baseline, due to increased punishment costs and less targeted norm enforcement. The effectiveness of punishment in raising efficiency thus depends on the informational environment, specifically whether punished individuals can clearly associate punishment with their own prior actions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard linear PGG with four players and 20 rounds, enabling peer punishment increases efficiency relative to no-punishment, provided that feedback links punishment to past contributions. The effect is robust to a five-period delay in punishment, as long as feedback is salient. If the design omits such feedback, punishment may still increase cooperation but not efficiency, due to higher punishment costs and less effective norm targeting. For prediction, the presence or absence of feedback linking punishment to behavior is a critical moderator. Control efficiency (no-punishment) is lower than treatment efficiency (with punishment and feedback), but not when feedback is absent. Other design dimensions (player count, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, etc.) are held constant in this study, so the main moderator evidenced is the feedback/identification of norm violation.

- source: Kingsley, D. C., & Brown, T. C. (2016). Endogenous and costly institutional deterrence in a public good experiment. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a linear public goods game with a central authority that can monitor and punish free-riders, increasing the probability of monitoring (deterrence) increases contributions up to a point, but the benefit to group earnings depends on the cost of monitoring. When monitoring is cheap or moderate, higher deterrence increases both contributions and efficiency (group earnings as a share of the social optimum). When monitoring is expensive, the cost can outweigh the benefit, and efficiency does not improve or may decline. Allowing groups to endogenously select the level of monitoring does not increase contributions or efficiency at non-deterrent (low monitoring) levels, contrary to some previous literature. In fact, endogenous selection of non-deterrent institutions sometimes reduces contributions. The relationship between monitoring, punishment, and efficiency is thus strongly moderated by the costliness of institutional enforcement.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment-enabled institutions on efficiency in public goods games, especially when the punishment is implemented via a central authority with costly monitoring. The results show that the efficiency gain from punishment depends on both the probability of monitoring (deterrence) and the cost of monitoring. At low and moderate costs, enabling punishment increases efficiency, but at high costs, the efficiency gain is negated or reversed. The endogenous selection of non-deterrent institutions does not provide an efficiency boost. For prediction, the key moderators are the cost of monitoring (punishment_cost), the effectiveness of monitoring (punishment_tech), and whether the institution is deterrent (i.e., the expected cost of free-riding exceeds the benefit). Control efficiency (without punishment) is much lower than with effective, affordable punishment, but high punishment costs can erase the efficiency gain.

- source: Bühren, C., & Dannenberg, A. (2021). The demand for punishment to promote cooperation among like-minded people. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that the efficiency impact of punishment in public goods games is highly contingent on group type and information. Highly cooperative groups achieve near-optimal payoffs without punishment, and introducing punishment reduces efficiency due to its cost. Uncooperative groups only benefit from severe punishment if they are informed about their own composition and implement the institution early; otherwise, punishment is often misapplied or subverted, leading to low efficiency. Middle cooperators benefit from punishment only in the absence of information about group composition. Information about group cooperativeness allows groups to better match their institution choice to their needs, increasing overall efficiency. The study provides direct evidence that the efficiency effect of punishment is not uniform and is moderated by group composition and information.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the efficiency effect of punishment in public goods games is not a simple function of the game design dimensions alone, but is critically moderated by group composition (cooperativeness) and information about that composition. For prediction tasks, this means that the same punishment institution can have positive, negative, or null effects on efficiency depending on whether the group is composed of high, middle, or low cooperators, and whether they know this. In the absence of such information, punishment is often over- or under-used, reducing efficiency. The design dimensions most relevant for prediction from this paper are the presence and cost of punishment, the severity/technology of punishment, the availability of information about group composition, and the baseline cooperativeness of the group. Control efficiency (no punishment) is a strong predictor: if control efficiency is high, adding punishment reduces efficiency; if control efficiency is low, adding severe punishment with information can increase efficiency. The paper also highlights the importance of comprehension and early institution choice for realizing efficiency gains from punishment.

- source: Burton-Chellew, M. N., & Guérin, C. (2021). Decoupling cooperation and punishment in humans shows that punishment is not an altruistic trait. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study shows that in a standard repeated public goods game with punishment, contributions remain stable when all can punish and be punished. However, when some individuals are made immune to punishment, these immune individuals (even those classified as conditional cooperators) learn to contribute less over time, despite being able to punish others and being surrounded by high contributors. Punishment is frequent but often hypocritical or anti-social among immune individuals, and overall, the costs of punishment are greater than the benefits from increased contributions, making punishment destructive to group welfare. The results indicate that punishment does not reliably increase efficiency or group payoff, and that the link between cooperation and punishment is not altruistic but may be driven by confusion and learning.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, this paper provides strong evidence that punishment can be destructive: the costs of punishment can outweigh any gains from increased cooperation, leading to lower overall efficiency. The design closely matches standard PGGs with linear returns, four players, repeated rounds, and costly punishment (1 MU cost to reduce 3 MU from another). The paper does not report efficiency directly, but states that punishment is 'ultimately destructive' in terms of group welfare. Therefore, when using game design dimensions similar to this study (small groups, linear PGG, costly punishment, no communication, anonymous punishment), one should expect that enabling punishment may not increase, and may even decrease, group efficiency relative to the control. The findings are especially relevant for settings where punishment is frequent and costly, and where the marginal per capita return is less than one.

- source: Wang, C. Q., & Huang, C. C. (2022). Between local and global strategy updating in public goods game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Introducing the option for costly punishment in a repeated public goods game increases group contributions and group payoffs compared to the no-punishment baseline. This effect holds both when sanctions are observable (feedback given immediately) and when sanctions are unobservable (feedback delayed until the end). The mechanism is that subjects expect free riders to be punished and adjust their behavior to avoid being punished, even when they do not observe sanctions in real time. Sanctioning is mainly non-strategic and norm-driven, targeting free riders. The presence of punishment stabilizes cooperation and prevents the unraveling of contributions, with some groups achieving high cooperation and others not. The overall effect of punishment on efficiency (as measured by group payoffs and contributions as a fraction of maximum) is positive.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that enabling costly punishment in a standard linear public goods game increases group efficiency (payoff) compared to the no-punishment baseline. The effect is robust to whether punishment is observable or not during play, suggesting that the mere possibility of punishment, and the associated social norm, is sufficient to increase cooperation and efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that adding punishment to a repeated linear PGG with 4 players, 10 rounds, and MPCR=0.4 will increase efficiency, even if punishment is not immediately observable. The magnitude of the effect may vary by group, but the direction is reliably positive. The paper also provides parameter values for key design dimensions.

- source: Drouvelis, M., Malaeb, B., Vlassopoulos, M., & Wahba, J. (2021). Cooperation in a fragmented society: Experimental evidence on Syrian refugees and natives in Lebanon. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that: (1) Homogeneous groups (same nationality) contribute more to the public good than mixed groups, and this is mainly due to Lebanese participants reducing contributions in mixed groups. (2) Punishment is used more in homogeneous groups, and there is substantial antisocial punishment, especially among Lebanese. (3) The opportunity to punish does not increase contributions overall; in fact, average contributions decrease in the punishment stage for Syrians and do not change significantly for Lebanese or mixed groups. (4) Average earnings after the contribution stage are higher in homogeneous groups, but total earnings (including punishment costs) are not significantly different between treatments, as mixed groups assign less punishment and thus incur lower punishment costs. (5) The presence of punishment does not remedy the lower cooperation in mixed groups. (6) Behavior in mixed groups is more conditionally cooperative, while homogeneous groups show more unconditional cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence on the effect of punishment in a two-player repeated linear public goods game with a 0.75 MPCR and a 1:3 punishment cost-to-impact ratio. The main finding is that the introduction of punishment does not increase efficiency or average earnings; in fact, contributions do not rise and may even fall, especially due to antisocial punishment. While punishment increases costs (especially in homogeneous groups), it does not lead to higher total payoffs. Thus, for similar game designs (small groups, moderate MPCR, punishment with 1:3 cost-impact, no communication), one should not expect punishment to increase efficiency, especially in settings with potential for antisocial punishment. The paper also highlights that group composition (in-group vs. out-group) can moderate both cooperation and punishment, but the overall effect of punishment on efficiency is neutral or negative in this context.

- source: Harrell, A., & Wolff, T. (2023). Cooperation in Networked Collective-Action Groups: Information Access and Norm Enforcement in Groups of Different Sizes. *Social Psychology Quarterly*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This experiment demonstrates that punishment opportunities in public goods games (PGGs) increase cooperation and, over time, group earnings, especially in large and densely connected groups. In information-only conditions (no punishment), dense networks increase cooperation in small groups but not in large groups, where dense information can actually decrease cooperation. When punishment is enabled, dense networks increase cooperation in both small and large groups, but the effect is especially strong in large groups. Although punishment is initially costly and reduces earnings, the resulting increase in cooperation leads to higher group profits in later rounds. The study provides direct evidence that the efficiency gains from punishment depend on both group size and network density.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in PGGs increases group efficiency (as measured by group earnings/profits) over time, particularly in large and densely connected groups. For prediction tasks, the presence of punishment should be expected to have a more positive effect on efficiency in large groups with dense networks than in small or sparsely connected groups. The effect is less pronounced or even negative in large groups without punishment, and in small groups, dense information alone can suffice to sustain cooperation. The findings suggest that the interaction between group size, network density, and punishment availability is critical for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: DeAngelo, G., & Gee, L. K. (2020). Peers or police?: The effect of choice and type of monitoring in the provision of public goods. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This laboratory experiment uses a 2x2 design to compare exogenous vs. endogenous monitoring and peer vs. group monitoring in a linear public goods game with punishment. When monitoring is exogenously imposed, both peer and group monitoring yield similar public good provision and net earnings. When monitoring is endogenously chosen, group monitoring collapses (due to free riders refusing to pay for monitoring), leading to a 44% drop in public good provision, but net earnings are not significantly lower because less is spent on punishment. Peer monitoring, however, is robust to endogenization: public good provision and net earnings remain high, and willingness-to-pay for monitoring is not strongly correlated with contribution level. The results are robust to changes in the punishment mechanism and the voting rule for group monitoring. The key implication is that the effectiveness of punishment in improving efficiency depends on the monitoring regime and whether monitoring is exogenous or endogenous.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, this paper provides strong evidence that the impact depends on the monitoring regime and whether monitoring is exogenously imposed or endogenously chosen. If monitoring is exogenous (automatic), punishment increases or maintains efficiency (via higher public good provision) regardless of whether monitoring is peer-based or group-based. If monitoring is endogenous and requires group consensus, punishment may fail to improve efficiency because monitoring is not implemented (especially in the presence of free riders), leading to low public good provision but not necessarily lower net earnings due to reduced punishment costs. Peer monitoring is robust to endogenization: punishment maintains efficiency even when monitoring is endogenous. Thus, when using game design dimensions to predict treatment efficiency, it is crucial to account for whether monitoring is exogenous or endogenous and the type of monitoring (peer vs. group). Control efficiency (without punishment) is not directly reported, but the paper provides sufficient data to estimate the relative effect of punishment under different monitoring regimes.

- source: Lefebvre, M., & Stenger, A. (2020). Short- & long-term effects of monetary and non-monetary incentives to cooperate in public good games: An experiment. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Monetary and non-monetary punishments and rewards all significantly increase contributions to the public good compared to baseline. Monetary punishment and non-monetary reward are the most effective. After incentives are removed, contributions remain higher than baseline for several rounds, indicating some long-lasting effect, but eventually decline. The effect of punishment and reward on later contributions depends on whether individuals were high or low contributors and how many points they received. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, so the findings are limited to contribution behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment (especially monetary punishment) increases contribution rates in a standard linear public goods game with four players and 30 rounds. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results are highly informative for predicting contribution behavior, but less so for efficiency unless one assumes a direct mapping from contribution to efficiency (which is not always valid, especially when punishment is costly). The design dimensions are well specified, so the paper is useful for understanding the behavioral response to punishment and reward mechanisms in similar lab PGGs.

- source: Kumakawa, T. (2013). Social Rewards and Sanctions: A Voluntary Contribution Mechanism Experiment. *Journal of Neuroscience Psychology and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that in a two-player public goods game, the opportunity to send social (free-form) or monetary rewards/sanctions does not increase first-round contributions compared to a baseline with no such opportunity. However, when participants actually experience social sanctions (i.e., receive a negative message from their partner), their contributions in the next round increase significantly. Social rewards (positive messages) do not have a significant effect. Monetary sanctions and rewards are rarely used and do not significantly affect subsequent contributions. The study does not report group efficiency or payoff outcomes, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a two-player public goods game with a game-theoretic payoff table and no prior knowledge of a second round, social sanctions (blame via free-form messages) can increase subsequent contributions, but only after they are actually experienced. The mere opportunity for sanctions or rewards does not affect cooperation. However, the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are most relevant for predicting behavioral (contribution) responses to social sanctions, not efficiency outcomes. The design (two players, two rounds, no chat except for the sanction/reward stage, full information about partner's contribution, and a linear MPCR of 0.7) should be considered when generalizing to other settings.

- source: Chapkovski, P. (2021). Strike one hundred to educate one: Measuring the efficacy of collective sanctions experimentally. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Collective sanctions are not effective at increasing cooperation or contributions in a public good game, and may even reduce them compared to individual sanctions, especially when peer punishment is available. The presence of peer punishment increases contributions overall, but under collective sanctions, contributions are significantly lower than under individual sanctions with peer punishment. Average earnings are slightly lower under collective sanctions, but this difference is not statistically significant. There are notable gender differences: men contribute more under collective sanctions, women less. Perceptions of fairness mediate these effects, with collective sanctions seen as less fair, especially by women.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a standard PGG with 3 players, 15 rounds, continuous contributions, and a fixed threshold for external punishment, enabling collective sanctions (punishing the whole group for one member's under-contribution) does not improve efficiency or group payoff compared to individual sanctions, and may even reduce cooperation and contributions, especially when peer punishment is also available. The presence of peer punishment increases contributions, but collective sanctions reduce this effect. The paper does not report efficiency as a ratio to the fully cooperative outcome, but average earnings are slightly lower under collective sanctions. For prediction tasks, this suggests that switching from individual to collective sanctions is unlikely to increase efficiency, and may decrease it, particularly in small groups with peer punishment. Gender composition may moderate effects.

- source: Paál, T., & Bereczkei, T. (2015). Punishment as a Means of Competition: Implications for Strong Reciprocity Theory. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a 10-round, 4-player PGG with a competitive, rank-based payoff and costly punishment enabled from round 6 onward, average contributions declined steadily and did not increase after punishment was introduced. Punishment was not targeted at low contributors but at those with higher accumulated profits, indicating strategic rather than norm-enforcing use. There was no association between contributions and punishment received or imposed. The results suggest that in competitive environments, punishment can become a tool for rivalry rather than for sustaining cooperation, and does not reverse the decline in cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that in a PGG with a competitive, tournament-style payoff structure and full information about others' profits, enabling punishment does not increase efficiency or group payoff. Instead, punishment is used for strategic competition, and cooperation continues to decline. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly context-dependent: in competitive, rank-based settings, punishment may not improve and may even undermine group efficiency. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, so this inference is based on contribution behavior and the described use of punishment.

- source: Harrell, A. (2018). Competition for Leadership Promotes Contributions to Collective Action. *Social Forces*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that the opportunity to compete for an elected leadership position (with punishment power) increases contributions to the public good before the leader is chosen, but only when voters can observe and use prior contribution behavior in their decision. After the leader is installed, groups with elected leaders maintain higher contributions than those with appointed leaders. The leader's use of punishment does not differ significantly between elected and appointed conditions. The main outcome is contribution rate; no explicit group payoff or efficiency measures are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the process of leader selection (election vs. appointment) and the visibility of contribution history can significantly affect contribution rates in public goods games with punishment. However, because the paper does not report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare outcomes, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency changes due to punishment. Its findings are most useful for predicting how leader selection mechanisms and information about contributions affect cooperation, which may indirectly inform efficiency predictions if contribution rates are assumed to map closely to efficiency in similar designs.

- source: Bond, R. M. (2019). Low-cost, high-impact altruistic punishment promotes cooperation cascades in human social networks. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in public goods games, the presence and structure of punishment (especially low-cost, high-impact punishment) increases individual contributions and the persistence and spread of cooperative behavior through the network. The effect of one player's contribution on another's is stronger and endures longer in the low-cost, high-impact punishment condition. However, the paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral measures (contribution and its network effects).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that low-cost, high-impact punishment increases individual contributions and the spread of cooperation in public goods games, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results suggest that punishment (especially when low-cost and high-impact) is likely to increase efficiency if higher contributions translate to higher group payoffs, but this is an inference rather than a direct finding. The paper is most useful for understanding how punishment design affects contribution behavior and its propagation, rather than direct efficiency outcomes.

- source: Espín, A. M., Brañas-Garza, P., Gamella, J. F., Herrmann, B., & Martín, J. (2022). Culture and group-functional punishment behaviour. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that Gitanos punish much less than non-Gitanos in homogeneous groups, but increase punishment (especially Gitano males) in mixed groups with non-Gitanos, targeting Gitano free-riders. Non-Gitanos punish more overall, and in mixed groups, non-Gitano males punish Gitano free-riders more harshly than non-Gitano free-riders. Antisocial punishment (punishing cooperators) is generally low, but somewhat higher when outgroup members are present. Gender moderates punishment: Gitano females almost never punish, while males increase punishment in mixed groups. Contribution rates are high and do not differ significantly by ethnicity or group composition. The paper does not analyze efficiency or group payoff as a main outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides detailed evidence on how punishment behavior varies by culture, gender, and group composition in a one-shot PGG with punishment. However, it does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare as outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design and behavioral results may be useful for understanding moderators of punishment behavior, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. If using this paper for prediction, one should note that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not measured, and that the main findings concern who punishes whom, not whether punishment increases group payoff.

- source: Otten, K., Buskens, V., Przepiorka, W., & Ellemers, N. (2021). Cooperation between newcomers and incumbents: The role of normative disagreements. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Normative disagreement between newcomers and incumbents in a public goods game with peer punishment does not reduce average contributions to the public good. While group identification and the emergence of a shared social norm are negatively affected by normative disagreement, cooperation (as measured by contribution rate) is maintained. Punishment is used as a norm enforcement tool, especially targeting low-contributing newcomers, who subsequently increase their contributions to match incumbent standards. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a 3-player repeated public goods game with peer punishment, normative disagreement does not reduce average contributions, and punishment is effective at norm enforcement. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design and findings are relevant for understanding behavioral mechanisms, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes. The game design dimensions are well specified, so the paper can inform the mapping from design to expected contribution rates, but not to efficiency.

- source: Noussair, C. N., Tucker, S., Xu, Y. L., & Breaban, A. (2024). The role of emotions in public goods games with and without punishment opportunities. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that both monetary and non-monetary punishment increase cooperation relative to a baseline with no punishment, with monetary punishment sustaining higher contributions over time. Emotional states, especially happiness and negative valence, are correlated with contribution and punishment behavior, but not directly with payoffs. The effect of punishment on future contributions is stronger for monetary punishment, and the emotional state of the recipient moderates the effect of punishment. The study does not report efficiency or group earnings, but the behavioral patterns are consistent with prior literature showing that punishment increases group welfare by increasing cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard repeated public goods game with four players and 10 rounds, both monetary and non-monetary punishment increase cooperation compared to a no-punishment baseline. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used to directly predict the change in efficiency from enabling punishment. The findings support the general expectation that punishment increases efficiency via increased cooperation, but for quantitative prediction, one must rely on prior studies that report payoff or efficiency outcomes. The design details (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, etc.) are clearly specified and match canonical PGG lab experiments.

- source: Ramalingam, A., & Stoddard, B. (2024). Does reducing inequality increase cooperation?. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study implements a 2x2 design varying punishment (present/absent) and instruction format (short/long, less/more explicit). In the punishment treatments, longer and more explicit instructions lead to higher and more stable contributions, with punishment more effectively targeted at low contributors. In the absence of punishment, instruction format has little effect on average contributions over time. Decision times are lower with more explicit instructions, indicating better comprehension. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined, but the results suggest that the effectiveness of punishment in raising contributions depends on subjects' comprehension of the social dilemma.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effectiveness of punishment in raising contributions in public goods games depends on participants' comprehension of the incentive structure, which can be improved by more explicit instructions. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the realized effect of punishment on efficiency may be moderated by factors affecting comprehension, such as instruction clarity. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, it cannot be used to directly predict efficiency changes due to punishment. Instead, it provides indirect support that punishment is more likely to increase efficiency when comprehension is high and punishment is well-targeted.

- source: Schlösser, T., Berger, S., & Fetchenhauer, D. (2018). Justice Sensitivity and Cooperation Dynamics in Repeated Public Good Games. *Social Justice Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that allowing costly punishment in a repeated public good game leads to a substantial increase in average contributions (from 9.18 to 18.34 tokens per round out of 20). This replicates the classic result that punishment options strongly promote cooperation. The effect of individual justice sensitivity on cooperation is strong in the absence of punishment but disappears when punishment is available, as cooperation becomes strategic to avoid sanctions. Punishment is used infrequently, but its mere availability is sufficient to sustain high cooperation. Although efficiency or group payoff is not directly reported, the high contribution rates in the punishment condition imply much higher group efficiency compared to the no-punishment condition.
  prediction_guidance: For the prediction task, this paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in a standard repeated public good game with four players, ten rounds, and a punishment technology similar to Fehr & Gächter (2002) leads to a large increase in group efficiency, as measured by average contributions. The control (no-punishment) condition yields about 46% of maximum possible contributions, while the punishment condition yields about 92%. This suggests that, for similar game designs, enabling punishment will approximately double efficiency relative to the control. The paper's design and results are highly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated linear PGGs with similar parameters.

- source: Kubena, A. A., Houdek, P., Lindová, J., Príplatová, L., & Flegr, J. (2014). Justine Effect: Punishment of the Unduly Self-Sacrificing Cooperative Individuals. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a 12-player PGG, the introduction of punishment leads to a U-shaped relationship between contribution and punishment received: both the lowest and highest contributors are punished, with the most severe punishment falling on the highest contributors (the 'Justine Effect'). Antisocial punishment is primarily carried out by free riders and low contributors. While the paper reports that average contributions nearly double when punishment is available, it does not analyze or report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, nor does it compare these outcomes between the punishment and no-punishment conditions. The focus is on the behavioral dynamics of punishment, not on payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the behavioral dynamics of punishment in large-group PGGs, especially regarding antisocial punishment and its targets. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or payoff-based outcomes, which are central to the downstream prediction task. The finding that punishment increases average contributions suggests a possible positive effect on efficiency, but the presence of antisocial punishment (which reduces the payoffs of high contributors) could offset these gains. Without explicit efficiency or payoff data, the paper's value for predicting treatment efficiency is limited to indirect inference based on contribution patterns and punishment behavior.

- source: Dickson, E. S., Gordon, S. C., & Huber, G. A. (2022). Identifying legitimacy: Experimental evidence on compliance with authority. *Science Advances*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study demonstrates that a central authority's costly action to improve the fairness of punishment (procedural fairness) increases citizens' willingness to contribute to a public good, even when material incentives are held constant. The effect is robust and persists over time. The mechanism is shown to be intrinsic (normative legitimation) rather than reciprocity or conditional cooperation. However, the main outcome is contribution rate, not efficiency or group payoff. The paper provides strong evidence that legitimacy-enhancing actions by authorities can increase cooperation, but does not directly report on efficiency or total group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong evidence that features of the punishment regime that enhance perceived legitimacy (such as procedural fairness) can increase contribution rates in public goods games with centralized punishment. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. The findings suggest that, in similar game designs, legitimacy-enhancing punishment regimes may increase efficiency indirectly via increased contributions, but the magnitude of the effect on efficiency is not quantified here. The paper is most useful for understanding mechanisms and moderators of cooperation under punishment, rather than for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Bogdan, P. C., Dolcos, F., Moore, M., Kuznietsov, I., Culpepper, S. A., & Dolcos, S. (2023). Social Expectations are Primarily Rooted in Reciprocity: An Investigation of Fairness, Cooperation, and Trustworthiness. *Cognitive Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Across four studies, including a reanalysis of a large cross-cultural PGG with punishment, the paper finds that people punish others whose contributions deviate from their own prior behavior, rather than from the group norm. This applies to both prosocial and antisocial punishment. The effect is robust across cultures and game types. However, the paper does not report on how punishment affects group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare. The only payoff-related results are manipulation checks (e.g., in Study 4, generous partners yield higher earnings). Thus, the findings are highly informative about the psychological mechanisms of punishment and norm enforcement, but do not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in PGGs with punishment, the main driver of punishment is the dissimilarity between a punisher's own prior contributions and the target's contribution, rather than deviation from the group norm. This insight is useful for predicting when and whom players will punish, including antisocial punishment. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare as outcomes, it does not directly inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency. The findings may be used to inform mechanistic models of punishment assignment, but not to calibrate the effect of punishment on group efficiency.

- source: Bone, J., Silva, A. S., & Raihani, N. J. (2014). Defectors, not norm violators, are punished by third-parties. *Biology Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that third-party punishment in a one-shot public goods game is overwhelmingly directed at defectors, regardless of whether defection is the norm in the group. The manipulation of norm violation (defection being typical or atypical) does not affect the likelihood of defectors being punished. Cooperators are rarely punished, even when they are in the minority. The cost structure of punishment is fixed, so punishing multiple players is not more expensive than punishing one. Social rejection patterns mirror punishment: defectors are less preferred as future partners, regardless of norm status. The study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a one-shot PGG with third-party punishment and a fixed punishment cost, punishment is targeted at defectors rather than norm violators. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design details (one-shot, third-party punishment, fixed cost, no communication, binary contribution) are informative for mapping to other studies, but the lack of payoff outcomes limits its direct utility for efficiency prediction. The findings suggest that, under these design features, punishment is likely to be used against defectors, but the impact on group efficiency remains unmeasured.

- source: Hopfensitz, A., & Miquel-Florensa, J. (2017). Mill ownership and farmer's cooperative behavior: the case of Costa Rica coffee farmers. *Journal of Institutional Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that, in a public good game, Costa Rican coffee farmers selling to cooperatives do not contribute more than those selling to private mills when paired with their own group. However, cooperative farmers contribute significantly less when paired with private mill farmers, while private mill farmers' contributions are unaffected by partner background. When a punishment mechanism (penalizing lowest contributors, with partial redistribution to the group) is available, contributions by cooperative farmers increase substantially, especially when paired with private mill farmers (over 100% increase in contribution). The effect of punishment on private mill farmers is negligible. The main outcome is contribution, not efficiency or group payoff, but the structure of the game allows inference that group welfare would increase with higher contributions under punishment, especially for cooperative farmers in mixed groups.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a punishment mechanism in a public good game can substantially increase contributions, especially among cooperative farmers interacting with out-group partners. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, so the effect on efficiency must be inferred from the increase in contributions. The punishment mechanism is costly (1 unit per participant), and only a fraction of the fine is redistributed, so efficiency gains may be less than the increase in contributions. The design is a one-shot or single-decision public good game with 4 players, MPCR 0.375, and a punishment mechanism that is not peer-to-peer but an impartial rule penalizing the lowest contributor. The findings are most informative for predicting contribution changes (and thus likely efficiency changes) when punishment is enabled in similar settings, especially for groups with strong in-group/out-group distinctions. The lack of direct efficiency analysis is a limitation for precise prediction.

- source: Bruttel, L., & Friehe, T. (2014). Can short-term incentives induce long-lasting cooperation? Results from a public-goods experiment. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment used a repeated linear public goods game with punishment, fixed groups of four, and 20 rounds. Treatments manipulated the marginal per-capita return (mpcr) and punishment cost for the first 10 rounds, then reverted to a common, less cooperative-friendly setting for rounds 11-20. High mpcr or low punishment cost increased contributions in rounds 1-10, but after reverting to baseline parameters, contributions in these groups declined rapidly, often below the control. The baseline (constant, moderate mpcr and punishment cost) showed stable contributions. The study finds no evidence that temporary strong incentives create persistent cooperation; instead, their removal can backfire. The main outcome is contribution, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a linear public goods game with punishment, temporary improvements in incentive structure (either via higher mpcr or lower punishment cost) do not produce lasting increases in cooperation once incentives revert to baseline. For the prediction task, this suggests that the efficiency gain from enabling punishment (or from temporarily improving punishment effectiveness or mpcr) is not persistent if the incentive is not maintained. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, so its value for predicting efficiency is indirect and must be inferred from contribution patterns. The results caution against expecting lasting efficiency improvements from short-term incentive changes.

- source: Weber, T. O., Schulz, J. F., Beranek, B., Lambarraa-Lehnhardt, F., & Gächter, S. (2023). The behavioral mechanisms of voluntary cooperation across culturally diverse societies: Evidence from the US, the UK, Morocco, and Turkey. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that differences in cooperation across culturally diverse societies are mainly explained by differences in beliefs about others' cooperativeness, not by differences in cooperative preferences or punishment behavior. In the presence of punishment, only participants in the US and UK significantly increase their contributions compared to the no-punishment condition. Actual punishment of free riding is similar across all four countries, and antisocial punishment is low everywhere. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff as a main outcome, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency is not analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence on how punishment affects contribution rates in one-shot public goods games across different societies, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. For the downstream prediction task, the findings suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency is likely to be positive in settings similar to the US and UK (where contributions increase with punishment), but negligible in settings similar to Morocco and Turkey (where contributions do not increase). However, because efficiency is not directly measured, this inference is indirect and based on contribution behavior. The game design dimensions are well specified, allowing for mapping to prediction inputs, but the lack of direct efficiency data is a limitation.

- source: Kirchkamp, O., & Mill, W. (2020). Conditional cooperation and the effect of punishment. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares three treatments: a baseline public goods game (no punishment), a treatment where the conditional decision maker (CDM) can be punished by an unconditional decision maker (UCPun), and a treatment where the CDM can punish an unconditional decision maker (CPun). The threat of punishment (UCPun) increases the slope of conditional cooperation but reduces the probability of being a conditional cooperator (i.e., more free riders). The power to punish (CPun) does not reduce conditional cooperation (no substitution effect on cooperation), and actually increases the slope of conditional cooperation due to a responsibility effect. There is evidence for substitution between punishment and cooperation in punishment behavior, but not in contribution behavior. The overall effect of punishment is to increase conditional cooperation, but with some increase in free riding. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the direct effect on efficiency is not measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in a one-shot public goods game increases the slope of conditional cooperation, but also increases the number of free riders. The net effect on cooperation is positive, but the effect on efficiency or group payoff is not directly measured. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that punishment is likely to increase cooperation rates, but may have ambiguous effects on efficiency if the increase in free riding offsets the gains from stronger conditional cooperation. The design features—such as costless punishment, no communication, and a one-shot game—should be considered when generalizing to other settings. The absence of direct efficiency or payoff analysis limits the ability to make quantitative predictions about efficiency outcomes.

- source: Hoeft, L., & Mill, W. (2017). An experimental investigation of designated punishment behavior in public goods. *Economics Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a repeated public goods game with a single designated punisher, the punisher contributes significantly less than non-punishers but enforces higher contributions from others through punishment. This double standard grows over time. The results are robust to a full-information treatment where all contributions are public. The paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so it is unclear whether the presence of a designated punisher increases, decreases, or leaves unchanged the overall efficiency of the group.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the behavioral effects of designated punishment in public goods games, specifically that punishers can enforce higher contributions from others while themselves contributing less. However, because the paper does not report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, it does not directly inform the prediction of efficiency outcomes when punishment is enabled. The design details (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, information structure) are well specified and may be useful for matching to other studies, but the lack of efficiency or payoff data limits the direct predictive value for the downstream efficiency prediction task.

- source: Fraser, S., & Nettle, D. (2020). Hunger Affects Social Decisions in a Multi-Round Public Goods Game but Not a Single-Shot Ultimatum Game. *Adaptive Human Behavior and Physiology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a 20-round PGG (10 rounds without punishment, 10 with punishment), groups of four played with either all members having eaten breakfast or all having skipped breakfast. In the no-punishment phase, hungry participants contributed more initially and were more likely to match their contributions to those of their groupmates in subsequent rounds. In the punishment phase, hungry participants punished less, especially when group contributions were high, making punishment less effective at boosting contributions. However, the study does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly determined from the reported results.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative behavioral evidence about how punishment and hunger interact to affect contribution and punishment behavior in a standard PGG. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that punishment is less effective at increasing contributions when participants are hungry, which may imply a smaller efficiency gain from punishment in such contexts, but this is not quantified in payoff terms. The game design dimensions are well specified and match standard PGG with punishment, so the behavioral patterns may inform models that link contribution and punishment behavior to efficiency, but direct efficiency data are lacking.

- source: Campos-Vazquez, R. M., & Mejia, L. A. (2016). Does corruption affect cooperation? A laboratory experiment. *Latin American Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that: (1) exposure to a high-corruption environment (low monitoring) reduces subsequent cooperation in a public goods game; (2) allowing a leader to centrally punish increases cooperation (contributions) compared to no punishment, with a stronger effect in low-corruption groups; (3) allowing group members to counter-punish the leader reduces cooperation compared to leader-only punishment. All findings are based on contribution rates, not on efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that punishment can foster cooperation, but its effectiveness is moderated by prior corruption exposure and is undermined by the threat of counter-punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that centralized punishment by a leader increases contribution rates in a standard linear public goods game, and that this effect is stronger in groups with less prior exposure to corruption. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, punishment cost/tech, etc.) are well specified, and the findings are highly relevant for predicting the effect of enabling centralized punishment on cooperation (contribution rates), but not directly on efficiency. The results also highlight that the presence of counter-punishment can reduce the positive effect of punishment on cooperation.

- source: Romaniuc, R., Farrow, K., Ibanez, L., & Marciano, A. (2016). The perils of government enforcement. *Public Choice*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that peer disapproval (private enforcement) increases contributions to the public good compared to baseline. Adding a mild, centralized monetary punishment (government enforcement) to peer disapproval stabilizes contributions but does not significantly increase average contributions compared to peer disapproval alone. Critically, if government enforcement is removed after being in place, the effectiveness of peer disapproval is undermined: low contributors become unresponsive to disapproval, and average contributions drop significantly below the level observed when only private enforcement was ever present. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but the results on contributions imply that punishment can have both stabilizing and crowding-out effects depending on the sequence of enforcement mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the sequence and combination of enforcement mechanisms (private and government) can have important effects on cooperation (as measured by contributions) in public goods games. While the study does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the findings suggest that enabling punishment can stabilize or increase contributions, but that removing punishment after it has been in place can lead to a sharp decline in cooperation, even below the level observed with private enforcement alone. For prediction tasks, this implies that the history of enforcement mechanisms and their removal are important moderators of punishment's effect on efficiency. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the ability to make quantitative predictions about efficiency outcomes.

- source: Chang, H. I., Dawes, C. T., & Johnson, T. (2018). POLITICAL INEQUALITY, CENTRALIZED SANCTIONING INSTITUTIONS, AND THE MAINTENANCE OF PUBLIC GOODS. *Bulletin of Economic Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that introducing a centralized sanctioning authority (who can punish low contributors at a cost) increases contributions to the public good compared to no authority. However, electing the authority (democratically) does not further increase contributions compared to random selection. When voting power is distributed unequally, contributions only decrease if the inequality is based on prior economic performance (earnings in the PGG), not if it is randomly assigned. The negative effect of wealth-based political inequality appears to operate through reduced perceived legitimacy of the institution. Average earnings are reported by condition, but efficiency is not directly analyzed as a main outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of a centralized punishment institution increases contributions (and likely efficiency) relative to no punishment, but does not support the claim that democratic selection of the punisher further increases efficiency. The effect of punishment on efficiency is likely positive in this design, but the paper does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoff as the main outcome. The findings suggest that the way punishment institutions are selected (random vs. democratic) may not matter for efficiency, unless political inequality is tied to economic wealth, which can reduce contributions. For prediction, the presence of centralized punishment should be coded as increasing efficiency, but the selection mechanism (random vs. democratic) should not be expected to matter unless wealth-based voting is used.

- source: Morgan, S. N., Mason, N. M., & Shupp, R. S. (2019). The effects of voice with(out) punishment: Public goods provision and rule compliance. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that: (1) Comments alone do not significantly increase contributions or compliance with the MCR compared to control. (2) Enforcement (punishment) alone increases contributions and compliance, but the effect is significantly larger when comments are combined with enforcement. (3) The comment with enforcement treatment leads to the highest and most sustained contributions and compliance. (4) Rule-makers exposed to comments set higher MCRs, which in turn increases contributions. (5) The effect of comments on contributions is partly mediated by the higher MCRs set by rule-makers. (6) The paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but the increased contributions and compliance in the comment with enforcement treatment imply higher efficiency in that condition.
  prediction_guidance: For the prediction task, this paper provides strong evidence that in a linear public goods game with a probabilistic punishment mechanism, adding a comment-based participation mechanism (private, one-way comments to a rule-maker who sets a minimum contribution rule) increases contributions and compliance above punishment alone. While efficiency is not directly measured, the structure of the game (MPCR = 0.4, group size 5, continuous contributions, no chat, no rewards) means that higher contributions and compliance will translate into higher efficiency. The effect of punishment alone is positive but smaller than the effect of punishment plus comments. The paper does not provide direct evidence on efficiency, so predictions should be based on the observed contribution and compliance patterns. The findings are most relevant for games with third-party rule-making, probabilistic exogenous punishment, and no peer-to-peer punishment or reward.

- source: Farrow, K., & Romaniuc, R. (2019). The stickiness of norms. *International Review of Law and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that both peer disapproval and face-saving mechanisms increase contributions relative to baseline. When a temporary monetary sanction is introduced and then removed, peer disapproval groups experience a significant drop in cooperation (contributions fall below baseline), indicating a negative post-intervention spillover. In contrast, face-saving groups maintain or even increase cooperation after the sanction is removed, showing no negative spillover. The results suggest that the type of informal norm enforcement moderates the legacy of formal sanctions, with face-saving mechanisms supporting more robust cooperation after sanctions end.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation (and by implication, efficiency) depends on the type of informal norm enforcement present. While the study does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the contribution data imply that punishment can have negative, neutral, or positive legacy effects depending on whether peer disapproval or face-saving is salient. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence and type of informal norm enforcement (especially observability/social image vs. anonymous peer disapproval) are important moderators of punishment's effect. However, because efficiency is not directly measured, the findings should be interpreted as applying primarily to contribution rates.

- source: Nolan, J. M. (2017). Environmental Policies Can Buttress Conservation Norms. *Society & Natural Resources*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a lab public goods game, introducing formal sanctions (either market-based or regulatory) increases contributions in the round where sanctions are present, and increases informal sanctioning (internal, indirect, and some direct measures) toward defectors. However, the effect on contribution does not persist after sanctions are removed. The study does not report group payoff, efficiency, or total earnings, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly determined. The field study supports the finding that formal sanctioning systems are associated with stronger informal sanctioning norms, but again does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that introducing punishment (formal sanctions) in a standard linear public goods game increases contributions while the sanction is present, and increases informal sanctioning. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design dimensions (player count, MPCR, punishment cost, etc.) are partially specified and can be inferred for the lab experiment, but the main outcomes are non-payoff behavioral measures. The paper is useful for understanding the psychological and social norm effects of punishment, but not for predicting efficiency changes.

- source: Windmann, S., Binder, L., & Schultze, M. (2021). Constructing the Facets of Altruistic Behaviors (FAB) Scale. *Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The FAB scale reliably measures three distinct facets of altruistic behavior, including peer punishment. In a pilot study, the peer punishment subscale correlates with the amount participants spend on punishing free riders in a public goods game with punishment. The public goods game used is a standard design (groups of four, endowment, MPCR=0.75, punishment with 1:3 fee-to-fine ratio), but the only behavioral outcome reported is the amount spent on punishment, not group efficiency or total earnings. The study provides evidence that self-reported peer punishment trait predicts willingness to punish in a PGG, but does not analyze or report the effect of punishment on group efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that self-reported peer punishment trait is associated with actual punishment behavior in a public goods game with punishment. However, it does not provide any evidence about the effect of enabling punishment on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare. The game design dimensions (player count, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, etc.) are described for the pilot PGG, but no efficiency or payoff outcomes are reported. Therefore, this paper does not directly inform prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency. It may be useful for understanding individual differences in punishment behavior, but not for predicting efficiency effects of punishment.
