- source: Li, Y., & Mifune, N. (2023). Punishment in the public goods game is evaluated negatively irrespective of non-cooperators' motivation. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games with punishment, punishers are evaluated more negatively than non-punishers, and this negative evaluation is robust to manipulations of the non-cooperator's motivation (fear vs. greed). In third-party punishment games, punishers are evaluated more positively than non-punishers, again regardless of the non-cooperator's motivation. The study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or any monetary outcomes, so it does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or related payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games. Its findings are limited to social evaluations of punishers and non-punishers, not behavioral or payoff outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment, but may be relevant for understanding reputational or social preference mechanisms that could indirectly affect cooperation or punishment behavior in other studies.

- source: Karakostas, A., Kocher, M. G., Matzat, D., Rau, H. A., & Riewe, G. (2023). The team allocator game: Allocation power in public goods games. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper experimentally compares standard public goods games (VCM) to versions with a team allocator (TAG) who can redistribute the public good among team members. Three production technologies are tested: linear, weakest-link, and best-shot. In the linear case, the team allocator increases both contributions and group efficiency (total profit), as the allocator uses allocation power to reward contributors and sanction free-riders without explicit punishment costs. In the best-shot case, the allocator increases contributions but causes over-contribution and reduces efficiency due to coordination problems. In the weakest-link case, the allocator has no significant effect on contributions or efficiency. Thus, the effect of hierarchical allocation power as an implicit sanction/reward mechanism depends crucially on the production technology.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of introducing a sanctioning/reward mechanism (via a team allocator) on efficiency depends on the public goods production function. In linear PGGs, such mechanisms can increase efficiency, but in best-shot games, they may reduce efficiency due to over-contribution. In weakest-link games, the effect is negligible. The design dimensions most relevant for prediction are the production technology (linear, best-shot, weakest-link), the presence of an allocator with discretionary redistribution power (as a proxy for punishment/reward), and the absence of explicit punishment costs. Control efficiency (VCM) is a strong predictor, but the treatment effect varies by technology. The findings suggest that the efficiency impact of punishment-like mechanisms is positive in linear games, negative in best-shot, and neutral in weakest-link games, even when the mechanism is costless to the allocator.

- source: Riehm, T., Fugger, N., Gillen, P., Gretschko, V., & Werner, P. (2022). Social norms, sanctions, and conditional entry in markets with externalities: Evidence from an artefactual field experiment. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that all-can-win intergroup competition (where multiple groups can be ranked first and avoid penalties) leads to large increases in cooperation and group output across all treatments. The effect is strongest when penalties for not being ranked first are sharp (T1, T2), supporting near-perfect cooperation and benefiting a majority of individuals, though not all. In these cases, average earnings are about the same with and without competition, as severe penalties for a few groups offset gains for the majority. In the weaker-penalty treatment (T3), cooperation increases less, and the effect on earnings is neutral. The results suggest that the design of the penalty function (i.e., the sharpness of incentives) is crucial for both cooperation and efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a rank-based, all-can-win intergroup competition mechanism in a standard linear public goods game can substantially increase cooperation and group output, especially when penalties for low-ranked groups are sharp. However, the effect on efficiency (average earnings) is not always positive: while most individuals benefit, severe penalties for a minority can offset group-level gains. For prediction, the sharpness of the penalty function (punishment tech) and the degree of coordination among groups are key moderators. If the control game (no competition) has low efficiency, introducing sharp all-can-win competition is likely to increase group output and may increase average earnings, but the risk of severe losses for some groups must be considered. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments with intergroup competition as a sanctioning mechanism.

- source: Eichenseer, M. (2023). Leading-by-example in public goods experiments: What do we know?. *Leadership Quarterly*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The paper synthesizes 62 experimental studies (mainly linear public goods games) and conducts meta-analyses and weighted least squares regressions. It finds that sequential play (leadership) increases group efficiency compared to simultaneous play. Centralized punishment and reward (where only the leader can punish or reward) further increase efficiency, with punishment having a stronger effect than reward. Peer punishment/reward (where all can punish/reward) have even larger effects. Rotating leadership reduces efficiency. The marginal per capita return (MPCR) and group size also positively affect efficiency, but to a lesser extent. Leaders contribute more than followers, and followers contribute about 79% as much as leaders. Inequality within groups does not generally increase with leadership. Leaders' payoffs are similar to those of average players in simultaneous games. The paper provides quantitative estimates of effect sizes for each moderator.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, quantitative evidence for predicting the effect of punishment (and reward) on efficiency in public goods games, conditional on game design dimensions. The meta-regressions include variables for group size, number of rounds, MPCR, presence and type of punishment/reward, and leadership structure. The results show that adding centralized punishment (by a leader) increases efficiency by about 0.26 (on a 0-1 scale of contributions relative to optimum), and peer punishment increases it by about 0.47. The effect of reward is positive but smaller. Rotating leadership reduces efficiency. These findings can be used to adjust predicted efficiency when punishment is enabled, given the control efficiency and game design. The paper is highly relevant for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Ozono, H., Kamijo, Y., & Shimizu, K. (2016). Institutionalize Reciprocity to Overcome the Public Goods Provision Problem. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: In a repeated four-person public goods game, embedding a reciprocal strategy as an institution (institutionalized reciprocity, IR) leads to significantly higher group efficiency (total profit) than a standard control game. The effect is strongest when the institution enforces a strict upper bound on contributions (S-IR), which enables reciprocators to coordinate against free riders and prevents excessive cooperation. In S-IR, group profits are significantly higher than in both the control and a less restrictive IR variant (NL-IR). The IR mechanism operates as a collective punishment system: if any player fails to meet the target, the group's future benefit is reduced for all. The study does not implement standard peer punishment, but the IR mechanism provides a collective sanction that is effective at increasing efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that institutionalized, rule-based collective punishment (via reduction of future group benefit after a deviation) can substantially increase efficiency in repeated public goods games, especially when the institution strictly limits contributions to a target. While the study does not test standard peer punishment, it shows that collective, institutionally enforced sanctions can outperform standard repeated games without punishment. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that introducing a credible, enforced institutional sanction (even if not peer punishment) can raise efficiency well above control, particularly when the rules are clear and binding. The results are most directly applicable to games with four players, 20 rounds, no chat, continuous contributions (in increments), and full feedback on others' contributions and payoffs.

- source: Brick, K., Visser, M., & Van der Hoven, Z. (2016). Cooperation and Climate Change: Can Communication Facilitate the Provision of Public Goods in Heterogeneous Settings?. *Environmental & Resource Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Communication (chat) between group members in a heterogeneous public good game increases average contributions and the likelihood of meeting a collective target, but does not fully solve the free-rider problem. Even with communication, many groups fail to reach the target, and free-riding remains common, especially among high marginal cost players. The experiment does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, so no direct evidence is provided on the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff outcomes. The paper suggests, based on prior literature, that punishment opportunities may be necessary to ensure compliance and improve efficiency, especially in heterogeneous settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence on the effect of communication (chat) in a heterogeneous public good game, but does not provide any experimental evidence on punishment or sanctioning. For the downstream prediction task (predicting efficiency with punishment enabled), this paper is not directly informative, as no punishment treatment is implemented. However, it does provide a useful control case: in a 4-player, one-shot, linear PGG with MPCR=0.42, no punishment, and no feedback on others' contributions, average group contributions are well below the social optimum, and communication increases but does not maximize efficiency. The paper's discussion and literature review suggest that punishment is likely to further increase cooperation and efficiency, but this is not tested here. Thus, this paper is best used as a control or baseline for settings without punishment.

- source: Greiff, M., & Paetzel, F. (2015). INCOMPLETE INFORMATION STRENGTHENS THE EFFECTIVENESS OF SOCIAL APPROVAL. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that enabling subjects to express social approval (assigning 0-10 stars to each group member after observing contributions) significantly increases contributions to the public good compared to a control with no approval. This effect is stronger when subjects have only partial information (see only others' contributions) than when they have full information (see both contributions and endowments). The study isolates the consumption value of social approval by using stranger matching and anonymity, ruling out reputation effects. No direct measures of efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings are reported; all main results are in terms of contribution rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that non-monetary, symbolic social approval can increase contributions in public good games, but the effect is sensitive to the information structure. However, because the study does not include monetary punishment or analyze efficiency or group payoff, it is only indirectly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is otherwise closely matched to standard PGGs, so the behavioral findings may be relevant for understanding how non-monetary incentives (like approval) interact with information, but cannot be directly used to predict efficiency outcomes from punishment interventions.

- source: Akpalu, W., Abidoye, B., Muchapondwa, E., & Simbanegavi, W. (2017). Public disclosure for carbon abatement: African decision-makers in a PROPER public good experiment. *Climate and Development*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that public disclosure of individual pollution levels (via visible color-coded cards) reduces pollution (i.e., increases cooperation) in a linear public good game. The effect persists even after disclosure is withdrawn. The study does not use monetary punishment or reward, but rather a reputational/shame mechanism. The main outcome is the number of red cards retained (pollution), not group efficiency or total payoff. The results suggest that public disclosure can be an effective non-monetary sanction to promote cooperation in public goods settings, but do not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that public disclosure (a reputational/shame-based sanction) can reduce free-riding in a linear public good game, even among experienced policy-makers. However, it does not test monetary punishment or reward, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. For prediction tasks focused on the effect of monetary punishment on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that non-monetary sanctions can increase cooperation, but does not quantify efficiency gains or compare to monetary punishment. The game design dimensions (e.g., player count, rounds, no chat, continuous contribution, known MPCR) are well described, but the absence of monetary punishment means the results are not directly transferable to settings with explicit punishment mechanisms.

- source: Li, J. B., Liu, X. L., Yin, X. L., Li, S. Q., Wang, G. R., Niu, X. F., & Zhu, C. K. (2018). Transcranial Direct Current Stimulation Altered Voluntary Cooperative Norms Compliance Under Equal Decision-Making Power. *Frontiers in Human Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that modulating rDLPFC activity via tDCS causally affects voluntary cooperative norm compliance (measured as contribution) in a linear public goods game with four players, asymmetric endowments, and no punishment or sanction options. Anodal stimulation increases contributions, cathodal decreases them, and sham is intermediate. The effect is observed for both high and low endowment players. The study also finds that participants' own norm perceptions (norm.own) are more predictive of compliance than their perceptions of others' norms (norm.other), and that anodal stimulation changes the relative influence of these norm perceptions. No efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes are reported, and the game does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanction mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its findings are limited to the effect of neural stimulation on voluntary cooperation (contribution) in a standard linear PGG. The game design dimensions (player count, rounds, MPCR, etc.) are reported and may be useful for context, but the absence of punishment means this paper cannot inform the downstream prediction task regarding the effect of punishment on efficiency. It may be useful as a control or baseline for voluntary cooperation in the absence of sanctions.

- source: Martinsson, P., & Persson, E. (2019). Public Goods and Minimum Provision Levels: Does the Institutional Formation Affect Cooperation?. *Scandinavian Journal of Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Imposing a minimum contribution level (5 tokens out of 20) in a linear public goods game increases average contributions compared to a standard voluntary contribution mechanism. This effect is robust to whether the minimum is imposed exogenously or chosen endogenously by group vote. The increase in contributions is mainly due to free riders being forced to contribute at least the minimum; there is little evidence of crowding out among those who previously contributed more than the minimum. The mode of institutional formation (democracy vs. authority) does not significantly affect the outcome. The study does not analyze efficiency or group payoff directly, but the results suggest that minimum contribution rules can increase public good provision in settings where punishment is not feasible.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is informative for predicting the effect of minimum contribution rules in public goods games, especially in the absence of punishment or reward mechanisms. It does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does show that institutional interventions (minimum levels) can increase contributions, mainly by raising the floor for low contributors. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that minimum contribution rules can be effective in increasing contributions, but does not inform about the effect of punishment. The game design dimensions (e.g., player count, MPCR, no chat, continuous contributions, no punishment/reward) are clearly specified, which helps in mapping to other studies. However, since efficiency or group payoff is not directly measured, the findings should be used with caution for efficiency prediction.

- source: Guillen, P., Fatas, E., & Brañas-Garza, P. (2010). Inducing efficient conditional cooperation patterns in public goods games, an experimental investigation. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The introduction of a grim trigger strategy threat (via possible automata) leads to a substantial increase in average contributions (by 40% before a surprise restart and 50% after) compared to a standard repeated public goods game. Contributions in the threat treatment are highly polarized (either full or zero), and the pattern of conditional cooperation changes: in the threat treatment, the minimum contribution in the previous round predicts current contributions, consistent with grim trigger logic. However, contributions still decline over time, and the effect is driven by belief manipulation rather than actual punishment or sanctions. The study does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but the higher contributions imply higher group payoffs, net of the absence of punishment costs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that belief in the possibility of strict conditional cooperation (grim trigger) can substantially increase contributions in repeated public goods games, even without actual punishment. However, because there is no real punishment mechanism (no cost, no assignment of punishment, no reduction of others' payoffs), the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task of estimating efficiency changes due to enabling punishment. The results suggest that the mere threat or belief in sanctions can have large effects on cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence on the efficiency impact of actual punishment mechanisms, especially considering the cost of punishment. The design dimensions are well specified, but the absence of real punishment means the findings should be used as indirect evidence for the prediction task.

- source: Wei, C. (2020). Can job rotation eliminate the Ratchet effect: Experimental evidence. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that ethnic dominance (caste hierarchy within groups) has a large negative effect on contributions in a public goods game, while ethnic diversity alone does not. Individuals display strong in-group bias, increasing their contributions in response to co-ethnic peers but not to others. The experiment did not include punishment or reward options, and all quantitative results are about contribution levels, not payoffs or efficiency. The authors discuss antisocial punishment and discrimination as real-world mechanisms but these are not present in the experimental design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or reward mechanisms were present in the experiment. It does, however, provide informative context about how group composition (especially ethnic dominance) can affect baseline cooperation rates, which may be relevant for interpreting control group efficiency in similar environments. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative about the effect of enabling punishment, but it does highlight that group structure (e.g., ethnic dominance) can strongly affect baseline cooperation, which could interact with punishment effects in other studies.

- source: Adams, Z., Ludwiczak, A., Sharma, D., & Osman, M. (2022). Verbal interaction in a social dilemma. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that the quality of communication—specifically, consensus-building language and alignment in chat—significantly predicts increases in cooperative behavior in a repeated public goods game. Groups with higher consensus scores in chat showed larger increases in contributions to the group pot in the next round. The analysis also shows that certain registers (gameshow, familial, socialist) are associated with more successful cooperation, while business and political registers are associated with less success. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that allowing chat/communication between rounds in a public goods game can increase cooperation, but the effect depends on the quality and style of the communication. However, it does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment or sanctions, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff. For the downstream prediction task (predicting efficiency with punishment enabled), this paper is only indirectly informative: it supports the importance of communication (chat) as a moderator of cooperation, but does not address punishment or efficiency directly. It is most useful for understanding the role of chat/communication as a game design dimension.

- source: Sun, X. P., Li, Y. B., Kang, H. W., Shen, Y., Peng, J., Wang, H. Y., & Chen, Q. Y. (2020). Co-Evolution of Complex Network Public Goods Game under the Edges Rules. *Entropy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that allowing players to break unsatisfactory connections and form new ones (edge rules) in a public goods game on a network leads to higher and more stable cooperation, especially at low gain factors (r=2). Both simulation and a 30-person lab experiment show that the fraction of cooperators increases and stabilizes under these rules, compared to a static network. The mechanism is interpreted as self-organized punishment (breaking ties with defectors) and reward (forming ties with cooperators), but there is no explicit costly punishment. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators; efficiency or group payoff is not reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that endogenous network adaptation (breaking and forming ties based on satisfaction) can promote cooperation in public goods games, even at low MPCR. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of explicit punishment on efficiency. The 'punishment' here is not a costly sanction but the breaking of ties, which is structurally different from standard punishment mechanisms. The findings may be useful for understanding the effects of network fluidity or endogenous partner choice, but not for predicting efficiency changes from adding explicit punishment to a fixed-group PGG.

- source: Tan, L. J., & Wei, L. J. (2014). VOLUNTARY CONTRIBUTION MECHANISM PLAYED OVER AN INFINITE HORIZON. *Pacific Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that, in the absence of explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, average contributions in infinitely repeated VCM games do not monotonically decline as in finitely repeated games. Instead, they show non-monotonic patterns, often sustained by the presence of 'contribution makers' who lead reciprocators to increase their contributions. There is a strong restart effect in finitely repeated games but not in infinitely repeated games. The paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but notes that groups with contribution makers achieve higher average contributions and, by implication, higher payoffs than groups without them.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. It does, however, provide evidence on baseline contribution dynamics in standard VCMs with continuous contribution choices, fixed groups of 5, MPCR of 0.5, and no communication or punishment. The findings suggest that, in the absence of punishment, efficiency (proxied by contribution rates) is sustained at moderate levels in infinitely repeated games, especially when 'contribution makers' are present. For prediction tasks, this paper is best used as a control/baseline for VCMs without punishment, not for estimating the effect of punishment.

- source: Stoop, J., van Soest, D., & Vyrastekova, J. (2018). Rewards and cooperation in social dilemma games. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that net-positive rewards (where the recipient gains more than the sender loses) dramatically increase cooperation and efficiency in standard public goods games (VCM), with most groups reaching high contribution levels when rewards are available. In contrast, in the Claim game—where players can undo others' contributions by taking from the public pool—rewards have only a marginal effect on cooperation, and average net contributions are not significantly different from zero. In the Claim game, rewards are often exchanged bilaterally and are not strongly linked to cooperative behavior in the social dilemma. The results suggest that the effectiveness of rewards in promoting efficiency depends critically on the structure of the social dilemma, particularly whether others can undo cooperative actions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effectiveness of reward mechanisms in increasing efficiency in public goods games depends on whether the game allows for negative actions (i.e., taking from the public pool) and whether the MPCR is certain. In standard VCMs (linear PGGs), rewards can substantially increase efficiency, but in environments where contributions can be undone (as in the Claim game or CPR games), rewards are much less effective. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of a 'take' option or uncertain MPCR is a critical moderator: control efficiency is not a sufficient predictor of treatment efficiency with rewards if the game allows for undoing others' contributions. The findings do not inform about punishment effects, as punishment is not studied.

- source: Qiu, H. Q., Zhang, Y. L., Hou, G. L., & Wang, Z. M. (2018). The Integrative Effects of Leading by Example and Follower Traits in Public Goods Game: A Multilevel Study. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a repeated PGG with no punishment or reward, the presence and quality of leading by example (leader's average contribution) significantly affect both group and individual payoffs. High leader contributions (good leadership) increase team and follower payoffs above the control (no leader) condition, while low leader contributions (bad leadership) decrease payoffs below control. The effect is robust across rounds, though contributions decline over time (end-of-game effect). Follower traits also moderate cooperation, with risk attitude positively and trust attitude negatively associated with follower contributions. No evidence is provided on punishment or sanction mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions in PGG-like environments. It is directly relevant for understanding the impact of leadership structure (sequential vs. simultaneous moves) and leader behavior on efficiency, but not for predicting the effect of enabling punishment. The control group (simultaneous move, no leader) and treatment groups (sequential move with leader) are compared on efficiency, but all conditions lack punishment. Therefore, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be informative for understanding baseline efficiency in repeated PGGs with and without leadership.

- source: Chung, D., Yun, K., Kim, J. H., Jang, B., & Jeong, J. (2011). Different Gain/Loss Sensitivity and Social Adaptation Ability in Gifted Adolescents during a Public Goods Game. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that gifted adolescents are more cooperative and earn higher total payoffs than average adolescents in a repeated binary public goods game with five players and no punishment or reward mechanisms. Three incentive conditions are tested: (1) standard PGG, (2) no-loss guarantee for cooperators, and (3) equalized bonus for all in successful rounds. Gifted adolescents show higher gain sensitivity and lower loss sensitivity, and are more strategic in maximizing payoffs. Average adolescents are more loss-averse, less strategic, and more influenced by social adaptation behaviors. The study provides detailed evidence on how group composition (gifted vs. average) and incentive structure affect efficiency and cooperation, but does not manipulate or analyze punishment or sanctioning.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence on efficiency and payoff outcomes in repeated binary PGGs without punishment or reward. It is directly informative for predicting efficiency in control (no-punishment) conditions with known game design parameters (player count, rounds, MPCR, etc.). However, it does not inform the effect of enabling punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present or discussed in the experimental design. Thus, it is useful for establishing baseline efficiency but not for predicting the effect of punishment.

- source: Noussair, C., & Soo, C. (2008). Voluntary contributions to a dynamic public good: Experimental evidence. *Economics Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that in a dynamic public goods game where the MPCR increases or decreases based on group contributions, many groups are able to sustain high contributions and achieve high efficiency, even in the absence of punishment or reward mechanisms. The average efficiency is .671 in the high initial MPCR treatment and .632 in the low initial MPCR treatment, both substantially above the efficiency that would result from full free-riding. There is substantial heterogeneity: some groups converge to high contributions and high MPCR, while others decline to low contributions. The initial MPCR does not significantly affect long-run outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a baseline for the efficiency of dynamic public goods games without punishment or reward. It shows that, in such settings, efficiency can be substantially higher than in standard static public goods games, due to the dynamic feedback of contributions on the MPCR. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is useful for estimating control (no-punishment) efficiency in dynamic PGGs, but it does not provide any evidence about the effect of punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present or discussed experimentally. Thus, it cannot directly inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment, but it does suggest that dynamic feedback mechanisms can themselves sustain high efficiency in some groups.

- source: Bartos, V. (2021). Seasonal scarcity and sharing norms. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that neither making ethnic identity salient nor priming participants with memories of past ethnic violence significantly affects cooperation or generosity in the public goods or dictator games. There is a modest negative effect of violence priming on coordination with in-group partners in the stag hunt game, but this does not generalize to the public goods game. No significant differences are found in trust or trustworthiness by treatment. The results suggest that, in this context, ethnic divisions and reminders of conflict do not meaningfully alter pro-social economic behavior in standard experimental games. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, so it provides no evidence on the effects of punishment on efficiency or cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in any of the games. It does, however, provide a well-powered null result for the effect of ethnic identity salience and conflict priming on cooperation in a two-player public goods game. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not relevant. For baseline efficiency in two-player PGGs without punishment, the results suggest that neither ethnic matching nor conflict salience meaningfully affect average contributions or pro-social behavior.

- source: Fosgaard, T. R., & Piovesan, M. (2015). Nudge for (the Public) Good: How Defaults Can Affect Cooperation. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that while most subjects do not simply accept the default contribution values, the presence of a default (either free rider or perfect conditional cooperator) significantly influences their stated cooperation strategies and actual contributions in a subsequent repeated public goods game. Specifically, exposure to a cooperative default increases later contributions, while a free rider default decreases them. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in any treatment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning is present. It does, however, show that non-binding defaults can shift contribution behavior, which may indirectly inform models of cooperation. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, as it does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes.

- source: Denant-Boemont, L., Masclet, D., & Noussair, C. (2011). ANNOUNCEMENT, OBSERVATION AND HONESTY IN THE VOLUNTARY CONTRIBUTIONS GAME. *Pacific Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that requiring subjects to make non-binding announcements of intended contributions, combined with making actual contributions publicly observable and attributable, significantly increases average contribution levels in a repeated public goods game. However, neither announcements alone nor observation alone have a significant effect. The increase in contributions is interpreted as arising from increased social pressure (guilt and shame) and greater honesty when actions are observable. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and does not report efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on behavioral outcomes (contribution, announcement, honesty).
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it does provide informative evidence about the effects of communication (announcements) and observability (public attribution of actions) on contribution behavior. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may be useful for understanding the baseline effects of non-punitive social mechanisms (communication, transparency) on cooperation. It also clarifies that, in the absence of punishment, such mechanisms can increase contributions, but do not always do so unless combined.

- source: Park, S. A., Jeong, S., & Jeong, J. (2013). TV programs that denounce unfair advantage impact women's sensitivity to defection in the public goods game. *Social Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a 10-round, 4-player, binary (all-or-nothing) threshold PGG, women are more cooperative than men in early rounds, but this difference disappears after both genders watch investigative TV programs about free-riding. After media exposure, women become more likely to free-ride in response to others' defection, adopting a tit-for-tat strategy. The main outcomes are cooperation rates and EEG measures; no efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported. There is no punishment or reward mechanism in the game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. It does, however, provide detailed information about a binary, threshold PGG with 4 players and 10 rounds, and shows how external context (media exposure) can modulate cooperation rates, especially among women. For the prediction task, this paper is not directly informative about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment, but it does confirm that in the absence of punishment, cooperation rates (and thus likely efficiency) can be sensitive to social context and gender. The absence of punishment and the focus on behavioral (not payoff) outcomes limit its direct relevance.

- source: Wang, G. R., Li, J. B., Wang, W. H., Niu, X. F., Wang, Y., & Selten, R. (2024). Confusion cannot explain cooperative behavior in public goods games. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that, after ensuring participants understand the public goods game, most still do not behave as pure free riders, especially when playing with humans. Social preferences and social norms, rather than confusion, explain much of the observed cooperation. The difference in contributions between human and computer treatments is attributed to social preferences. However, the paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. It is useful for understanding baseline cooperation and the role of confusion and social preferences in standard PGGs, but does not inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is only relevant as a control/baseline for PGGs without punishment, and does not provide evidence about any game design dimension's moderation of punishment effects.

- source: van den Berg, P., Dewitte, P., Aertgeerts, I., & Wenseleers, T. (2020). How the incentive to contribute affects contributions in the one-shot public goods game. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that average contributions in a one-shot public goods game increase with the marginal per capita return (MPCR), with most of the increase occurring at lower MPCR values (below 0.58–0.64). At higher MPCR, contributions plateau well below full cooperation. The experiment uses groups of three, no punishment or reward, and no communication. The findings provide a high-resolution mapping of how MPCR shapes contribution behavior, but do not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting baseline (control) efficiency in public goods games as a function of MPCR, group size, and the absence of punishment or reward. It does not provide any evidence about the effect of punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is useful for estimating control efficiency (without punishment) given MPCR and group size, but does not inform the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Fellner-Röhling, G., Kröger, S., & Seki, E. (2020). Public good production in heterogeneous groups: An experimental analysis on the relation between external return and information. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that in public goods games with heterogeneous external returns, the level and structure of information about group heterogeneity and contributor type significantly affect contribution behavior. When no information about heterogeneity is provided, both high- and low-ability types contribute similarly. When heterogeneity is known but contributions cannot be linked to type, low-ability types contribute more than high-ability types (a 'poisoning-of-the-well' effect). When contributions can be linked to type, high-ability types contribute more. Overall, more information about heterogeneity and contributor identity increases average contributions, but the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff with or without punishment, as no punishment mechanism is present and efficiency is not measured. However, it provides informative evidence about how information structure and heterogeneity in marginal returns affect contribution rates in public goods games. For prediction tasks involving efficiency, the results suggest that information about heterogeneity and contributor identity can increase contributions, but the translation to efficiency or payoff outcomes would require additional assumptions or data. The absence of punishment means the findings are not directly transferable to settings where punishment is enabled or disabled.

- source: Krause, J. S., Brandt, G., Schmidt, U., & Schunk, D. (2023). Don't sweat it: Ambient temperature does not affect social behavior and perception. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that ambient temperature in the range of 18°C to 28°C does not affect social behavior or perception in incentivized economic games, including the Public Goods Game. All behavioral outcomes (contribution, sharing, trust, etc.) and social perception measures (communion, agency, social distance, empathy) are statistically equivalent across temperature conditions. No evidence is found for either the 'warmth-prosociality' or 'heat-aggression' hypotheses in this context. The study does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides no direct evidence for the prediction of efficiency effects of punishment in public-goods-game-like environments, as punishment is not present in any game and efficiency or group payoff is not analyzed. However, it does provide high-quality evidence that ambient temperature in the tested range does not affect cooperation or related social behaviors in a standard two-player Public Goods Game. For prediction tasks, this suggests that temperature (within this range) is unlikely to be a relevant moderator of punishment effects or efficiency in similar lab settings. The absence of punishment and efficiency outcomes means this paper is not directly informative for the core prediction task.

- source: Kim, D. G. (2018). Population uncertainty in voluntary contributions of public goods. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that population uncertainty can increase voluntary contributions in public goods games when the production function is nonlinear and concave, but has no significant effect when the production function is linear. The main outcome measured is the average (percentage) contribution to the group account. No punishment or sanction mechanisms are present in the design. The results suggest that the effect of population uncertainty depends on the shape of the production function and the nature of warm-glow utility, but do not provide evidence about punishment or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform prediction of efficiency under punishment, as no punishment or sanction mechanism is present in any treatment. It does provide evidence about how population uncertainty and production function shape affect contribution rates, but not efficiency or payoff outcomes under punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly relevant, except as a control for understanding baseline contribution behavior in the absence of punishment.

- source: Yam, P. P. C., Ng, G. T. T., Au, W. T., Tao, L., Lu, S., Leung, H., & Fung, J. M. Y. (2018). The effect of subgroup homogeneity of efficacy on contribution in public good dilemmas. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that arranging participants into homogeneous or heterogeneous subgroups of efficacy does not significantly affect the number or proportion of contributions to the public good. This is because two mediating mechanisms—perceived efficacy and expectation of subgroup contribution—operate in opposite directions and cancel each other out. The experiment does not include any punishment, sanction, or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The findings are limited to contribution behavior and psychological mediators.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present or discussed. The design dimensions studied (subgroup homogeneity, efficacy) are not among the core prediction dimensions for punishment effects. The outcome measures are contribution rates and psychological variables, not efficiency or group payoff. Therefore, this paper should not be used to inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Alventosa, A., Antonioni, A., & Hernández, P. (2021). Pool punishment in public goods games: How do sanctioners? incentives affect us?. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The ratchet-up mechanism (contributions cannot decrease) reduces cooperation rates compared to a standard voluntary contribution mechanism. Adding a binding collective minimum contribution requirement (lowest common denominator) increases cooperation rates by about 25 percentage points compared to the ratchet-up mechanism alone, but does not reach the social optimum. A non-binding minimum has no significant effect. The difference is explained by conditional cooperation: non-compliance with the non-binding minimum leads to negative reciprocity and declining cooperation. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff as a main outcome, so findings are about contribution/cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a public goods game with a ratchet-up mechanism, adding a binding collective minimum contribution requirement increases cooperation rates, while a non-binding minimum does not. However, since efficiency or group payoff is not directly reported, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency. The absence of punishment or sanctioning means the results do not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design and results are highly relevant for understanding how minimum contribution rules (binding vs. non-binding) affect cooperation, but not for the effect of punishment per se.

- source: Zhang, Z., Zhang, X., & Putterman, L. (2019). Trust and cooperation at a confluence of worlds: An experiment in Xinjiang, China. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that both Uyghur and Han students contribute more in public goods games when grouped with coethnics than with outgroup members, and least with anonymous group members. However, allowing face-to-face communication in mixed-ethnicity groups leads to the highest cooperation levels, even exceeding those in homogeneous groups. The study does not include any punishment or reward mechanisms. The main outcomes are behavioral (contribution, trust, trustworthiness), and efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed by condition. The results suggest that communication can be a stronger facilitator of cooperation than ethnic homogeneity, and that social identifiability (even of outgroup members) increases cooperation relative to anonymity.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. However, it provides informative evidence about the effects of group composition and communication on cooperation rates. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may be useful for understanding baseline cooperation levels in the absence of punishment, and for considering the effects of communication and social context as moderators. The absence of punishment means it cannot inform the treatment-control efficiency difference for punishment-enabled games.

- source: Frey, U. J. (2019). Long-term evidence on cooperation and cultural differences in public goods dilemmas. *Biology Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in a real-world, online public goods game, average cooperation rates are low and relatively stable over time, with only minor differences between countries. Individual differences are more important than cultural, economic, or religious factors. The results suggest that cooperation in this context is lower than in typical laboratory public goods games, especially those with punishment. No punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the game, and the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a useful baseline for public goods games without punishment: average contribution rates are much lower than in laboratory experiments, and cultural background has little effect. However, it does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff, as no punishment mechanism is present or analyzed. For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment, this paper is only relevant as a control or baseline for no-punishment environments, especially in more naturalistic, long-term, or online settings.

- source: Lierl, M. (2016). Social sanctions and informal accountability: Evidence from a laboratory experiment. *Journal of Theoretical Politics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This laboratory experiment studies a public goods game with two beneficiaries and one provider, where the provider can embezzle contributions. The main manipulation is the structure of costly, mutual sanctioning (punishment and reward) relationships: (1) no sanctioning, (2) only among beneficiaries, (3) between provider and one or both beneficiaries, and (4) combinations thereof. The key finding is that mutual sanctioning among beneficiaries increases public goods provision and efficiency compared to no-sanctioning control, but this effect disappears or reverses when the provider is also part of the sanctioning network. The highest efficiency is observed when only beneficiaries can sanction each other. When both beneficiaries and the provider can mutually sanction, efficiency is not improved and can be lower than control. Actual punishment/reward is rare; the effect is mainly anticipatory. The results challenge the idea that denser or more encompassing sanctioning networks always improve efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games depends critically on the network structure of sanctioning relationships. If only beneficiaries can punish each other, enabling punishment increases efficiency relative to control. If the provider can also punish or be punished, the effect is reduced or negative. For prediction, the presence and direction of punishment effects should be conditioned on who can punish whom. The game is a close variant of a standard PGG, with the addition of a third-party provider who can embezzle, so results are most directly applicable to co-production or agency settings. Control efficiency (no-sanction) is reported, and treatment efficiency (with punishment) varies by network structure. The findings suggest that simply enabling punishment is not always efficiency-improving; the details of the punishment network matter.

- source: Colombier, N., Masclet, D., Mirza, D., & Montmarquette, C. (2011). GLOBAL SECURITY POLICIES AGAINST TERRORISM AND THE FREE RIDING PROBLEM: AN EXPERIMENTAL APPROACH. *Journal of Public Economic Theory*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a laboratory experiment simulating international counterterrorism cooperation as a public good with a stochastic loss, the authors find that, absent institutional incentives, most participants defect and invest in private protection rather than the collective good. Introducing punishment (sanction) or reward institutions significantly increases investment in the collective account. However, while both punishment and reward reduce the probability of a loss event and increase first-stage payoffs, only reward institutions lead to higher overall welfare. Punishment increases cooperation but the direct costs of punishing (borne by both punishers and targets) more than offset the efficiency gains from increased cooperation, resulting in lower final payoffs than the baseline. Reward institutions, by contrast, increase both cooperation and final payoffs. Thus, punishment is not efficiency-enhancing in this environment, while reward is.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a public-goods-like environment with costly punishment, enabling punishment increases cooperation but reduces overall efficiency compared to a no-punishment baseline. The costs of punishment (to both punisher and target) outweigh the benefits from increased cooperation. Therefore, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, one should expect a less positive or even negative effect on group payoff/welfare, especially when punishment is costly and the structure is similar to this experiment (n=4, 40 rounds, costly punishment, no identification of punishers, no chat, continuous contributions). The paper also shows that reward institutions can increase both cooperation and efficiency, in contrast to punishment.

- source: Abbink, K., Ryvkin, D., & Serra, D. (2020). Corrupt police. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares three main conditions: no police, honest police (can only fine), and corrupt police (can fine or demand bribes). With no police, crime rates are very high and citizen payoffs are low. Honest police reduce crime and increase citizen payoffs. Corrupt police also reduce crime compared to no police, but citizen payoffs are much lower than with honest police due to high bribe payments to officers, resulting in strong inequality. Bribes are used as a substitute for fines, and are often targeted at law-breakers, but imperfectly so—many law-abiding citizens are also extorted. When the maximum bribe is capped at the fine level, crime rates rise to near the no-police level, showing that the deterrent effect of bribes depends on their size. Reward mechanisms for officers based on low bribery rates (but not low crime rates) moderately reduce both bribery and crime, and increase citizen payoffs, but do not eliminate the efficiency loss from corruption.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that enabling punishment (via police, even if corrupt) increases group efficiency compared to no-punishment baselines, but the efficiency gain is much smaller when punishment is corrupt and redistributive (bribes) rather than legal (fines). The efficiency effect of punishment depends on both the targeting and the cost/impact of punishment: when bribes are large and targeted at law-breakers, crime is deterred, but citizen payoffs are much lower due to wealth transfer to officers. If bribes are capped at the fine level, the deterrent effect is lost. Reward mechanisms for officers based on low bribery rates can partially mitigate the efficiency loss from corruption. For prediction, the paper suggests that the presence of punishment increases efficiency relative to no-punishment, but the structure (honest vs. corrupt, bribe size, targeting) and the presence of anti-corruption incentives are critical moderators.

- source: Bravo, G., & Squazzoni, F. (2013). Exit, Punishment and Rewards in Commons Dilemmas: An Experimental Study. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a modified public goods game with six players per group and ten rounds per treatment, the introduction of punishment (a 5 MU fine for non-cooperation) significantly increased cooperation and efficiency compared to the control (no incentives). However, the highest efficiency (profit as a proportion of the theoretical optimum) was achieved when both voluntary participation (exit option) and positive incentives (5 MU reward for cooperation) were present. Punishment alone (No-Neg) led to 67% of the optimum, while reward plus exit (Ex-Pos) led to 69%. The control (No-Null) and punishment plus exit (Ex-Neg) both yielded 58%. Thus, while punishment increases efficiency over the control, it is less effective than the reward-plus-exit combination. The paper also finds that exit alone has little effect, and that the interaction between exit and incentives is important for maximizing efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a repeated public goods game with six players, ten rounds, no communication, and fixed institutional punishment (5 MU fine per defection), enabling punishment increases efficiency relative to the control (no punishment), but the efficiency gain is smaller than that achieved by combining rewards and voluntary participation. The efficiency effect of punishment is positive but not maximal. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, no chat, continuous contribution, fixed MPCR, fixed punishment cost/tech, no reward in punishment-only treatments, no identification of punishers, and full information about group outcomes) are all specified, making this paper highly informative for predicting efficiency under similar conditions.

- source: Wegmann, J., & Musshoff, O. (2019). Groundwater management institutions in the face of rapid urbanization - Results of a framed field experiment in Bengaluru, India. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that both externally imposed punishment and reward rules, implemented via random monitoring and sanctions/rewards for exceeding/following optimal extraction, significantly reduce over-extraction of groundwater in a framed field experiment with 600 real-world users. Social efficiency (group payoff relative to the social optimum) increased from 86% in the control to 97% under punishment and 95% under reward, with welfare gains of 8% and 6% over control, respectively (both statistically significant at 1%). Communication alone had a much smaller effect (90% efficiency, 1% gain, not significant). The effect of punishment and reward was not significantly different between rural and urban areas. User types (myopic, rational, social optimal) persisted across treatments, but all treatments crowded out the most pro-social (social optimal) users to some extent. The main outcome is that externally imposed monitoring and sanctioning (punishment) is highly effective at increasing efficiency in this CPR context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong, directly relevant evidence that, in a dynamic CPR game with real-world users, adding an externally imposed punishment mechanism (random monitoring, monetary penalty for over-extraction) increases group efficiency (payoff/welfare) by a substantial and statistically significant margin compared to a control with no punishment. The effect is robust to user type and urbanization. The game is not a standard linear PGG but is a close CPR variant with continuous extraction, group interdependence, and profit-based payoffs. The punishment cost and technology are specified, and the efficiency gains are quantified. This evidence should increase predicted efficiency for similar games when punishment is enabled, especially when the control efficiency is below the social optimum.

- source: van Miltenburg, N., Przepiorka, W., & Buskens, V. (2017). Consensual punishment does not promote cooperation in the six-person prisoner's dilemma game with noisy public monitoring. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares three punishment institutions (individual decision rule, CDR2, CDR3) in a six-person prisoner's dilemma, with and without 20% noise in public monitoring of contributions. In the absence of noise, all punishment institutions increase cooperation and earnings relative to the baseline, with collective decision rules (especially CDR3) performing best. With noise, however, all punishment institutions result in lower earnings than the baseline (no-punishment) condition. The individual decision rule (IDR) produces slightly higher cooperation than collective rules under noise, but still does not improve earnings. The main reason is that noise leads to both mistaken punishment of cooperators and missed punishment of defectors, so resources are wasted and cooperation is not effectively enforced. Thus, punishment does not promote efficiency in noisy environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a six-person all-or-nothing prisoner's dilemma with 20% noise in public monitoring, enabling punishment (regardless of whether it is individual or requires consensus) reduces group efficiency compared to the control (no-punishment) condition. The effect is robust across different punishment decision rules. The findings suggest that, when noise is present, the predicted efficiency with punishment enabled should be lower than the control efficiency. The results are directly relevant for settings with similar group size, binary contribution, and noisy monitoring, and less so for linear PGGs or settings with perfect information.

- source: De Geest, L. R., & Stranlund, J. K. (2019). Defending public goods and common-pool resources. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a 2x2 lab experiment (PG/CPR x Theft/No Theft), groups of four insiders played 15 rounds, with or without two outsiders who could steal surplus. Insiders could use costly sanctions (1:3 cost:impact) against outsiders. The main findings are: (a) In the CPR setting, the threat of theft increased cooperation and surplus creation, leading to higher group profits and surplus net of theft compared to the no-theft control. In the PG setting, the threat of theft did not significantly affect cooperation or surplus creation, and group profits decreased. (b) Sanctions were used more often in the PG setting, but were not effective at deterring outsider theft in either setting. (c) The presence of punishment did not restore efficiency to the fully cooperative benchmark, as insiders could not coordinate sanctions well enough to fully deter outsiders. (d) The introduction of theft increased variation in group outcomes, especially in the PG setting. Thus, the effect of punishment on efficiency is mixed: it does not reliably increase efficiency, and its effectiveness depends on the game structure and the ability of insiders to coordinate.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence on the effect of punishment (sanctions) on efficiency in public-goods-like environments with an external threat (outsider theft). The design allows for direct comparison of efficiency (group profit, surplus net of theft) with and without punishment enabled, under known game parameters. The results show that punishment does not reliably increase efficiency: in the CPR setting, the threat of theft (with punishment available) increases surplus creation and efficiency, but in the PG setting, efficiency decreases. In both cases, punishment is not effective at fully deterring theft, and group variation increases. For prediction, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is contingent on the type of social dilemma (PG vs CPR), the presence of an external threat, and the group's ability to coordinate sanctions. Control efficiency (no-theft, no-punishment) is higher than treatment efficiency (theft, punishment) in the PG setting, but lower in the CPR setting. The paper is highly informative for predicting treatment efficiency from design dimensions and control efficiency, especially in environments with external threats and endogenous punishment.

- source: Javaid, A., & Falk, T. (2015). Incorporating local institutions in irrigation experiments: evidence from rural communities in Pakistan. *Ecology and Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study implemented a sequential common-pool resource (CPR) game with five players per group, modeling irrigation dilemmas with asymmetric access. In the baseline (no institutional intervention), groups achieved high efficiency (average group earnings at 74% of the social optimum, group investment at 98% of the optimum). When external punishment (costly, probabilistic fines) was introduced, group earnings and efficiency significantly decreased (regression coefficient for group earnings: -9.03, p<0.1). The punishment mechanism reduced over-extraction by head-users but did not increase tail-user earnings; instead, it led to more water being wasted and lower group welfare. Communication and traditional authority treatments did not significantly affect group earnings or investment. The high baseline efficiency is attributed to strong local norms and the public revelation of individual extraction decisions. The main effect of punishment was to create additional coordination problems, not to improve efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in environments with high baseline efficiency and strong social information (public feedback on individual actions), introducing an external punishment mechanism can reduce group efficiency and welfare. The punishment treatment in this design (costly, probabilistic, not directly linked to rule violations) led to more resource wastage and lower group earnings. For prediction tasks, if the control (no-punishment) efficiency is already high and social information is available, enabling punishment may decrease efficiency. The effect is specific to the punishment design used here (costly, probabilistic, not directly rewarding the punisher). Other institutional interventions (communication, traditional authority) had no significant effect. The findings are most applicable to sequential CPR games with asymmetric access, public feedback, and close-knit communities.

- source: Cason, T. N., & Gangadharan, L. (2016). Swords without covenants do not lead to self-governance. *Journal of Theoretical Politics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment tested four treatments (baseline, punishment only, communication only, both) in a repeated nonlinear common pool resource game with groups of six. Peer punishment (costly, 3:1 fine-to-fee ratio, up to 5 points per target) did not significantly increase group yield or efficiency relative to baseline.
