Communication (anonymous chat, twice per session) led to dramatic and statistically significant increases in yield/efficiency, with most groups achieving near the social optimum. When both mechanisms were available, communication made punishment largely redundant and unused. The inefficacy of punishment is attributed to the complexity of the nonlinear environment and insufficient use of strong punishment. The findings challenge the generalizability of positive punishment effects from linear public goods games to more realistic, nonlinear settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in nonlinear common pool resource environments (with concave private benefits and endogenous group costs), enabling peer punishment does not improve efficiency over baseline. Therefore, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment in similar nonlinear games, one should expect little or no efficiency gain, even with a punishment technology that is effective in linear games. The presence of communication is a much stronger moderator, with large positive effects on efficiency. The results suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly sensitive to the payoff structure (linear vs nonlinear), and that control efficiency alone is not sufficient to predict treatment efficiency without considering the game's nonlinearity and the availability of communication.

- source: Vollan, B., Pröpper, M., Landmann, A., & Balafoutas, L. (2019). Self-Governance and Punishment: An Experimental Study among Namibian Forest Users. *Economic Development and Cultural Change*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study implemented a framed field/lab-in-the-field experiment with Namibian forest users, using a threshold public goods/extraction game with two punishment treatments: internal (peer) and external (random monitoring). Both punishment regimes reduced extraction compared to the baseline, but neither increased group or individual earnings (efficiency). Internal punishment was more effective at disciplining high extractors, especially those of low status, due to more targeted and severe peer punishment. However, internal punishment also led to high levels of antisocial punishment, which, together with the direct costs of punishment, resulted in lower overall earnings than both the external punishment and the no-punishment baseline. Ethnic heterogeneity increased antisocial punishment but did not reduce cooperation or prosocial punishment. The study concludes that while self-governance with peer punishment can discipline free riders, it may also generate substantial welfare losses due to costly and vengeful punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a threshold public goods/extraction game with both internal (peer) and external (centralized) punishment, the introduction of punishment mechanisms does not necessarily increase efficiency (group or individual earnings) and may even reduce it, especially when peer punishment is available. The main reason is that the costs of punishment (both direct and indirect, including antisocial punishment) can outweigh the benefits from increased cooperation. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, in similar environments (5 players, 20 rounds, MPCR 0.4, threshold/tipping point, no chat, anonymous punishment, no rewards), enabling punishment is likely to reduce efficiency relative to the control (no-punishment) condition, especially when peer punishment is allowed. The effect may be less negative or neutral with centralized punishment, but still not positive. The findings also highlight the importance of group composition (status, ethnicity) and the potential for antisocial punishment to undermine efficiency gains.

- source: Shreedhar, G., Tavoni, A., & Marchiori, C. (2020). Monitoring and punishment networks in an experimental common pool resource dilemma. *Environment and Development Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a nonlinear CPR lab experiment with three punishment network structures (complete, undirected circle, directed circle), the authors find that average appropriation (resource extraction) is similar across all networks. However, the amount and type of punishment, as well as efficiency (net payoffs), differ. The complete network, with perfect monitoring and punishment, leads to the highest punishment expenditures and the lowest efficiency. Both imperfect networks (undirected and directed circles) have lower punishment expenditures and higher efficiency. The undirected circle network elicits more severe and more prosocial punishment than the other two, but overall, both imperfect networks are more efficient than the complete network. The results suggest that reducing the number of potential punishers (i.e., making monitoring and punishment less than perfect) can increase efficiency by reducing costly punishment, even if cooperation (appropriation) does not change.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in nonlinear CPR games with punishment enabled, the structure of the punishment network (i.e., the number of potential punishers and the network's density/directedness) can significantly affect efficiency. Specifically, reducing punishment opportunities (imperfect networks) increases efficiency compared to perfect monitoring and punishment, because less punishment is used and thus less is wasted on costly sanctions. Appropriation (cooperation) rates remain similar, so the efficiency gain is due to lower punishment costs. For prediction, this suggests that, all else equal, enabling punishment in a perfect monitoring/punishment environment may reduce efficiency relative to imperfect punishment environments, and that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not always positive—it depends on the punishment network's structure. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, especially the structure of punishment opportunities.

- source: Ntuli, H., Crépin, A. S., Schill, C., & Muchapondwa, E. (2023). Sanctioned Quotas Versus Information Provisioning for Community Wildlife Conservation in Zimbabwe: A Framed Field Experiment Approach. *Environmental & Resource Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that introducing a sanctioned quota system (with costly punishment for exceeding quotas) leads to significantly higher efficiency in resource use compared to a baseline with no intervention. Information about ecological thresholds also improves efficiency, but not as much as punishment. Combining both interventions does not yield efficiency gains beyond punishment alone. The results are robust across multiple measures (efficiency, group payoff, resource stock, depletion cases) and are supported by regression analysis controlling for individual and group characteristics. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in CPR games with similar design features.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong, directly relevant evidence that enabling punishment (in the form of sanctioned quotas with costly, probabilistic enforcement) increases efficiency in a dynamic, multi-round, continuous-harvest CPR game with communication allowed. The effect is substantial: average efficiency rises from 0.42 (baseline) to 0.71 (punishment) and 0.77 (punishment+information). The design closely matches public-goods-game-like environments, though the context is a renewable resource with dynamic feedbacks. The results suggest that, for similar games with 4 players, communication, and continuous contribution/harvest, enabling punishment is likely to yield a large positive effect on efficiency, even when the control (no-punishment) efficiency is low. The effect is robust to the presence of communication and does not depend on end-game knowledge. The paper also provides evidence that the marginal per-capita return (mpcr) and punishment cost/tech are important, but the main result is that punishment is highly effective in this context.

- source: Koike, S., Nakamaru, M., Otaka, T., Shimao, H., Shimomura, K. I., & Yamato, T. (2018). Reciprocity and exclusion in informal financial institutions: An experimental study of rotating savings and credit associations. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment compared four treatments in a fixed-order ROSCA game: baseline (no sanctions), punishment rule (forfeiture), voting (exclusion), and both punishment and voting. Exclusion by voting led to significantly higher contribution rates and mean payoffs than the other treatments. The punishment rule, which prevented non-contributors from receiving payouts, did not increase contributions or payoffs relative to the baseline. The combination of voting and punishment was less effective than voting alone. The results suggest that social exclusion based on reputation is a more effective mechanism for sustaining cooperation and improving group efficiency than the explicit punishment rule tested here.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games, this paper provides strong evidence that a costless, rule-based punishment (forfeiture of payout for non-contributors) does not improve efficiency or mean payoff compared to a baseline with no punishment. In contrast, exclusion by peer voting (reputation-based exclusion) substantially increases both contributions and efficiency. The game structure is a close variant of a public goods game (ROSCA), with fixed group size, known rounds, and full information about others' actions. The findings suggest that not all punishment mechanisms are equally effective: exclusion based on group voting is effective, but a non-costly, rule-based punishment is not. This distinction is important for predicting the effect of punishment in similar environments.

- source: Robbett, A. (2016). Sustaining cooperation in heterogeneous groups. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a public goods game with heterogeneous, private or observable incentives, peer punishment (sanctions) does not significantly increase group contributions or efficiency above the baseline. This holds both when group members' types are private and when they are observable. In the private information setting, punishment is rarely used except against unambiguous free-riders, and the magnitude is too low to change incentives. When types are observable, punishment is more accurately targeted but still fails to reliably increase contributions, as some high-demanders resist increasing their contributions even when punished. In contrast, both unrestricted communication and a centralized bonus/fine mechanism (Falkinger) substantially increase efficiency, with communication allowing groups to coordinate on the socially optimal contribution vector. The results suggest that punishment is a weak tool for promoting efficiency in heterogeneous groups, especially when contribution norms are ambiguous.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public goods games with heterogeneous and possibly private incentives, enabling peer punishment does not reliably increase efficiency or group payoff above the baseline. The effect of punishment is weak or null, even when punishment is relatively effective (3:1 impact/cost ratio) and types are observable. Therefore, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment in similar heterogeneous PGG-like environments, one should expect little or no increase in efficiency relative to the control. The results also highlight the importance of communication and centralized mechanisms as more effective alternatives. The findings are directly relevant for settings with 3 players, 10 rounds, no chat (in punishment treatments), continuous contributions, and a non-linear payoff structure with interior optima.

- source: Fatas, E., Jimenez-Jimenez, F., & Morales, A. J. (2010). Blind Fines in Cooperatives. *Applied Economic Perspectives and Policy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that a blind, probabilistic punishment mechanism based on group performance can significantly increase average group quality (success ratio) in a cooperative production setting, but only when the cost of contributing high quality is not too high. In the LC treatment, average group quality rises from about 40% to about 70% of the maximum after punishment is introduced, while in the HC treatment, there is no significant improvement. The mechanism does not require individual monitoring, and its effectiveness depends on the underlying cost structure. The main outcome is group quality, which is closely related to efficiency, though not reported as explicit group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a group-level, blind punishment mechanism can substantially increase group performance (as measured by average quality/success ratio) in public-goods-like environments, but only when the cost of cooperation is not too high. For prediction tasks, the effect of punishment on efficiency is likely to be positive when the marginal cost of cooperation is low relative to the group benefit, and negligible when costs are high. The design features—no chat, fixed group size (n=4), 20 rounds per block, no individual monitoring, and punishment based on group outcome—are all relevant for mapping to prediction settings. The main limitation is that the outcome is group quality, not direct group payoff, but the two are tightly linked in this design.

- source: Jiang, L. L., Chen, Z., Perc, M., Wang, Z., Kurths, J., & Moreno, Y. (2023). Deterrence through punishment can resolve collective risk dilemmas in carbon emission games. *Chaos*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a lab experiment simulating a collective-risk public goods game (carbon emission game), costly punishment of free-riders (with fines imposed probabilistically and costs shared by all group members) increases the probability that groups reach the collective target, but only when the punishment risk is high (p > 0.8). At low punishment risk, deterrence is weak, and group failure rates remain high. Larger groups are less likely to succeed at any given punishment risk, requiring higher punishment risk for similar deterrence. The introduction of punishment shifts most players to fair-share contributions, reducing both free-riding and maximal altruism. The effect of punishment on group success (a proxy for efficiency) is robust to different loss probabilities. The study does not report efficiency as a ratio of actual to maximum possible group payoff, but the probability of reaching the target and average investment are closely related and show a positive effect of punishment when sufficiently strong.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in collective-risk public goods games with a threshold target and probabilistic costly punishment, enabling punishment increases group success rates (and thus efficiency) only when the punishment risk is high. The effect is moderated by group size (larger groups require higher punishment risk) and is robust to different loss probabilities. For prediction, the presence and strength of punishment (punishment risk, fine size, cost structure), group size, and the absence of communication are key moderators. The control (no-punishment) condition yields low group success rates, especially as group size increases. The findings support a more positive effect of punishment on efficiency when punishment is strong and credible, but little or no effect when punishment is weak or infrequent.

- source: Xu, C. Y., Qin, B. T., & Rawlings, D. (2022). Motivational crowding effects of monetary and nonmonetary incentives: Evidence from a common pool resources experiment in China. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a lab CPR game with 5 players per group and 18 rounds, the introduction of mandates with fines (punishment) for over-extraction significantly reduced extraction (i.e., increased conservation and group welfare) compared to a normative information baseline. High fines had a persistent 'crowd-in' effect on conservation, especially among those who initially extracted the least. Low fines had weaker or no persistent effects. In contrast, a public revelation mechanism (where one player's extraction is revealed each round) did not reduce extraction and led to a 'crowd-out' effect among conditional cooperators, likely because the revealed extractions were higher than expected, shifting descriptive norms upward. The study does not report efficiency as a ratio, but the extraction results map directly to group welfare. Heterogeneity analyses show that the effects of punishment and public revelation depend on behavioral type and prosocial orientation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a CPR game with 5 players, continuous extraction choices, no chat, and a known social optimum, the introduction of monetary punishment (especially with high fines) increases group welfare by reducing extraction, both during and after the intervention. The effect is strongest for high fines and among those with low initial extraction. Public revelation mechanisms may backfire, especially among conditional cooperators, if the revealed behavior is less cooperative than expected. For prediction, the presence of monetary punishment (with sufficient magnitude and justification) should be expected to increase efficiency relative to a normative information baseline. The mapping from extraction to efficiency is direct, so these results are highly informative for predicting efficiency outcomes in similar CPR or PGG-like environments.

- source: Grimalda, G., Belianin, A., Hennig-Schmidt, H., Requate, T., & Ryzhkova, M. (2022). Sanctions and international interaction improve cooperation to avert climate change. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment tested the effect of sanctions (costly punishment) in a repeated collective-risk social dilemma with German and Russian participants, both in national and international groups. Sanctions increased group cooperation (as measured by the probability of loss avoidance, PLA), especially for Russian participants and in international groups. However, the cost of sanctioning meant that final payoffs were actually higher in the no-sanction treatments, as shown in the supplementary material. The effect of sanctions was positive for increasing cooperation and loss avoidance, but negative for monetary efficiency. The combination of international interaction and sanctions was particularly effective at raising cooperation among low cooperators (Russians), leading to convergence with high cooperators (Germans). The effect of revealing nationality was minimal. Sanctioning was mostly prosocial in international groups, and antisocial sanctioning (punishing high contributors) was reduced. Only a small fraction of the endowment was spent on sanctions, but this was sufficient to increase cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling costly punishment (sanctions) in a repeated collective-risk social dilemma increases group cooperation (PLA) but reduces final payoffs due to the cost of sanctioning. For prediction tasks, this means that the efficiency of the punishment-enabled game may be higher or lower than the control, depending on whether the benefit from increased loss avoidance outweighs the cost of sanctions. In this experiment, the cost of sanctions was high enough that final payoffs were higher in the no-sanction condition, even though cooperation (PLA) was higher with sanctions. The effect is especially pronounced for groups with low baseline cooperation, and in international groups. The design dimensions most relevant for prediction are the presence and cost of punishment, group composition (nationality/culture), and the structure of the risk/loss. The game is a close variant of a PGG, but with a threshold and probabilistic loss, so direct transfer to standard PGGs should be cautious.

- source: Khadjavi, M., Lange, A., & Nicklisch, A. (2017). How transparency may corrupt - experimental evidence from asymmetric public goods games. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in an asymmetric public goods game (one official can embezzle, others can only contribute), transparency alone (without punishment) increases embezzlement by the official and reduces public good provision. Introducing a peer punishment stage (where group members can spend resources to reduce others' payoffs) increases public good provision and curbs embezzlement, but only if the official's identity is known. When both identity and actions are transparent (high transparency), punishment is more accurately targeted, reducing stigmatization and further increasing cooperation. Mean income is higher in punishment treatments, and income inequality between officials and citizens is reduced when punishment is possible and the official's identity is known.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling peer punishment in a public goods game with asymmetric roles (embezzlement possible for one player) increases group payoff and public good provision, especially when the official's identity is known. The effect is robust across both low and high transparency, but high transparency reduces erroneous punishment (stigmatization) and allows more targeted sanctions. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment mechanism (with a 1:3 cost-to-impact ratio), group size of 4, 10 rounds, and no communication, is associated with a substantial increase in efficiency-related outcomes compared to control. The effect is strongest when both identity and actions are transparent, but even low transparency with punishment is effective. Control efficiency (without punishment) is lower under high transparency due to increased embezzlement. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in asymmetric PGG-like environments with similar design features.

- source: Noussair, C. N., van Soest, D., & Stoop, J. (2015). Punishment, reward, and cooperation in a framed field experiment. *Social Choice and Welfare*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that introducing punishment or reward opportunities does not increase cooperation compared to baseline: average catch and effort are statistically indistinguishable across treatments. Punishment is directed at non-cooperators, but does not induce greater cooperation in subsequent periods. Reward is used more frequently than punishment, but is not targeted based on recipient cooperation. The results suggest that, in this field setting, peer punishment and reward are ineffective at increasing cooperation, in contrast to many laboratory PGG studies. No direct measures of group efficiency or total payoff are reported, but the behavioral proxies (catch and effort) do not improve with punishment or reward.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a field setting with a common-pool resource framed as a social dilemma, enabling peer punishment (and reward) does not increase cooperation or its behavioral proxies, compared to a baseline with no sanctions. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the positive effect of punishment on efficiency observed in many lab PGGs may not generalize to field environments with similar incentive structures but different context, framing, or real-world constraints. However, because the paper does not report explicit efficiency or group payoff outcomes, its direct value for predicting efficiency is limited; it is most informative as a caution about generalizability and as evidence that punishment may not always improve group outcomes, even when the game structure is similar to a PGG.

- source: Akpalu, W., & Martinsson, P. (2012). Ostracism and Common Pool Resource Management in a Developing Country: Young Fishers in the Laboratory. *Journal of African Economies*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Allowing group members to ostracize (permanently exclude) over-extractors via majority vote, even at a cost to the remaining group, sharply reduced overfishing compared to a baseline with no ostracism. In the baseline, extraction effort increased over time toward the Nash equilibrium (over-exploitation), while with ostracism, effort dropped toward the social optimum. The effect was immediate and strong after ostracism was introduced. Voting to ostracize was targeted at over-extractors, and the number of votes received was the main determinant of reduced extraction. The costliness of ostracism did not prevent its use. The study does not report group payoffs or efficiency ratios, but the reduction in extraction effort implies improved efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling a credible, group-administered punishment mechanism (ostracism) in a CPR environment can substantially reduce over-extraction and move behavior toward the social optimum, even when punishment is costly to the group. While efficiency is not directly measured, the reduction in extraction effort is a strong proxy for increased efficiency in this context. For prediction tasks, the presence of a strong, enforceable punishment mechanism (ostracism) is likely to increase efficiency relative to a no-punishment baseline, especially in settings with no communication, fixed group membership, and a known time horizon. The specific design (majority vote, permanent exclusion, cost to punishers) is important for generalization.

- source: de Melo, G., & Piaggio, M. (2015). The perils of peer punishment: Evidence from a common pool resource framed field experiment. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Nonmonetary peer punishment (social disapproval) reduces resource extraction (increases cooperation) in mixed-community groups but has no significant effect in in-group (same community) groups. The effect is measured as a reduction in the average number of nets used per person. There is substantial antisocial punishment (punishing cooperators), which can undermine the effectiveness of punishment. The study does not directly report group efficiency or total payoff, but lower extraction implies higher efficiency. The presence of antisocial punishment and lack of a unique social norm limit the positive effect of punishment. The findings suggest that the context (group composition) moderates the effect of punishment on cooperation, and that coordination is needed for punishment to be effective.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that enabling peer punishment (social disapproval) can reduce resource extraction (increase cooperation) in common pool resource games, especially in mixed-group settings. However, the effect on efficiency is not directly measured; it must be inferred from extraction levels. The presence of antisocial punishment and lack of norm consensus can limit or even reverse the positive effect of punishment. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that punishment is more likely to improve efficiency in mixed-group contexts than in homogeneous groups, but the risk of antisocial punishment and unclear norms can reduce or negate efficiency gains. The specific game design (field context, nonmonetary punishment, no communication, continuous extraction choice) should be considered when generalizing.

- source: Gatiso, T. T., & Vollan, B. (2017). Democracy and cooperation in commons management: experimental evidence of representative and direct democracy from community forests in Ethiopia. *Environment and Development Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Democratic procedures (either direct or representative) for rule or leader selection significantly increase cooperation in a dynamic CPR extraction game compared to imposed rules or leaders. The effect is not due to the presence of sanctions per se, but to the legitimacy conferred by democratic procedures. Sanctions only increase cooperation when democratically chosen. There is no significant difference in cooperation between direct and representative democracy. The main outcome is behavioral (harvest ratio), not efficiency or group payoff. The study does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or total group earnings, but the design allows for such calculation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that democratic procedures for rule or leader selection increase cooperation in CPR-like environments, and that sanctions only increase cooperation when democratically chosen. However, the main outcome is behavioral (harvest ratio), not efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results are only indirectly informative: higher cooperation (lower HR) likely implies higher efficiency, but the paper does not quantify this. The game is a dynamic CPR extraction game, not a standard linear PGG, but is closely related. The findings suggest that the legitimacy of the process (democratic choice) is a key moderator of punishment/sanction effectiveness. The paper does not provide direct evidence on how punishment affects efficiency, so its value for efficiency prediction is indirect and should be weighted accordingly.

- source: Mulder, L. B., van Dijk, E., & De Cremer, D. (2009). When sanctions that can be evaded still work: The role of trust in leaders. *Social Influence*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that the effectiveness of sanctions in promoting cooperation depends on both the trustworthiness of the leader who installs the sanction and the general trust participants have in authorities. When an alternative, more harmful defection option is available (i.e., sanctions can be evaded), participants with low general trust in authorities are less likely to cooperate if the leader is untrustworthy, and instead shift to the evasion option. High-trust participants are less sensitive to leader trustworthiness. The main outcomes are behavioral allocations; the paper does not report group payoff or efficiency, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the presence of an evadable punishment option (i.e., a trilemma with an alternative defection route) can undermine the effectiveness of sanctions in promoting cooperation, especially among participants with low trust in authorities and when the leader is perceived as untrustworthy. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. It does suggest that in designs where punishment can be evaded, the positive effect of punishment on cooperation (and thus efficiency) may be reduced or even reversed for some subgroups. For prediction, this paper is most informative about the moderating role of evasion options and trust, but not about the quantitative effect on efficiency.

- source: De Cremer, D., Hoogervorst, N., & Desmet, P. (2012). Procedural Justice and Sanctions in Social Dilemmas: The Moderating Effects of Group Feedback and Identification. *Journal of Applied Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that accurate sanctioning systems (those that monitor all contribution sessions) increase contributions relative to inaccurate systems, but this effect depends on group identification and feedback. For high group identifiers, procedural justice (accuracy) increases contributions only after group failure; for low identifiers, it increases contributions only after group success. The experiment uses a step-level public good game with a threshold and a punishment mechanism for the lowest contributor, but does not report efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings. The findings highlight the importance of procedural justice in sanctioning systems for promoting cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence about effects on efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing contributions depends on the perceived procedural justice (accuracy) of the sanctioning system, and that this effect is moderated by group identification and feedback about group success or failure. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results suggest that, in step-level public good games with punishment, the design of the punishment system (especially its perceived fairness and accuracy) and the social context (identification, feedback) can strongly moderate behavioral responses, which may in turn affect efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that punishment will be more effective at increasing cooperation (and possibly efficiency) when the system is accurate and procedurally just, but the effect is context-dependent.

- source: Kornhauser, L., Lu, Y. J., & Tontrup, S. (2020). Testing a fine is a price in the lab. *International Review of Law and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment shows that introducing a fine for non-compliance (either small or proportional) reduces compliance (performance) among pro-social subjects (crowding-out effect) and increases it among pro-self subjects (price effect). When the fine is framed as a penalty (collected by the experimenter, not the partner), the crowding-out effect disappears and pro-socials' compliance is restored. The overall mean performance across all subjects does not differ significantly between treatments with and without fines, due to the offsetting effects. The study provides strong evidence for the fine-is-a-price mechanism as the cause of crowding out, but does not measure or report group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment/fines on compliance in public-goods-like environments depends on the social value orientation of participants: fines crowd out compliance among pro-socials but increase it among pro-selfs. However, because the study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about efficiency outcomes. The findings suggest that the net effect of punishment on efficiency may be neutral in mixed populations, but could be negative in highly pro-social groups and positive in highly pro-self groups. The design and manipulation of punishment (including framing as price vs. penalty) are critical moderators. For prediction tasks, the absence of explicit efficiency data limits direct applicability, but the behavioral patterns are informative for understanding potential efficiency effects in similar designs.

- source: Inglis, R. F., West, S., & Buckling, A. (2014). An experimental study of strong reciprocity in bacteria. *Biology Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in bacterial populations, cooperative punishers (who both produce a public good and punish non-cooperators) can invade a population of cheats only when the traits for cooperation and punishment are tightly linked. When the linkage is broken (by mixing punishing and non-punishing cooperators), invasion fails. Non-punishing cooperators alone also cannot invade. The main outcome is the frequency and survival/extinction of the different strains, not group efficiency or total payoff. The results support theoretical predictions that linkage is necessary for the evolutionary success of strong reciprocity in this context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. It shows that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation (and by implication, group efficiency) depends critically on the linkage between cooperation and punishment. However, the study does not measure efficiency or group payoff directly, and the outcomes are evolutionary (strain invasion/extinction) rather than economic payoffs. The findings suggest that in systems where punishment and cooperation are not tightly linked, punishment may not improve group outcomes. For prediction tasks, this paper is most informative about the importance of linkage or coupling between cooperation and punishment, but less so about the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency in standard public goods games.

- source: Makowsky, M. D., & Wang, S. Y. (2018). Embezzlement, whistleblowing, and organizational architecture: An experimental investigation. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a laboratory experiment with 6-player groups, participants played a common pool resource game with a whistleblowing option: after each withdrawal, any non-first-mover could choose to 'blow the whistle', setting all group payoffs to zero. The game was played under six different organizational structures, varying the number of sequential levels (from 1 to 6) and the arrangement of players (pyramid, inverted pyramid, flat, etc.). The main findings are: (1) Embezzlement rates are high in all structures, but increase with the number of sequential levels; (2) The probability of group-level punishment (whistleblowing) increases with the number of levels; (3) Average group income (efficiency) decreases as the number of levels increases; (4) Flatter (simultaneous) structures have higher efficiency than taller (sequential) ones; (5) Pyramid structures have lower embezzlement than inverted pyramids, but this does not translate into significant efficiency differences due to similar rates of group-level punishment. The punishment mechanism is highly costly and does not improve efficiency; rather, it reduces it further when triggered.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public-goods-like environments with a costly, group-level punishment mechanism (whistleblowing that sets all payoffs to zero), enabling punishment does not improve efficiency and may reduce it, especially as the game becomes more sequential (taller). The efficiency loss is driven by both high embezzlement and the destructive nature of the punishment. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that, when the control (no-punishment) game already has low efficiency due to high embezzlement, adding a highly destructive punishment option (like whistleblowing) is likely to further reduce efficiency, particularly in games with more sequential moves or more levels. The design dimensions most relevant are the number of players, number of rounds, the structure of play (sequential vs. simultaneous), and the nature/cost of punishment. The results are most applicable to settings where punishment is costly and group-level (not targeted), and less so to standard peer-to-peer punishment in classic PGGs.

- source: Freytag, A., Güth, W., Koppel, H., & Wangler, L. (2014). Is regulation by milestones efficiency enhancing? An experimental study of environmental protection. *European Journal of Political Economy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: This laboratory experiment tests whether adding intermediate contribution targets ('milestones') to a cumulative threshold public goods game increases efficiency, measured by group payoffs and the probability of reaching a final target. The game is played in groups of 5 over 6 rounds, with treatments varying the strictness of milestones, the marginal per-capita return (mpcr), and the probability of group loss if targets are missed. The main findings are: (1) In a scenario with no continuous efficiency gains (i.e., only reaching the target matters), strict milestones increase both contributions and the probability of reaching the target, thus enhancing efficiency. (2) In a scenario with continuous efficiency gains (i.e., contributions always increase payoffs), strict milestones actually reduce average contributions and efficiency, as groups focus on meeting milestones rather than maximizing payoffs. (3) In an intermediate scenario, the positive and negative effects cancel out. The effect of milestones is thus context-dependent, and their efficiency impact is not universally positive.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment-like milestone mechanisms on efficiency in public-goods-like games is highly sensitive to the underlying payoff structure. When the only way to generate group payoffs is to reach a threshold (no continuous efficiency gains), strict milestones (punishment for missing intermediate targets) can improve efficiency by increasing coordination and contributions. However, when continuous efficiency gains are present (i.e., contributions always increase payoffs), strict milestones can reduce efficiency, as they may distract from maximizing group welfare. For prediction, this means that the presence and structure of milestone/punishment mechanisms, the marginal per-capita return, and the risk of group loss must all be considered. Control efficiency (without milestones/punishment) is a strong moderator: if control efficiency is already high, milestones may not help and can even hurt. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from design dimensions in cumulative threshold public goods games with milestone/punishment features.

- source: Sääksvuori, L. (2014). Intergroup conflict, ostracism, and the evolution of cooperation under free migration. *Behavioral Ecology and Sociobiology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: This paper experimentally studies a dynamic public goods game with endogenous group formation, free migration, and treatments varying the presence of intergroup competition and ostracism (social exclusion by majority vote). The main findings are: (1) Ostracism increases average group size and contributions to the public good, resulting in higher net payoffs and aggregate returns, both with and without intergroup competition. (2) Intergroup competition alone does not increase group size or contributions, and leads to more imbalanced group structures, which reduces aggregate returns. (3) The combination of ostracism and competition still produces higher cooperation and payoffs than competition alone. (4) The threat of ostracism reduces the number of free-agents and increases the desirability of joining groups, outweighing the effect of involuntary exclusion. (5) Intergroup competition reduces the likelihood of ostracism, as groups are less willing to expel members when facing external threats.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong evidence that enabling a social sanctioning mechanism (ostracism) in a dynamic, migration-allowed public goods environment increases both cooperation and group-level payoffs (i.e., efficiency), regardless of whether intergroup competition is present. The effect of ostracism is robust and positive for efficiency-related outcomes. Intergroup competition alone does not improve efficiency and may even reduce it by fragmenting groups. The design features—such as endogenous group formation, free migration, and ostracism by majority vote—are important moderators. The paper does not use classic costly punishment (with explicit cost per punishment point), but ostracism functions as a group-level sanction. Thus, for environments with similar group formation and migration, the presence of ostracism is a strong positive predictor for efficiency gains from sanctioning.

- source: Diekert, F., Eymess, T., Luomba, J., & Waichman, I. (2022). The Creation of Social Norms under Weak Institutions. *Journal of the Association of Environmental and Resource Economists*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that providing social information about high or low cooperation rates only leads to stable differences in cooperation when a weak social sanctioning institution is present. With sanctioning, cooperation rates remain high if cooperation is emphasized and low if defection is emphasized; without sanctioning, cooperation declines over time regardless of the message. The sanctioning mechanism is a group vote to exclude a strategy from a small bonus, and is used infrequently. The effect of social information and sanctioning is stronger for participants with higher social proximity to others in their session. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on cooperation rates and beliefs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of a (weak) social sanctioning institution is necessary for norm-based interventions (social information) to create stable high cooperation in repeated binary social dilemma games. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is close to a public goods game (binary prisoner's dilemma with group returns), and the sanctioning is a group-level exclusion from a small bonus, not direct costly punishment. The findings suggest that, in similar binary games with weak sanctioning, punishment can stabilize cooperation rates, but the effect on efficiency is not directly measured. The results are most useful for predicting behavioral (cooperation) outcomes rather than efficiency.

- source: Bastian, B., Brewer, M., Duffy, J., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2019). From cash to crickets: The non-monetary value of a resource can promote human cooperation. *Journal of Environmental Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that highlighting the non-monetary value of a resource (e.g., the moral significance of not harming crickets) leads to reduced personal consumption in a resource dilemma game, indicating increased cooperation. This effect is robust across two studies and is not moderated by individual differences in empathy or moral expansiveness. When a punishment mechanism is introduced (participants can pay to fine a greedy player), there is no significant difference in punishment behavior between the moral-value and control conditions. The main outcome is behavioral (points taken), not direct group efficiency or payoff, but the results suggest that moral framing can promote cooperation even in the presence of defection and punishment opportunities.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that moral or non-monetary framing of the resource can increase cooperation (reduce consumption) in resource dilemma games, which may translate to higher group efficiency if mapped to payoff outcomes. However, the study does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, and the punishment mechanism (costly punishment of defectors) did not significantly affect behavior. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that game design features that increase the perceived moral value of the resource may enhance cooperation, but the effect of punishment per se on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper. The design is close to a public goods/common pool resource game, but not a standard PGG, and the main outcomes are not efficiency or payoff-based.

- source: Brick, K., & Visser, M. (2010). Meeting a national emission reduction target in an experimental setting. *Climate Policy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Voluntary cooperation alone is insufficient to meet a group emission reduction target in a public good game with heterogeneous players. Allowing communication increases average contributions and the proportion of groups meeting the target (from 35% to 50%), but also increases polarization between full cooperation and free-riding. Introducing a tax-based sanction (automatic fine for under-contribution) leads to near-universal compliance with the target (88–91% of groups meet the target), but also crowds out voluntary contributions above the minimum required. The effect of taxation on contributions above the minimum differs by player type: for capital, taxation reduces excess contributions; for labour, taxation can encourage excess contributions relative to baseline. The study does not directly analyze efficiency as a ratio of actual to maximum possible group payoff, but the compliance rates and average earnings suggest that tax-based sanctions are highly effective at achieving the group target.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that automatic, rule-based sanctions (taxes/fines for under-contribution) are highly effective at increasing compliance with group targets in public good games, even with heterogeneous players. Communication alone improves cooperation but is less reliable than sanctions. The findings are most relevant for predicting the effect of institutional (not peer) punishment on group compliance and contributions, especially in settings with asymmetric player types and marginal returns. However, because the study does not report efficiency in the standard sense (group payoff as a share of maximum possible), its direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. Still, the high compliance rates under tax-based sanctions suggest that efficiency would be much higher with punishment enabled than in the voluntary or communication-only conditions.

- source: Solda, A., & Villeval, M. C. (2020). EXCLUSION AND REINTEGRATION IN A SOCIAL DILEMMA. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment investigates endogenous and exogenous exclusion (ostracism) in a club-good setting where group members can vote to exclude others who over-extract from the group resource. Exclusion is primarily targeted at those who withdraw more than the group average, supporting the idea that exclusion enforces group norms. Upon reintegration, previously excluded members tend to reduce their extraction, indicating some deterrent or corrective effect of exclusion. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total group payoff, or welfare, so the effect of exclusion on efficiency cannot be directly assessed. The findings are most relevant for understanding behavioral responses to exclusion and the use of ostracism as a sanction, rather than for predicting efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the behavioral effects of exclusion (ostracism) as a sanctioning mechanism in public-good-like environments. It shows that exclusion can deter norm violations (over-extraction) and that reintegrated members may adjust their behavior. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment (exclusion) on efficiency. The design dimensions (e.g., group size, exclusion rules) are described, but their effects are only analyzed in terms of behavioral outcomes, not efficiency. Thus, this paper is useful for understanding the mechanisms and behavioral pathways by which exclusion might affect efficiency, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes.

- source: Corgnet, B. (2012). PEER EVALUATIONS AND TEAM PERFORMANCE: WHEN FRIENDS DO WORSE THAN STRANGERS. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that using peer evaluations to allocate team profits in a real-effort team task leads to lower team performance (measured as a percentage of maximum possible profits) than an equal split rule. This effect is statistically significant and is stronger in teams where members are acquaintances or friends. The mechanism appears to involve a crowding-out of intrinsic motivation and increased negative reciprocity when peer evaluations are used among friends. The majority of peer assessments follow equality norms, but a significant minority use equity (contribution-based) norms. The results suggest that peer evaluation mechanisms may not always improve efficiency, especially in contexts with strong interpersonal relationships.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in small real-effort team settings with face-to-face communication and high social proximity, introducing a peer evaluation mechanism for allocating payoffs (a form of endogenous sanction/reward) can reduce efficiency compared to a fixed equal split. This is contrary to standard public goods game findings where punishment or reward options often increase efficiency. The negative effect is especially pronounced when team members are friends or classmates. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of introducing peer-based sanction/reward mechanisms on efficiency is context-dependent: in environments with high social ties and communication, such mechanisms may reduce efficiency relative to control. The findings are most relevant for small teams with real-effort tasks, face-to-face interaction, and endogenous allocation of payoffs via peer assessment.

- source: Lévy-Garboua, L., Montmarquette, C., Vaksmann, J., & Villeval, M. C. (2017). Voluntary Contributions to a Mutual Insurance Pool. *Journal of Public Economic Theory*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Contribution-based coverage, which ties insurance payouts to individual contributions, significantly increases the mean coverage rate (efficiency) compared to equal coverage, where payouts are independent of contribution. In the experiment (n=12, 50 rounds), the mean coverage rate is about 26% under equal coverage and 47% under contribution-based coverage for the full sample, with even higher rates for risk-averse (high-contributing) subgroups. However, full insurance is not achieved due to coordination failures and heterogeneity in risk attitudes. The endogenous 'punishment' of low contributors (via low coverage) is effective at raising efficiency, but only partially. Aggregate earnings are similar across treatments, but the distribution of earnings is more equitable under contribution-based coverage, with reduced transfers from high to low contributors.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that mechanisms which endogenously punish low contributors (by reducing their insurance coverage) can substantially increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments with risk and voluntary contributions. The effect is robust but incomplete: efficiency rises from about 26% to 47% of the optimum when moving from equal to contribution-based coverage, but does not reach full efficiency. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of introducing a punishment-like mechanism (here, endogenous sanctioning via coverage rules) in similar games. The results are most applicable to settings with continuous contributions, no explicit communication, and group sizes around 12. The paper does not study explicit costly punishment, but the contribution-based coverage mechanism functions as an endogenous, payoff-based sanction. Control efficiency (equal coverage) is low, and the treatment (contribution-based) nearly doubles efficiency. Heterogeneity in risk attitudes is a key moderator.

- source: Perez, I., Yu, D. J., Janssen, M. A., & Anderies, J. M. (2015). Social roles and performance of social-ecological systems: evidence from behavioral lab experiments. *Ecology and Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that group success in a lab irrigation game is best explained by the presence of certain combinations of emergent social roles, rather than any single role. All groups had leaders, knowledge generators, and followers, but successful groups also had connectors and either moralists or observers. The enforcer role (which involves punishing others' behavior, e.g., via threats to withhold investment) was not necessary for group success and was more common in unsuccessful groups. The presence of enforcers appears to be a symptom of conflict and lack of cooperation, not a cause of improved efficiency. The experiment does not include a formal punishment mechanism (e.g., costly punishment with explicit payoff consequences), so findings about 'enforcer' roles are based on communication and threats rather than actual sanctioning actions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of enabling or disabling formal punishment mechanisms on efficiency in public goods or common-pool resource games. Instead, it provides evidence that informal enforcement roles (as expressed in chat) are not sufficient or necessary for group success, and that their presence may indicate underlying conflict. For prediction tasks about the effect of punishment on efficiency, this paper suggests that the mere presence of enforcement or sanctioning behavior (in the absence of formal mechanisms) does not guarantee improved efficiency. The findings are most relevant for settings with communication and emergent informal roles, not for games with explicit, costly punishment options.

- source: Brent, D. A., Gangadharan, L., Mihut, A., & Villeval, M. C. (2019). Taxation, redistribution, and observability in social dilemmas. *Journal of Public Economic Theory*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Both Pigovian tax and increasing block rate mechanisms, when combined with redistribution, lead to significantly higher group efficiency (mean payoff as a ratio to the social optimum increases from 0.86 in baseline to 0.97–0.98 with tax, p<0.01) and lower inequality, regardless of whether individual decisions are observable. Observability alone (without tax) increases extraction and reduces efficiency (mean payoff ratio drops to 0.82), contrary to the expectation that it would promote cooperation. When tax mechanisms are present, observability has no significant effect on efficiency or extraction. There is no significant difference in efficiency between the two tax mechanisms, but the Pigovian tax reduces inequality more. The results are robust across group types and over time.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a CPR environment with heterogeneous extraction capacities, introducing a monetary sanction mechanism (taxation with redistribution) substantially increases efficiency compared to a baseline with no sanctions, regardless of whether decisions are observable. The effect is robust to the presence or absence of observability (i.e., information about others' actions). The design is not a standard linear PGG, but the CPR game is closely related and the efficiency outcome is directly comparable. The findings suggest that, for similar game designs, enabling a monetary sanction mechanism (taxation) will predictably increase efficiency, and the effect is not moderated by observability. The results do not speak directly to peer-to-peer punishment, but do inform the effect of centralized monetary sanctions.

- source: Lang, M. R., Chvaja, R., & Purzycki, B. G. (2024). The role of costly commitment signals in assorting cooperators during intergroup conflict. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper demonstrates that costly commitment signals can facilitate the assortment of cooperators in public-goods-game-like environments with intergroup competition, leading to higher group efficiency and greater probability of winning. However, the relationship between signal cost and efficiency is not monotonic: low-cost signals are generally more effective at producing efficient, cooperative groups, except when the cost is directly subtracted from the investable endowment during intergroup conflict, in which case high-cost signals can create highly efficient, winning groups. Forced signaling (where participants are assigned to signal or not) does not reliably increase efficiency or group payoff. The study does not include a punishment phase or peer-sanctioning mechanism, so its direct relevance to punishment is limited, but the costly signal serves as a commitment device that can be interpreted as a form of self-imposed cost or pre-commitment. The findings suggest that the design of commitment mechanisms (including their cost structure and voluntariness) is crucial for predicting their effect on group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting the effect of costly commitment mechanisms (but not peer punishment) on group efficiency in public-goods-game-like settings with intergroup competition. It shows that voluntary, low-cost signals are generally more effective at producing efficient groups, except when the cost is directly subtracted from the investable endowment, in which case high-cost signals can be effective. Forced (non-voluntary) signaling does not reliably increase efficiency. The findings suggest that the effect of commitment mechanisms on efficiency is sensitive to the cost structure, voluntariness, and whether the cost reduces investable resources. However, because the study does not include peer punishment or sanctions, its direct applicability to standard punishment treatments is limited. For prediction tasks involving commitment devices or entry costs, the results are directly relevant; for peer punishment, the transfer value is more indirect.

- source: Stoddard, B. V., Cox, C. A., & Walker, J. M. (2021). Incentivizing provision of collective goods: Allocation rules. *Southern Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: In a lab experiment with repeated collective good provision games, the introduction of a third-party allocator who can flexibly assign shares of the group fund (based on observed contributions) leads to higher group provision than a baseline with equal sharing. The two most flexible allocator mechanisms (PA-Flex and PA-Rules) both result in significantly higher provision than the baseline (PE-PE), and also outperform a less flexible allocator mechanism (PA-SWW). The highest levels of provision are observed in the most flexible mechanism (PA-Flex), though a small number of groups with ineffective allocators show low provision. The study does not directly report efficiency as a normalized payoff ratio, but group provision is closely related and higher provision implies higher group payoffs. The findings indicate that flexible allocation rules that allow for contribution-contingent rewards and punishments can strongly increase collective good provision.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public-goods-like environments where a third-party allocator can flexibly assign shares of the collective good based on contributions, group provision (and thus efficiency) increases substantially compared to equal sharing. The effect is robust across two flexible allocator mechanisms and is strongest when the allocator has maximal flexibility. The results suggest that, when predicting the effect of introducing a flexible, contribution-contingent allocation mechanism (a form of endogenous reward/punishment), one should expect a large positive effect on group efficiency, provided the allocator uses the mechanism effectively. However, the effect may be heterogeneous if some allocators do not use the mechanism to incentivize contributions. The study does not use classic peer punishment, but the allocation mechanism serves a similar incentive function. The findings are most directly relevant for settings where a third party can allocate payoffs after observing contributions, rather than direct peer-to-peer punishment.

- source: Angelovski, A., Neugebauer, T., & Servátka, M. (2019). RANK-ORDER COMPETITION IN THE VOLUNTARY PROVISION OF IMPURE PUBLIC GOODS. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: Rank-Order-VCM, which rewards higher contributors with a larger share of the public good (via a higher personal multiplier), leads to significantly higher and more sustained contributions than a control treatment with random assignment of multipliers. In Rank-Order-VCM, median contributions are at the maximum in almost all rounds, while in the control, contributions decline to near zero. The mechanism avoids the efficiency loss of fixed-prize contests by not subtracting a prize from the pool. The results suggest that introducing rank-based competition for access to an impure public good can mitigate free-riding and move outcomes closer to the social optimum, increasing group welfare and implied efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a rank-based competition mechanism (where higher contributors receive a larger share of the public good) can dramatically increase contributions and implied efficiency compared to a control with random assignment of benefits. While the main reported outcomes are contributions, the payoff structure ensures that higher contributions translate into higher group payoffs and efficiency, especially since no costly prize is subtracted from the pool. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that mechanisms which create endogenous, contribution-dependent rewards (even without explicit punishment or costly rewards) can outperform standard VCMs and fixed-prize contests in terms of efficiency. However, the results are most directly applicable to impure public goods with preferential access, not to standard PGGs with explicit punishment or peer-sanctioning. The control efficiency (with random assignment) is much lower than in the treatment, so the predicted treatment efficiency should be substantially higher under similar design parameters.

- source: Howe, E. L., Murphy, J. J., Gerkey, D., & West, C. T. (2016). Indirect Reciprocity, Resource Sharing, and Environmental Risk: Evidence from Field Experiments in Siberia. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The experiment found that voluntary sharing was substantial and directed toward those in need, especially when reputations could be built. Sharing was conditioned on cooperative behavior when such information was available. However, the presence of sharing, rewards, and reputation mechanisms did not increase contributions to the public good (i.e., did not increase cooperation in the production domain). The expected returns from contributing to the group activity, even with the possibility of receiving sharing, were not high enough to incentivize greater cooperation. Thus, the mechanisms studied did not improve group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper suggests that, in environments where sharing is voluntary and not highly efficient, and where rewards/punishments are not strong or direct, the introduction of sharing and reputation mechanisms may not increase group efficiency or cooperation in public goods games. The findings imply that unless the payoff structure makes rewards or punishments highly effective, simply enabling sharing or reputation is unlikely to boost efficiency. For prediction, if the control game has low efficiency and the only change is the addition of a voluntary, non-committal sharing/reward mechanism (with no direct punishment and no high-impact reward), the efficiency in the treatment is unlikely to increase. The paper provides indirect evidence that the effectiveness of punishment/reward mechanisms depends on their efficiency and observability.

- source: Becchetti, L., Pelligra, V., & Salustri, F. (2018). The impact of redistribution mechanisms in the vote with the wallet game: experimental results. *Social Choice and Welfare*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The introduction of an ex post redistribution mechanism (taxing defectors and subsidizing cooperators) in a repeated, all-or-nothing public good game significantly increases the share of cooperative choices compared to the baseline. In the baseline, cooperation decays over time, but with redistribution, cooperation is stabilized at a higher level. Framing the responsible product as socially beneficial also increases cooperation, but the effect of redistribution is robust across frames. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so the effect on efficiency cannot be directly determined, but the increase in cooperation rates suggests likely higher group payoffs under redistribution.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that a centralized, ex post redistribution mechanism (akin to a tax/subsidy rather than peer punishment) can substantially increase cooperation rates in a repeated, all-or-nothing public good game with 10 players and no communication. While efficiency or group payoff is not directly reported, the structure of the payoffs and the increase in cooperation rates imply that group efficiency would also increase under the redistribution mechanism. The findings are most directly relevant for predicting the effect of centralized, automatic sanction/reward mechanisms (not peer punishment) in all-or-nothing, no-communication, 10-player repeated games with known rounds and MPCR of 0.3. The results are less directly applicable to standard peer punishment settings, but suggest that institutionalized redistribution can be highly effective at sustaining cooperation.

- source: Cacault, M. P., Goette, L., Lalive, R., & Thoenig, M. (2015). Do we harm others even if we don't need to?. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when participants are given the option to harm an out-group (even at a cost or with no additional benefit to their own group), a significant minority choose to do so, and this behavior is often motivated by a desire to benefit the in-group at the expense of the out-group. The presence of anti-social options (aggression) increases non-selfish contributions and stabilizes in-group cooperation over repeated rounds, compared to a standard public good game where cooperation declines over time. The effect is especially pronounced in later rounds. However, the paper does not analyze or report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, so it is unclear whether the increased cooperation offsets the welfare losses from aggression. The findings are relevant for understanding the behavioral complementarity between in-group cooperation and out-group aggression, but do not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the availability of anti-social options (aggression) can increase in-group cooperation and non-selfish contributions in a public good game variant, especially in later rounds.
