However, because the anti-social options reduce the payoffs of out-groups and the paper does not report group efficiency or total earnings, it is not possible to determine whether the net effect on efficiency is positive or negative. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff, this paper suggests that behavioral responses to punishment-like options can increase cooperation, but may also introduce welfare-reducing behaviors. The design is adjacent to standard punishment mechanisms (since aggression is not used to enforce norms but to harm others), so caution is needed in transferring these findings to standard PGG punishment settings.

- source: Secchi, S., & Banerjee, S. (2019). A dynamic semester-long social dilemma game for economic and interdisciplinary courses. *Journal of Economic Education*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that students, when given the opportunity, may devise and implement their own institutional solutions to the common-pool resource dilemma, including costly peer punishment. The game is effective for teaching about the dynamics of cooperation, cheating, and institutional innovation in resource management. However, the paper does not provide quantitative evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it systematically compare outcomes with and without punishment. The main findings are qualitative and pedagogical, emphasizing the diversity of student-devised solutions and the context-dependence of group behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides limited direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games. While it confirms that peer punishment can emerge and is sometimes used by participants to address over-extraction, it does not provide quantitative or systematic evidence on its impact on efficiency or group payoff. The game design dimensions are described in detail, but the effect of punishment is only discussed anecdotally. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that allowing for endogenous punishment mechanisms can lead to creative solutions and potentially improved group outcomes, but does not provide data to calibrate the size or direction of the effect on efficiency.

- source: D'Exelle, B., & van den Berg, M. (2014). AID DISTRIBUTION AND COOPERATION IN UNEQUAL COMMUNITIES. *Review of Income and Wealth*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, show_n_rounds
  findings: The introduction of a representative who allocates aid (selected as the highest contributor) increases group contributions compared to equal sharing, due to both competition for the representative role and anticipation of punishment (i.e., receiving less aid for low contributions). However, representatives keep a large share of the aid for themselves, so the increased total aid does not translate into higher payoffs for the rest of the group. Representatives do consider fairness, giving more to low-endowment and higher-contributing members, but still capture more than their proportional share. Endowment inequality increases contributions under equal sharing but not under representative sharing. The study does not report group efficiency or total group payoff as a ratio to the social optimum, so the effect of the representative mechanism on efficiency is not directly assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (here, representative allocation with the ability to reward or punish via aid shares) on group contributions and the distribution of payoffs. The representative mechanism increases contributions (a proxy for cooperation), but the efficiency gain for the group as a whole is ambiguous because the representative captures a disproportionate share of the aid. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency (total group payoff relative to the maximum), this study suggests that mechanisms allowing targeted allocation can increase contributions, but may not improve average group payoffs for all members unless the allocation is constrained. The absence of direct efficiency analysis limits the ability to make quantitative predictions about efficiency outcomes.

- source: Sadowski, J., Spierre, S. G., Selinger, E., Seager, T. P., Adams, E. A., & Berardy, A. (2015). Intergroup Cooperation in Common Pool Resource Dilemmas. *Science and Engineering Ethics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The studies found that, in a complex, multi-group common-pool resource game with real stakes and open communication, groups were able to achieve cooperative outcomes and avoid the tragedy of the commons, even in the absence of explicit punishment mechanisms. Cooperation was facilitated by the emergence of ethical leaders and effective intergroup communication. In both studies, groups coordinated to ensure equitable resource distribution, resulting in moderate but sustainable payoffs for all. The game design allowed for a variety of strategies, but did not include punishment or reward options. The findings suggest that leadership and communication can substitute for formal sanctions in promoting cooperation and efficiency, but do not provide evidence on the effect of punishment per se.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods or common-pool resource games, as it does not include or manipulate punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. It does, however, provide evidence that, in the absence of punishment, cooperation and moderate efficiency can be achieved through communication and leadership. For prediction tasks, this paper may help estimate baseline efficiency in control conditions without punishment, especially in games with communication and group identity. It does not provide evidence on how efficiency would change if punishment were enabled.

- source: Messer, K. D., Suter, J. F., & Yan, J. B. (2013). Context Effects in a Negatively Framed Social Dilemma Experiment. *Environmental & Resource Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The experiment finds that pre-play communication and voting significantly increase contributions to the public account in a negatively-framed VCM. The effect of the status quo (initial endowment in private vs. public account) is reversed compared to positively-framed games: with communication, a private account status quo leads to higher and more persistent contributions than a public account status quo. Voting alone does not significantly affect contributions beyond communication. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and all quantitative results are reported as contribution rates, not direct efficiency or payoff measures. The findings highlight the importance of context and framing in social dilemmas, but do not provide evidence on the effects of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions in public goods games or their variants. It is not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, as no punishment mechanism is present in any treatment. However, it does provide evidence on how communication, voting, and status quo framing affect contribution rates (and thus implied efficiency) in a negatively-framed VCM. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not relevant. For tasks focused on communication, voting, or framing, it is informative.

- source: Zhang, Q. H., Chen, Y. Y., Tao, Y., Farid, T., & Ma, J. H. (2019). How Consistent Contributors Inspire Individuals to Cooperate: The Role of Moral Elevation and Social Value Orientation. *Sustainability*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: This study demonstrates that introducing a consistent contributor (CC) into a step-level public-goods-dilemma game increases both cooperation and group/individual earnings. The effect is robust across both pro-social and pro-self participants, though the underlying psychological mechanisms differ: pro-selfs are influenced via increased moral elevation, while pro-socials respond directly to the CC's behavior. The design does not include punishment or sanction mechanisms, but provides strong evidence that motivational (role model) interventions can increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in the absence of punishment or sanction mechanisms, introducing a consistent contributor (role model) can increase group efficiency (payoff) in step-level public-goods games. The findings are most relevant for predicting efficiency in games with similar motivational interventions, but do not inform the effect of punishment. The design and results can serve as a control or baseline for understanding efficiency in the absence of punishment, and highlight the potential for non-punitive mechanisms to improve outcomes.

- source: Espinosa, M. P., Fatás, E., & Ubeda, P. (2019). Linguistic diversity and out-group discrimination in bilingual societies. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that the effect of ethno-linguistic diversity on efficiency in public goods provision is context-dependent. In Bilbao, diversity (mixed-language groups) leads to significantly lower contributions to the global public good and lower earnings, indicating reduced efficiency. In Valencia, diversity has a positive or neutral effect on global public good contributions and earnings, especially among Catalan speakers. The results are robust to controls for individualism/collectivism and are supported by belief elicitation in a follow-up experiment, which shows that negative effects in Bilbao are associated with normative tolerance for out-group discrimination, while positive effects in Valencia are linked to optimistic expectations about out-group cooperation. No punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in the absence of punishment, the effect of diversity on efficiency in public goods games depends on the level of underlying group conflict. The nested public goods game is structurally similar to a standard PGG but with a local/global allocation choice. The findings suggest that, when predicting efficiency in control (no-punishment) games, group composition and social context (e.g., group conflict) are important moderators. However, because there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the design, this paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. It is useful for understanding baseline efficiency and the role of diversity, but not for predicting the effect of punishment.

- source: Zhang, Y., & He, L. F. (2021). Theory and experiments on network games of public goods: inequality aversion and welfare preference. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiments show that in network public goods games with a dominant strategy, subjects systematically contribute more than the self-interest level, resulting in higher group payoffs and welfare. This over-contribution is best explained by a welfare preference model, where individuals care about the payoffs of their neighbors. Inequality aversion explains some behavioral patterns but is less predictive overall. The effect of social preferences is moderated by network structure (star, circle, complete) and by the level of economic return: welfare preference dominates in star networks and at low returns, while self-interest becomes more important in circle networks and at high returns. No punishment or sanctioning mechanisms are present or studied.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning is present in the design. However, it provides strong evidence that, in the absence of punishment, efficiency can be higher than predicted by self-interest due to social preferences, especially welfare preference. The findings suggest that network structure and the level of economic return moderate the extent of over-contribution and efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper is informative for estimating control (no-punishment) efficiency in networked public goods games, but not for the incremental effect of punishment.

- source: Nockur, L., Arndt, L., Keller, J., & Pfattheicher, S. (2020). Collective choice fosters sustainable resource management in the presence of asymmetric opportunities. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: In a lab common resource game with groups of four, participants played under three systems: individual choice, median choice, and majority voting. In both symmetric and asymmetric opportunity conditions, collective choice systems (median and majority voting) led to lower average extraction rates and higher profits than individual choice. The effect was especially pronounced for disadvantaged group members in asymmetric groups, whose profits increased substantially under collective choice. Advantaged group members' extraction was curtailed under collective choice, but their profits did not increase. Satisfaction and fairness ratings were also higher under collective choice. No punishment or sanctioning mechanisms were present in any condition.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in common resource games with or without asymmetric extraction opportunities, implementing collective choice mechanisms (median or majority voting) increases efficiency and group payoff compared to individual choice. However, the study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are most relevant for predicting the effect of collective decision rules (not punishment) on efficiency, and less relevant for the downstream task of predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The game structure is close to a public goods dilemma, but the absence of punishment means the results are not directly transferable to punishment-enabled settings.

- source: Caria, A. S., & Fafchamps, M. (2019). Expectations, network centrality, and public good contributions: Experimental evidence from India. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment finds that central players do not contribute more than others, despite being much more efficient at generating public good. This results in only about half of the potential efficiency being realized. Disclosing group expectations (elicited from participants) increases the likelihood that central players match those expectations, but does not increase average contributions or efficiency overall, except in groups with weak social ties, where a significant positive effect is observed. The dominant behavioral pattern is conditional cooperation, even when efficiency and equality would require higher contributions from the centre. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in the absence of punishment or reward mechanisms, even highly efficient central players do not contribute more than others, resulting in substantial efficiency losses. The findings suggest that, without punishment, efficiency is limited by conditional cooperation norms, not by the potential for high-impact contributions. The effect of expectation disclosure is context-dependent and does not generally increase efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper provides a clear control case for games with no punishment: efficiency is about 50% of the potential maximum, even with strong efficiency incentives for some players. The absence of punishment is critical—adding punishment would likely change these outcomes, but this paper does not provide direct evidence on that.

- source: McEvoy, D. M., Cherry, T. L., & Stranlund, J. K. (2015). Endogenous Minimum Participation in International Environmental Agreements: An Experimental Analysis. *Environmental & Resource Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that when the efficient coalition size is the full group (6 of 6), groups adopt the efficient minimum membership requirement about 77% of the time, and efficient coalitions form in over 90% of those cases, resulting in high efficiency (87.4%). When the efficient coalition is a subset (3 of 6), the efficient threshold is also adopted about 77% of the time, but efficient coalitions form only about half the time, and efficiency is lower (79.6%). Blocking of efficient coalitions is observed, especially in the partial coalition treatment, and is attributed to inequality aversion. The endogenous minimum participation mechanism is effective at facilitating cooperation and efficiency when full participation is required, but less so when partial participation allows for free riding.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effects of punishment or sanctions in public goods games, so it does not directly inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. However, it provides high-quality evidence on the effects of endogenous minimum participation requirements as a coordination device. The results suggest that such mechanisms can achieve high efficiency, especially when the efficient outcome requires full participation. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may be useful for understanding alternative institutional mechanisms for promoting cooperation and efficiency.

- source: Hergueux, J., Henry, E., Benkler, Y., & Algan, Y. (2023). Social Exchange and the Reciprocity Roller Coaster: Evidence from the Life and Death of Virtual Teams. *Organization Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study links laboratory measures of reciprocity (from a one-shot public goods game) to field measures of reciprocity and project-level outcomes in open-source software teams. It finds that, while teams with more highly reciprocal members can achieve higher success scores (activity percentile) if they survive, they are also more likely to fail. The mechanism is that reciprocity amplifies both positive and negative contribution dynamics: highly reciprocal teams excel when cooperation is high, but are more fragile and prone to collapse after negative shocks or periods of inactivity. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and thus does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency. The outcomes are group performance and survival, not direct PGG efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. Its findings are relevant for understanding the dynamics of reciprocity and cooperation in repeated public goods-like environments without punishment. The absence of punishment is a key design feature. The results suggest that, in the absence of punishment, high reciprocity can lead to both high performance and high fragility, but do not inform how enabling punishment would affect efficiency. Therefore, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be useful as a control case for environments without punishment.

- source: Civai, C., Caserotti, M., Carrus, E., Huijsmans, I., & Rubaltelli, E. (2022). How perceived scarcity predicted cooperation during early pandemic lockdown. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that, in hypothetical public goods games contextualized to the COVID-19 pandemic, perceived scarcity of socio-psychological wellbeing (e.g., missing social contact) predicts greater willingness to contribute to the public good (both money and time), while perceived scarcity of freedom (e.g., movement restrictions) predicts less willingness to contribute time. Material scarcity does not significantly predict cooperation. No punishment, sanction, or reward mechanisms are present in the games. The study does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or any actual payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanction mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. Its main relevance is in showing how individual differences in perceived scarcity relate to willingness to cooperate in hypothetical, one-shot, non-incentivized PGG-like scenarios. It does not inform the effect of punishment or any game design dimension related to sanctions, rewards, or efficiency. Thus, it should not be weighted heavily for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Antinyan, A., Corazzini, L., & Neururer, D. (2015). Public good provision, punishment, and the endowment origin: Experimental evidence. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The presence of two less privileged (LP) members—either with lower productivity (lower MPCR) or with no feedback information—reduces average group contributions compared to the baseline with no LPs. The type of LP affects the pattern of contribution decay and spillovers: less productive LPs contribute least and trigger lower contributions in the group, while LPs without feedback information are exploited by their neighbors but themselves contribute more. The positioning of LPs (close vs. distant) also affects contribution dynamics, especially in the lower productivity treatment. However, the study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare directly, and there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. Its findings on heterogeneity and feedback information may be contextually informative for understanding moderators of cooperation, but it does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The main outcomes are contribution rates, not efficiency or group payoff.

- source: Weng, Q. F., He, N. R., Hu, L. W., & Chen, X. J. (2021). Heterogeneous investment with dynamical feedback promotes public cooperation and group success in spatial public goods games. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that communication mechanisms—especially chain (one-on-one) communication and the combination of information display with chain communication—substantially increase the frequency and stability of successful group provision of the public good (EPGs via GECs) compared to a baseline with no intervention. Star network (information display only) and global communication also improve outcomes relative to baseline, but less so than chain communication. The most effective intervention is the combination of information display and chain communication. However, the study does not include any punishment or sanctioning treatments, and does not report group efficiency or total payoffs. The findings are thus about behavioral contributions and threshold success, not efficiency per se.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or variants. It is useful for understanding the effects of communication and information display on contribution behavior and threshold success in a TPGG, but cannot inform predictions about punishment-enabled efficiency. None of the experimental treatments include punishment, sanctions, or peer enforcement. Therefore, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be informative for the effects of communication and information structures in threshold public goods settings.

- source: Cardador, M. T., Northcraft, G. B., Rockmann, K. W., & Grant, B. C. (2016). Characteristics of affected third parties and cooperative behavior in social dilemmas. *Journal of Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The presence of a needy third party increases individual cooperative behavior (contributions) in a public-goods-like game. If the third party is seen as unreliable, this effect is diminished, and cooperation returns to baseline (no third party) levels. The effect is mediated by prosocial motivation and, in some cases, intra-group trust. The amount of money raised for the third party (group total contributions) is higher when the third party is needy and reliable. No punishment or reward mechanisms are studied, and efficiency or group payoff relative to the optimum is not directly analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctions on efficiency or payoff in public-goods games. It is not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. However, it does provide evidence that non-structural, prosocial motivational interventions (making third-party beneficiaries salient) can increase cooperation in the absence of punishment. The game design is a standard linear public goods game with four players, one-shot, no punishment, no reward, and no communication. The findings may be useful for understanding baseline cooperation rates in control conditions without punishment, but not for predicting the effect of punishment.

- source: Frey, U. J. (2017). Cooperative strategies outside the laboratory - evidence from a long-term large-N-study in five countries. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in a context-rich, long-term, repeated threshold public goods environment (an online game), the distribution of cooperative strategies differs from laboratory public goods games: free riding is as common as in the lab, but high cooperation is less frequent and conditional cooperation is somewhat less common. These strategies are stable over time and across different group contexts. The results suggest that context matters for the prevalence of cooperative strategies, but do not provide evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor on the effects of punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. However, it is informative about the baseline prevalence and stability of cooperative strategies in a long-term, context-rich threshold public goods environment without punishment. For prediction tasks, it suggests that in the absence of punishment, free riding remains common, high cooperation is rare, and conditional cooperation is less prevalent than in some lab settings. The findings may help calibrate expectations for control (no-punishment) efficiency in similar environments, but do not inform the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Kelly, D., Claessens, S., Sibley, C. G., Chaudhuri, A., & Atkinson, Q. (2022). Cooperative phenotype predicts climate change belief and pro-environmental behaviour. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that individuals who behave more cooperatively in a suite of economic games (including a public goods game) are more likely to believe in climate change and to report pro-environmental behaviors. The relationship between cooperative phenotype and climate change belief/behavior is robust to many demographic and personality controls, but is attenuated by political party support. Mediation analyses suggest that climate change belief may mediate the effect of cooperative phenotype on pro-environmental behavior. However, the study does not analyze or report on efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment or sanctions in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. While it uses a public goods game as part of a battery to estimate cooperative phenotype, it does not manipulate or report on punishment, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.

- source: Kimbrough, E. O., & Rubin, J. (2015). Sustaining Group Reputation. *Journal of Law Economics & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated trust game with random rematching and group reputation, the introduction of an in-group punishment mechanism (jury) leads to a significant increase in the amount sent by senders, which directly translates to higher total welfare/efficiency. Information sharing about group members' experiences also increases welfare, but mainly by reinforcing positive or negative group reputations (path dependence). The effects of punishment and information sharing are additive rather than superadditive or substitutive. The presence of punishment increases both the amount sent and the amount returned, with the largest gains in the treatment where both punishment and information sharing are present. The effect of punishment is primarily due to deterrence—the threat of being punished is enough to change behavior, not just actual punishment events.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in trust-game-like environments with group reputation and random rematching, enabling a punishment mechanism (with moderate cost and effectiveness) increases efficiency (total welfare) relative to a control with punishment disabled. The effect is robust and additive with information sharing. The findings suggest that, for similar game designs, the predicted efficiency with punishment enabled should be higher than with punishment disabled, especially when punishment is feasible and not prohibitively costly. The results are most directly applicable to trust games but are relevant for adjacent public-goods-like settings where group reputation and in-group punishment are possible. The paper also provides evidence that the marginal per-capita return (here, the tripling of the amount sent) is important for welfare, and that communication (cheap talk) alone does not have a significant effect. The design dimensions most critical for prediction are the presence and cost of punishment, information sharing, and the structure of the trust game.

- source: Grimm, V., & Mengel, F. (2011). Matching technology and the choice of punishment institutions in a prisoner's dilemma game. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that a local, exogenous punishment institution can sustain high cooperation and high average payoffs (efficiency) when the matching structure (population viscosity) is high, i.e., when group members mostly interact with each other. As viscosity decreases (more random mixing), both cooperation and average payoffs decline. In the most separated case (x=0), average profits are 373 (out of a possible 400), while in the most mixed case (x=1), average profits drop to 230 (the mutual defection payoff is 200). A control with internalized punishment (punishment always applies) at low viscosity (x=2/3) yields higher average profits (243) than local punishment (230). Thus, punishment institutions can increase efficiency, but their effectiveness depends strongly on the matching structure and the scope of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the efficiency gain from enabling punishment depends on the matching structure (population viscosity) and the scope of punishment (local vs internalized). When predicting treatment efficiency from game design, the presence of a punishment institution will increase efficiency only if the environment allows for sufficient group separation (high viscosity) or if punishment is internalized (applies in all matches). In low viscosity or random matching, local punishment is much less effective. The control efficiency (without punishment) is the mutual defection payoff (200), and the treatment efficiency with punishment can approach the cooperative payoff (400) under high viscosity, but remains low under random matching. The paper also shows that the technical details of punishment (local vs internalized) matter for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Pansini, R., Campenni, M., & Shi, L. (2020). Segregating socioeconomic classes leads to an unequal redistribution of wealth. *Palgrave Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: In a repeated dyadic Prisoner's Dilemma with a costly punishment option, the ability to punish was restricted to one class (rich or poor) in segregated treatments, and randomly assigned in the integrated treatment. In segregated conditions, the class with punishment power earned nearly double the other, increasing inequality, but total group earnings were similar across all treatments. In the integrated condition, earnings were more equal and the marginal increase in total earnings was small. Thus, punishment's effect on efficiency (total earnings) is neutral, but it strongly affects the distribution of payoffs depending on who can punish.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency (total group earnings) in a repeated dyadic social dilemma is minimal: enabling punishment does not substantially increase or decrease total earnings compared to control. However, the distribution of earnings is highly sensitive to who can punish: if only one class can punish, that class captures a much larger share of the total payoff. For prediction, this suggests that in similar dyadic games, enabling punishment may not improve efficiency, but can increase inequality if punishment power is asymmetric. The design dimensions most relevant are the symmetry of punishment access and the matching structure (segregated vs integrated).

- source: Kopányi-Peuker, A., Offerman, T., & Sloof, R. (2017). Fostering cooperation through the enhancement of own vulnerability. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Allowing players to endogenously make themselves vulnerable to punishment (i.e., to enable the partner to punish them at a cost) increases cooperation and efficiency in a repeated prisoner's dilemma compared to a control with no punishment. The Gradual mechanism, where vulnerability can be increased stepwise and conditionally, leads to higher mutual cooperation and efficiency than the Leap mechanism, where vulnerability is chosen in one step. Efficiency gains are only observed when the vulnerability choice is endogenous and can be used as a signal; exogenous assignment of punishment possibilities does not improve and may even reduce efficiency. The effect is driven by the signaling value of voluntary vulnerability, not by the mere presence of punishment. The results suggest that the design of punishment mechanisms—especially the ability to signal intent and coordinate—critically moderates their effect on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling endogenous, voluntary punishment (where players can signal intent by making themselves vulnerable) increases efficiency in repeated prisoner's dilemma games compared to no-punishment controls. The effect is stronger when the mechanism allows gradual, conditional increases in vulnerability (i.e., more opportunity for signaling and coordination). The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of endogenous, voluntary punishment mechanisms—especially those allowing conditional, stepwise commitment—should be expected to yield higher efficiency than both no-punishment and exogenous punishment settings. The results are most directly applicable to two-player, repeated prisoner's dilemma games, but the mechanism is adjacent to public goods games. The effect is contingent on the ability to signal intent; mere punishment possibility without signaling does not improve efficiency.

- source: Wright, J. (2013). Punishment strategies in repeated games: Evidence from experimental markets. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that the vast majority of subjects do not use harsh, disproportionate punishment strategies. Instead, most use graduated or lenient strategies, such as Tit-for-tat, which respond proportionally to deviations. Regression analysis shows that adopting Tit-for-tat leads to significantly higher present discounted value of earnings than harsher punishment strategies, even after controlling for past earnings. Communication between subjects increases the likelihood of cooperation (high prices) but does not significantly increase the use of harsh punishment. The findings suggest that in repeated games with rich action spaces, softer punishment strategies are more effective for sustaining cooperation and maximizing group payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that in repeated games with a rich action space (such as price competition), enabling punishment does not require harsh, maximal punishment to achieve high efficiency. Instead, proportionate, graduated punishment (e.g., Tit-for-tat) is more effective for maximizing group payoffs. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of a punishment mechanism will likely increase efficiency, but the effect depends on the nature of punishment strategies adopted. Communication further supports cooperation but does not necessarily increase the use of harsh punishment. The findings are most directly relevant to repeated duopoly/oligopoly settings, but the payoff-based results are informative for adjacent public-goods-like environments.

- source: Abbink, K., Sadrieh, A., & Zamir, S. (2004). Fairness, public good, and emotional aspects of punishment behavior. *Theory and Decision*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that making punishment (rejection) visible to proposers increases the frequency of punishment and changes proposer behavior (more equal offers over time), but this does not translate into higher efficiency. Instead, efficiency is lower in the open (punishment-enabled) treatment compared to the covered (punishment-disabled) treatment, because the cost of punishment (lost payoffs from rejections) outweighs any gains from increased fairness. Only when rejection rates are either very high (deterring almost all unfair offers) or very low (almost no punishment) does efficiency approach the maximum. The study provides direct evidence that punishment can reduce efficiency in bargaining environments, especially when it is used at intermediate rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment (making it visible and actionable) in a repeated ultimatum minigame reduces efficiency compared to a control where punishment is hidden/disabled. The effect is driven by increased costly punishment (rejections), which reduces total group payoffs. The design is not a standard public goods game, but the punishment mechanism and efficiency analysis are directly relevant. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, in similar two-player bargaining games, enabling punishment is likely to reduce efficiency unless punishment is either very rare or so frequent that it almost eliminates unfair offers. The findings caution against assuming that punishment will improve efficiency in environments where its cost is high and its deterrence effect is partial.

- source: Szekely, A., Andrighetto, G., Payette, N., & Tummolini, L. (2020). Aggression, Conflict, and the Formation of Intimidating Group Reputation. *Social Psychology Quarterly*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that when group members can use peer punishment to enforce an intimidating group reputation, they increase their punishment of outsiders (A), but this leads to higher conflict and lower earnings for both group members (B) and outsiders (A) in the short run. Without peer punishment, group reputation is exploited and aggression is low, with higher earnings. Individual reputation (with individual history) leads to more aggression than group reputation without peer punishment, but still results in higher earnings than the group reputation with peer punishment condition. Agent-based simulations suggest that in the long run, peer punishment can eventually increase group earnings as reputation deters conflict, but this is not realized in the 11-round experiment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling peer punishment to enforce group reputation in a conflict/aggression game increases punishment and conflict, but reduces efficiency and group earnings in the short run. For prediction tasks, this suggests that adding a peer punishment mechanism (with moderate cost and effectiveness) to a group setting where reputation is at stake will likely decrease efficiency compared to a control without punishment, at least over a moderate number of rounds. The effect may reverse in much longer games, but this is only supported by simulation, not experimental data. The design is not a standard public goods game, but is a social dilemma with conflict and punishment, so results are most transferable to adjacent settings.

- source: Bolle, F., Breitmoser, Y., & Schlächter, S. (2011). Extortion in the laboratory. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that when one player can punish another at low cost in a repeated extortion game, punishment is used frequently and harshly, especially as experience accumulates. The threat of punishment induces compliance with moderate demands, but actual punishment does not improve and may reduce future compliance. Over time, P's demands and income increase, while A's income decreases. The combined earnings of both players are about 80% of the maximum possible, indicating a notable efficiency loss compared to full cooperation. The results suggest that low-cost punishment leads to persistent, severe punishment and lower group efficiency, and that subject heterogeneity (different behavioral types) is important for understanding aggregate outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that in repeated asymmetric games with low-cost, high-impact punishment, the introduction of punishment reduces group efficiency compared to the maximum possible. The threat of punishment increases compliance with moderate demands, but actual punishment does not improve efficiency and may worsen it. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that when punishment is cheap and effective, efficiency is likely to be lower than in a fully cooperative scenario, and the presence of punishment can lead to persistent efficiency losses. The results are most directly relevant to asymmetric, dyadic games rather than standard symmetric public goods games, but the mechanisms (punishment cost, effectiveness, behavioral response) are informative for predicting efficiency in related environments.

- source: Kamei, K. (2018). The role of visibility on third party punishment actions for the enforcement of social norms. *Economics Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that when third party punishment is visible to another punisher, punishment of defectors becomes much stronger and punishment of cooperators is nearly eliminated. This shifts the incentive structure from a prisoner's dilemma to a coordination game, where mutual cooperation is a Nash equilibrium and cooperating yields a higher expected payoff than defecting. In the standard (low visibility) treatment, punishment is weaker and less well-targeted, and the incentive structure remains a prisoner's dilemma, with defection being more profitable. The findings suggest that increasing the visibility of punishment actions can enhance the effectiveness of third party punishment in promoting cooperation and improving group payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that increasing the visibility of punishment actions (i.e., making punishment observable to other punishers) can substantially increase the effectiveness of third party punishment in promoting cooperation and improving expected payoffs. While the game is a prisoner's dilemma rather than a standard public goods game, the mechanism is closely related. The results suggest that, all else equal, enabling punishment visibility should be predicted to increase efficiency (group payoff) in similar environments. The paper also provides quantitative data on expected payoffs under different punishment regimes, which can inform predictions about the magnitude of efficiency changes when punishment is enabled and made visible.

- source: Tan, F. F., & Xiao, E. T. (2012). Peer punishment with third-party approval in a social dilemma game. *Economics Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment compares two treatments in a repeated prisoner's dilemma: (1) baseline, where players can directly punish each other, and (2) TPA (third-party approval), where a third party must approve any punishment before it is implemented. The TPA treatment results in significantly lower cooperation rates (0.47 vs. 0.70) and marginally significantly lower average earnings (23.52 vs. 25.30 tokens per period) compared to the baseline. Third-party approval reduces antisocial punishment but also reduces punishment of defectors, which appears to undermine the positive effect of punishment on cooperation. The study suggests that, in settings where antisocial punishment is rare, requiring third-party approval for punishment can reduce both cooperation and group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a repeated prisoner's dilemma with peer punishment, introducing a third-party approval step for punishment reduces both cooperation and average earnings compared to direct peer punishment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that adding procedural hurdles or external approval to punishment mechanisms may decrease the efficiency gains typically associated with peer punishment, especially when antisocial punishment is infrequent. The findings are most directly relevant to adjacent settings (prisoner's dilemma rather than public goods), but the mechanism—peer punishment with and without third-party approval—maps closely to PGG-like environments. The control (baseline) efficiency is higher than in the treatment with third-party approval, indicating a less positive effect of punishment on efficiency when third-party approval is required.

- source: Fiedler, M., & Haruvy, E. (2017). The effect of third party intervention in the trust game. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a trust game, third party monitoring increases investment and return rates compared to no third party. Adding punishment or reward capabilities to the third party does not significantly increase aggregate cooperation or efficiency beyond monitoring alone, especially in random partner settings. In fixed partner settings, punishment may have a somewhat greater effect, but the difference is not large. Individual players are responsive to punishment and reward, but the overall aggregate effect is muted because the actual use of punishment/reward is infrequent and the absence of punishment/reward can itself act as reinforcement. The paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome, but the design allows for inference about total payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that in trust game environments (which are adjacent to public goods games), third party punishment does not substantially increase efficiency or aggregate payoff beyond what is achieved by third party monitoring alone, especially when partners are randomly matched. The effect of punishment is more pronounced in fixed partner settings, but still not dramatically higher than monitoring. For prediction tasks, this suggests that in similar trust/investment games, enabling punishment may not yield large efficiency gains over monitoring, particularly with random matching. However, the results may not transfer directly to public goods games, and efficiency is not directly measured.

- source: Pérez, R. L., & Kisst, H. J. (2012). Do People Accurately Anticipate Sanctions?. *Southern Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that subjects are generally able to anticipate the sign and strength of sanctions (punishment/reward) in two-player allocation games, with moderate errors. Punishment is better anticipated than reward, and nonmonetary sanctions are anticipated with smaller errors than monetary ones. Systematic underestimation occurs mainly where rewards are prevalent. The study does not analyze or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and the games are not public goods games or close variants.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The games studied are two-player allocation games with a sanctioning stage, not multi-player public goods games. The main outcomes are about belief accuracy, not efficiency or payoff. While the findings suggest that punishment is more salient and better anticipated than reward, and that errors in expectation are moderate, these insights are only indirectly relevant to predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The paper may inform mechanism design regarding the salience and predictability of sanctions, but does not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence on efficiency outcomes.

- source: SCHMITT, D. R., & MARWELL, G. (1970). REWARD AND PUNISHMENT AS INFLUENCE TECHNIQUES FOR ACHIEVEMENT OF COOPERATION UNDER INEQUITY. *Human Relations*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a dyadic task with unequal cooperative payoffs, the ability to reward (transfer money) is much more effective than the ability to punish (subtract money) in inducing and sustaining high rates of cooperation. Groups where the reward strategy was used spent more than twice as much time cooperating compared to those using punishment or no strategy. Communication between subjects further increased the use of rewards and the likelihood of achieving steady-state cooperation, particularly among males. Punishment, when used alone, was generally ineffective in producing cooperation, and its use declined over time. The study does not report direct measures of group efficiency or total earnings, but the behavioral outcomes suggest that reward-based influence is more conducive to sustained cooperation than punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of punishment and reward on cooperation in a setting adjacent to public goods games. The findings suggest that punishment alone is generally ineffective at inducing sustained cooperation, while reward (and especially the combination of reward and communication) is much more effective. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is dyadic, with asymmetric payoffs and the ability for only one player to reward or punish, which differs from standard PGGs. Thus, the main value for prediction is as a caution that punishment may not always increase cooperation or efficiency, especially when reward is also available and more salient.

- source: Batistoni, T., Barclay, P., & Raihani, N. J. (2022). Third-party punishers do not compete to be chosen as partners in an experimental game. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishment and helping are both used as signals of cooperative intent, but helping is a stronger and less ambiguous signal than punishment. Observability increases investments in both, but the possibility of partner choice does not further escalate investments. Higher investments in punishment or helping increase the likelihood of being chosen as a partner and being trusted, but this effect is much stronger for helping. Investments in both are positively associated with actual trustworthiness in a Trust Game. The study does not address group efficiency or total payoff in a public goods game, but provides evidence on the reputational and signaling functions of punishment and helping.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on group efficiency in public goods games. The experimental design is adjacent to PGGs, using a Dictator Game with third-party punishment/helping and a subsequent Trust Game. The main outcomes are individual-level signaling, partner choice, and trust/trustworthiness, not group efficiency or total payoff. The findings suggest that punishment is a weaker and more ambiguous signal of cooperative intent than helping, and that reputational incentives may increase costly punishment/helping, but do not escalate under partner choice. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in PGGs, this paper provides only indirect, contextual evidence about the social and reputational motivations for punishment, not about its effect on group payoff or efficiency.

- source: Konishi, N., & Ohtsubo, Y. (2015). Does dishonesty really invite third-party punishment? Results of a more stringent test. *Biology Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that, even after removing five key methodological confounds (including adding a reward option and ensuring anonymity), a significant minority of third-party observers are willing to incur a personal cost to punish a dishonest trustee, even when the trustee's allocation is fair. The rate of punishment is lower than in previous studies (27% vs. 53%), but still significantly higher in the dishonesty condition than in the honesty condition. Participants were more likely to punish than reward in response to dishonesty. The main outcomes are the frequency and cost of punishment/reward, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that third-party punishment of norm violations (specifically dishonesty) can occur even when methodological confounds are controlled. However, the design is a modified trust game with third-party punishment and reward, not a public goods game or direct group efficiency context. The outcomes are behavioral (punishment/reward rates and costs), not efficiency or group payoff. Thus, while the paper supports the robustness of third-party punishment as a behavioral phenomenon, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public-goods-game-like environments. Its main value is in showing that punishment can persist even when alternative explanations are minimized, but it does not inform the quantitative effect of punishment on group efficiency.

- source: Leibbrandt, A., & López-Pérez, R. (2014). Different carrots and different sticks: do we reward and punish differently than we approve and disapprove?. *Theory and Decision*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that monetary punishment is used more often and more intensely than non-monetary disapproval, but monetary reward and non-monetary approval are used at similar rates. The determinants of monetary punishment/reward are primarily payoff-based (inequity aversion), while non-monetary approval/disapproval is more strongly driven by reciprocity and norm-based considerations. Strict payoff equality reduces disapproval and increases approval. The experiment is not a repeated public goods game but a set of one-shot, two-player allocation games, so efficiency or group payoff outcomes are not reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the psychological and motivational underpinnings of punishment and reward, distinguishing between monetary and non-monetary forms. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes in a repeated public goods context, nor does it manipulate or report most of the game design dimensions relevant for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. Its main value is in clarifying that the impact of punishment/reward on behavior may depend on whether the mechanism is monetary or non-monetary, and that motivations differ. For direct prediction of efficiency effects in PGGs, this paper is only indirectly informative.

- source: Mieth, L., Buchner, A., & Bell, R. (2021). Cognitive load decreases cooperation and moral punishment in a Prisoner's Dilemma game with punishment option. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that cognitive load reduces both cooperation and moral punishment in a one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma game with punishment. Hypocritical and antisocial punishment are not significantly affected by cognitive load, but a general punishment bias (punishing regardless of partner behavior) increases under cognitive load. The amount spent on punishment, when punishment occurs, does not differ between conditions. The results suggest that both cooperation and moral punishment require cognitive resources and are not purely intuitive. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a two-player one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma with a punishment option, the presence of punishment (and its purposeful use) depends on cognitive resources. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings are most relevant for understanding how cognitive load moderates the behavioral use of punishment and cooperation, not their impact on group efficiency. The game is a Prisoner's Dilemma, not a public goods game, so transfer to PGG settings should be cautious.

- source: El-Bialy, N., Aranda, E. F., Nicklisch, A., Saleh, L., & Voigt, S. (2022). To cooperate or not to cooperate? An analysis of cooperation and peer punishment among Syrian refugees, Germans, and Jordanians. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that Syrian refugees in both Germany and Jordan are more likely to cooperate with other refugees than with host country nationals, indicating in-group favoritism in cooperation. However, punishment behavior (the likelihood of punishing a defector) does not differ significantly based on whether the target is an in-group or out-group member. Host country participants (Germans and Jordanians) do not show significant differences in cooperation or punishment based on the partner's group. The study does not analyze efficiency or group payoff, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence on the behavioral effects of punishment opportunities in a one-shot prisoner's dilemma with a punishment stage, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Therefore, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. However, it does show that the presence of punishment does not lead to group-differentiated sanctioning, and that cooperation rates are high overall, especially within in-groups. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative, as it does not provide the necessary payoff-based outcome data.

- source: Nese, A., O'Higgins, N., Sbriglia, P., & Scudiero, M. (2018). Cooperation, punishment and organized crime: a lab-in-the-field experiment in southern Italy. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that Camorra inmates are highly cooperative and strongly punish defectors, consistent with their internal codes of conduct. However, when external punishment (third-party punishment) is introduced, their cooperation decreases, indicating a rejection of external authority. Ordinary criminals, in contrast, increase cooperation when external punishment is possible, suggesting opportunism. Students' cooperation also decreases with TPP. The main outcomes are behavioral (cooperation and punishment), not efficiency or group payoff. The findings highlight the importance of group-specific social norms and attitudes in shaping responses to punishment opportunities.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence on how the introduction of punishment affects cooperation rates in a Prisoner's Dilemma setting, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it uses a two-player PD and a three-player PD with third-party punishment, rather than a standard multi-player PGG. The results suggest that the effect of punishment on cooperation (and by extension, possibly on efficiency) is highly dependent on group norms and attitudes: punishment can reduce cooperation in some groups (students, Camorra) and increase it in others (ordinary criminals). However, because efficiency is not measured, the paper's direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The evidence is most useful for understanding behavioral responses to punishment under different social norms, rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes.

- source: Davis, D. D., & Holt, C. A. (1999). Equilibrium cooperation in two-stage games: Experimental evidence. *International Journal of Game Theory*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that the use of contingent punishment/reward strategies in the second stage of a two-stage game is strongly associated with higher rates of cooperation in the first stage. This effect is robust across different treatments, including those where the punishment is not a Nash equilibrium or is Pareto dominated. The structure of the second-stage equilibria does not reliably predict cooperation rates. Instead, initial cohort propensities and first-stage payoff structure are more important. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the availability and use of punishment/reward strategies can increase cooperation rates in two-stage games, even when the punishment is not a Nash equilibrium. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The findings suggest that behavioral responses to punishment opportunities are important, but the translation to efficiency outcomes is not addressed. The results are most useful for understanding the behavioral mechanisms by which punishment can support cooperation, rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes.

- source: Dorrough, A. R., Olsson, M. I. T., Froehlich, L., Glöckner, A., & Martiny, S. E. (2021). Does she compensate the victim while he punishes the perpetrator? No gender differences in anonymous economic games across 11 nations. *Journal of Behavioral Decision Making*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Across two large studies (one multinational, one German student sample), there is no evidence for systematic gender differences in third-party punishment or compensation behavior in response to unfairness. Both men and women are more likely to compensate the victim than to punish the perpetrator. Increasing the effectiveness of punishment (punishment impact per unit cost) does not substantially change this pattern. The study does not analyze or report group efficiency, welfare, or total payoff, and the game is not a repeated public goods game but a one-shot third-party punishment/compensation scenario.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the behavioral use of punishment and compensation options in a third-party context, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it involves norm enforcement and costly punishment, but is not a repeated or group-based contribution game. The main decision-relevant finding is that, even when punishment is made more effective, participants prefer compensation over punishment, suggesting that punishment may be underutilized in similar designs. However, since efficiency or group payoff is not measured, the paper does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The evidence is most relevant for understanding behavioral preferences for punishment versus compensation, not for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Li, S. Y., Ma, S. M., Wang, D. Y., Zhang, H. J., Li, Y. Z., Wang, J. X., Li, J. Y., Zhang, B. Y., Gross, J., De Dreu, C. K. W., Wang, W. X., & Ma, Y. N. (2022). Oxytocin and the Punitive Hub-Dynamic Spread of Cooperation in Human Social Networks. *Journal of Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in repeated networked games (trust game, ultimatum bargaining, and two-stage prisoner's dilemma with punishment), oxytocin administration to central (hub) players increases their willingness to enforce cooperation norms via costly punishment. This enforcement, rather than mere cooperation or trust, is what enables cooperation to spread throughout the network. Agent-based simulations confirm that high punishment rates by central nodes are more effective than high cooperation rates alone in promoting network-wide cooperation. Manipulating peripheral nodes' punishment or cooperation has much weaker effects. The main outcomes are cooperation and punishment rates, not group efficiency or total payoff, though some payoff equality measures are reported in the ultimatum game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence and effectiveness of costly punishment, especially when wielded by central/influential players, is a key moderator of cooperation in repeated networked social dilemmas. However, the main outcomes are behavioral (cooperation, punishment, norm enforcement), not group efficiency or total payoff. The findings suggest that, in networked repeated games with punishment, enabling effective punishment by central players can substantially increase cooperation rates, and by extension, may improve efficiency, but this is not directly measured. The results are most relevant for predicting behavioral outcomes (cooperation, norm enforcement) rather than efficiency as defined by group payoff relative to full cooperation. The design dimensions most strongly supported are network structure (heterogeneity, centrality), punishment existence and effectiveness, and repeated interaction. The study does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency change due to punishment, so its use for predicting efficiency outcomes is indirect and should be supplemented with studies that report group payoff or efficiency directly.

- source: Pansini, R., Shi, L., & Wang, R. W. (2016). Women Tend to Defect in a Social Dilemma Game in Southwest China. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a repeated dyadic Prisoner's Dilemma with a punishment option, women in Southwest China defect more and cooperate less than men, a pattern that contrasts with previous findings from Western and other Asian samples. Both genders earned less when they punished, and there was no significant gender difference in overall monetary earnings. The use of punishment did not increase individual earnings, and the paper does not analyze group efficiency or total welfare. The main findings are about behavioral strategies and gender differences, not about the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that in a dyadic iterated Prisoner's Dilemma with a punishment option, punishment is used more frequently than in comparable Western samples, but its use is associated with lower individual earnings. The design is not a public goods game but a two-player PD, so direct prediction for PGG efficiency is limited. The main decision support is that punishment, as implemented here, does not increase individual payoffs and may reduce them. The paper does not report group efficiency or the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, so its value for predicting efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments is limited.

- source: Chen, M. L. (2012). The Effect of Leader Reward and Punishment Behaviors on Subordinates' Budget Reports. *Engineering Economist*.
