  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that hands-off (no reward or punishment) is least effective in reducing budgetary slack. Punishment alone reduces slack more than reward alone, but the combination of reward and punishment (carrot-stick) is most effective. The effect of punishment is stronger when the punishment is large relative to the benefit of slack creation. Rewards alone, regardless of size, have little effect. Requiring a factual assertion (honesty) also reduces slack. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (slack/honesty).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment, especially when combined with reward, reduces dishonest behavior (budgetary slack) in a principal-agent reporting game. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes in public goods games. The design is adjacent to PGGs, as it is a one-superior/one-subordinate budget reporting game, not a multi-player public goods game. The findings are most useful for understanding the behavioral impact of punishment and reward on honesty/slack, but less so for predicting efficiency in PGG-like environments.

- source: Goeschl, T., & Jarke, J. (2016). Second and third party punishment under costly monitoring. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that when monitoring is free, almost all punishers are 'targeted punishers' who punish defectors more than cooperators. As monitoring becomes costly, the share of targeted punishers drops sharply, and more subjects either do not punish at all or punish indiscriminately. The average punishment for defectors falls substantially as monitoring costs rise, and the difference in punishment between defectors and cooperators (the 'incentive to cooperate') is much lower under costly monitoring. These patterns are similar for both second and third party punishment, with some subtle differences in how subjects switch between punisher types across roles. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the authors argue that lower monitoring costs should lead to more efficient outcomes by making punishment more effective at supporting cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the cost of monitoring (i.e., the ability to condition punishment on observed behavior) is a critical moderator of the effectiveness of punishment in social dilemma games. When monitoring is costly, punishment is less targeted and less effective at creating incentives for cooperation. While the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, the findings suggest that in games where monitoring is costly or imperfect, enabling punishment will have a weaker (or possibly negligible) effect on efficiency compared to settings with free or perfect monitoring. For prediction tasks, the presence and cost of monitoring should be considered a key dimension when estimating the efficiency impact of punishment. However, because the study does not report actual efficiency or payoff outcomes, its direct predictive value for efficiency is limited.

- source: Xie, E. H., Liu, M. D., Liu, J. Q., Gao, X. X., & Li, X. C. (2022). Neural mechanisms of the mood effects on third-party responses to injustice after unfair experiences. *Human Brain Mapping*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that individuals who experience disadvantageous unfairness (receiving less than others) are more likely to choose third-party punishment in a subsequent justice game, while those who experience advantageous unfairness (receiving more than others) are more likely to choose third-party compensation. These effects are mediated by mood (negative emotion increases punishment, positive emotion increases compensation) and sense of unfairness. Neural data show that increased DLPFC activity is associated with compensation after advantageous unfairness, while decreased DLPFC activity is associated with punishment after disadvantageous unfairness. The study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and the design is not a standard public goods game but a third-party justice game with costly punishment and compensation options.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The design is adjacent to PGGs, focusing on third-party responses to unfairness rather than first-party cooperation or group provision. However, it provides informative evidence about the psychological and neural mechanisms that may underlie punishment and compensation choices, and how prior experiences and mood can shift the likelihood of punishment versus compensation. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly relevant, as it does not address how punishment affects group payoff or efficiency.

- source: Bone, J. E., McAuliffe, K., & Raihani, N. J. (2016). Exploring the Motivations for Punishment: Framing and Country-Level Effects. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that punishment behavior in a two-player stealing game is strongly influenced by country of origin. US-based participants are more likely to punish in response to both losses and disadvantageous inequality, while India-based participants are more punitive overall and engage in more antisocial punishment, but only increase punishment when experiencing disadvantageous inequality. Methodological factors such as stake size and punishment cost (within the tested range) do not significantly affect punishment rates. The study does not analyze efficiency or group payoff, and all outcome measures are behavioral (punishment frequency) rather than payoff-based.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not report or analyze efficiency, welfare, or total earnings. However, it provides informative evidence about how punishment behavior varies with country of origin and with the presence of disadvantageous inequality, which may be relevant for understanding when punishment is likely to be used. The design is adjacent to PGGs (two-player stealing game with punishment), but not a PGG or threshold public goods game. The findings suggest that punishment rates and motives can vary substantially by context and population, which may moderate any efficiency effects in related games.

- source: Li, J. B., Pan, J. J., Zhu, C. K., & Wang, Y. W. (2022). Inter-brain synchronization is weakened by the introduction of external punishment. *Social Cognitive and Affective Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of external punishment (i.e., making responder rejection payoff-relevant in the rrUG) leads to significantly higher mean offers from proposers compared to the rrDG (no punishment). However, the main focus is on neural outcomes: inter-brain synchronization is significantly lower in the punishment condition (rrUG) than in the no-punishment condition (rrDG). There is no significant effect of punishment on the rejection rate of responders. The study interprets the neural results as evidence that punishment crowds out moral motivation, reducing shared cognitive/emotional processing between players. No direct measures of group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that introducing punishment (via responder rejection in a two-player ultimatum game) increases proposer generosity (mean offer size), which could be associated with higher group payoffs if responders accept. However, the study does not report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, so it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. The design is adjacent to public goods games but is not a PGG or close variant. The main outcome is neural (inter-brain synchronization), not payoff-based. Thus, the paper offers only weak, indirect support for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Bauer, M., Cahlfková, J., Chytilová, J., Roland, G., & Zelinsky, T. (2023). Shifting Punishment onto Minorities: Experimental Evidence of Scapegoating. *Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that when a member of the punisher's own group is harmed by a wrongdoer, majority-group punishers are much more likely to punish an innocent minority (Roma) scapegoat than an innocent majority scapegoat. The amount of punishment assigned to the scapegoat increases with the intensity of harm done by the wrongdoer, and this increase is twice as large when the scapegoat is from the minority group. No ethnic bias is observed when no harm is done. The effect is robust to controls and is not explained by collective responsibility, over-attribution of blame, or unconditional spite. The design does not involve a public goods game or measure efficiency, but provides direct evidence on punishment behavior and ethnic bias in scapegoating.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on efficiency or group payoff in public goods or social dilemma settings, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in such games. However, it provides high-quality evidence on how punishment behavior can be biased by group identity and context, especially in situations where punishment is displaced onto innocent parties. The punishment mechanism here is highly effective (low cost, high impact), and punishment is purely destructive. The findings may be relevant for understanding the social and psychological moderators of punishment behavior, but not for predicting efficiency outcomes in standard PGG-like environments.

- source: Boschini, A., Muren, A., & Persson, M. (2011). Men among men do not take norm enforcement seriously. *Journal of Socio-economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that the inclination to punish norm-breakers (interpreted as norm enforcement) is context-dependent: men among men are less likely to punish than women or men in mixed-gender settings. This result holds both for individuals and for groups. The difference is substantial (about 25% lower punishment rates for men-among-men) and statistically significant. However, the paper does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined from this study. The findings are relevant for understanding who enforces cooperation norms, but not for predicting the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-like environments. It does, however, provide informative evidence about the behavioral propensity to punish under different gender and group contexts in a sequential prisoner's dilemma. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that the presence and composition of gender in groups may affect the likelihood of punishment being used, but it does not inform how this translates into efficiency outcomes. The game is not a standard public goods game, and efficiency is not measured or discussed. Thus, the paper is only indirectly relevant for predicting efficiency effects of punishment, and should be weighted accordingly.

- source: Rommel, J. (2015). What can economic experiments tell us about institutional change in social-ecological systems?. *Environmental Science & Policy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that allowing third-party punishment of traders (who cause negative externalities) leads to a significant reduction in market entry rates compared to the baseline, especially for sellers. About 86% of observers are willing to punish, spending on average more than half their endowment to do so. The conditional entry treatment also reduces market entry, as about a quarter of participants condition their entry on others' behavior, reflecting norm conformity. The main outcome is the proportion of participants who choose to trade (i.e., cause the externality), not group efficiency or total payoff. The findings show that punishment can enforce social norms and reduce harmful market behavior, but the study does not measure efficiency as defined in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the availability of costly punishment reduces the frequency of norm-violating behavior (trading that causes negative externalities) in a large market setting. However, because the outcome is market entry rate (not group efficiency or total payoff), the results are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency in public goods games. The punishment mechanism is effective at deterring harmful behavior, especially among sellers, and most observers are willing to punish at a cost. For prediction tasks, the main signal is that enabling punishment reduces the rate of norm violation, but the mapping to efficiency or group payoff in standard PGGs is indirect. The market structure, one-shot design, and externality to a third party differ from standard PGGs, so caution is needed in transferring quantitative effects.

- source: Baum, W. M., Paciotti, B., Richerson, P., Lubell, M., & McElreath, R. (2012). Cooperation due to cultural norms, not individual reputation. *Behavioural Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that in a trust game with a conditional fine, trustees return more to investors from their own ethnic group when no fine is available. When a fine is available and imposed, trustworthiness increases in cross-ethnic pairs, eliminating the in-group advantage. In same-ethnic pairs, the fine has no effect or may crowd out trustworthiness. The effect is similar when the fine is available but not used. The study does not report group efficiency or total earnings as a main outcome, but the behavioral results suggest that punishment is more effective at increasing trustworthiness across ethnic lines than within them.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation (measured as trustworthiness) depends on group identity: punishment increases trustworthiness in cross-ethnic pairs but not in same-ethnic pairs. However, because the outcome is not group efficiency or total payoff, the results should be used with caution for predicting efficiency. The design is a modified trust game, not a public goods game, so transferability to PGGs is limited but informative for settings where group identity and punishment interact. The punishment is costless to the punisher and has a fixed impact, which should be considered when mapping to other designs.

- source: Jordan, J. J., & Kteily, N. S. (2023). How reputation does (and does not) drive people to punish without looking. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that reputational incentives can increase the likelihood that individuals engage in punishment (signing punitive petitions), and as a byproduct, some of this punishment occurs without considering opposing perspectives. However, when the process of 'looking' is made observable, individuals are more likely to consider opposing perspectives, and punishment without looking does not increase. Evaluators (the audience) reward punishment in general, but do not reward punishment without looking; in fact, they see it as less fair and competent. The effect is stronger with more ideological audiences. The study does not use a public goods game or measure group efficiency, but rather focuses on reputational and behavioral outcomes in a Dictator Game context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or social dilemmas. Its design is adjacent: it uses a reputational Dictator Game with real-world punitive actions (signing petitions) and manipulates observability of punishment and information-gathering. The main outcomes are behavioral (punishment, looking) and reputational (evaluations, money shared as a reward), not group efficiency or welfare. The findings suggest that making punishment observable increases punishment (and punishment without looking), but making deliberation observable increases deliberation. For prediction tasks about efficiency in PGGs, this paper provides only indirect, contextual evidence about how reputational incentives can increase punishment rates, but not about their effect on group efficiency or welfare.

- source: Zhang, L., Jin, Y., Xia, L., Xu, B. B., & Abdullah, S. M. S. (2022). The Effects of Social Distance and Asymmetric Reward and Punishment on Individual Cooperative Behavior in Dilemma Situations. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that (1) cooperation rates are higher with friends than with strangers; (2) symmetric rewards and punishments lead to higher cooperation rates than asymmetric ones; (3) introducing a punishment option reduces cooperation rates compared to a baseline with only cooperation/defection; (4) asymmetric punishment especially reduces cooperation among friends. The main outcomes are behavioral (cooperation, defection, punishment), not efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that punishment, especially when asymmetric, can undermine cooperation, but the paper does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that introducing punishment (especially asymmetric punishment) can reduce cooperation rates in iterated prisoner's dilemma games, and that this effect is stronger among socially close participants. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. The findings suggest that in similar environments, enabling punishment may not increase efficiency and may even reduce it if it reduces cooperation, but this is an inference from behavioral outcomes, not a direct measurement of efficiency.

- source: Weiss, M., Saulin, A., Iotzov, V., Hewig, J., & Hein, G. (2023). Can monetary incentives overturn fairness-based decisions?. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that offering monetary incentives that conflict with fairness-based punishment or compensation reduces the frequency of fairness-congruent decisions and slows the decision process (lower drift rate in DDM). This effect is similar for both punishment and compensation domains. The effect of conflicting incentives is stronger at higher incentive levels. The study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, but rather focuses on behavioral and cognitive process outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that monetary incentives can undermine fairness-based punishment and compensation behavior, reducing the likelihood and efficiency of such decisions. However, because the study does not report group efficiency or payoff outcomes, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The design is adjacent to PGGs, using a modified dictator game with punishment/compensation framing, but lacks repeated interaction, group structure, or explicit efficiency measures. The main predictive value is in showing that the cost and alignment of incentives can strongly moderate the behavioral impact of punishment mechanisms.

- source: Martin, J. W., Martin, S., & McAuliffe, K. (2021). Third-Party Punishment Promotes Fairness in Children. *Developmental Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Third-party punishment (costly, by an adult experimenter) increases the rate at which younger children (ages 6-7) choose the fairer allocation in a forced-choice dictator game, compared to both a baseline and a reputation-only (observer) condition. The effect is not present in older children (ages 8-9), who already show high rates of fair choices. The mechanism appears to be direct feedback from punishment, not reputational concern. The study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, and the design is a dictator game rather than a public goods game or its direct variant.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that third-party punishment can increase norm-compliant (fair) behavior in a resource allocation game among children, especially those in a developmental window where fairness norms are emerging. However, the outcome is not efficiency or group payoff, and the game is a forced-choice dictator game, not a public goods game or a repeated social dilemma. Thus, the findings are only indirectly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The main value is as adjacent evidence that punishment can shape norm compliance in allocation tasks, especially for younger participants, but it does not provide direct evidence for efficiency or payoff outcomes.

- source: Li, J., Zhu, L. Q., & Chen, Z. (2018). The association between punishment and cooperation in children with high-functioning autism. *Journal of Experimental Child Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Punishment (administered by a third party, the experimenter) for defection in a repeated PDG increases the rate of cooperation among both HFA and TD children. The effect is robust to IQ and group (HFA vs TD), with no significant interaction. The study provides evidence that children with HFA understand the concept of punishment and respond to it by increasing cooperation. However, the study does not report on group payoff, efficiency, or total earnings, so the effect of punishment on efficiency or welfare cannot be determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that introducing punishment in a repeated two-player PDG increases cooperation rates among children, including those with high-functioning autism. However, because the primary outcome is cooperation rate and not efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency outcomes. The design is adjacent to public goods games (PDG vs PGG), and the punishment is third-party and automatic for defection. The study supports the idea that punishment can increase cooperative behavior, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or welfare effects.

- source: Mieth, L., Buchner, A., & Bell, R. (2017). Effects of Gender on Costly Punishment. *Journal of Behavioral Decision Making*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that costly punishment is used frequently in a two-player prisoner's dilemma with a punishment option, primarily as moralistic punishment (punishing defection after cooperating). Participants spend more money punishing defecting female partners than male partners, but this is due to higher cooperation rates with female partners, not a direct gender bias in punishment. Female participants are more likely to cooperate with and punish female partners, while male participants do not differentiate by partner gender. The presence of punishment by partners increases cooperation rates, but the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoffs, nor does it compare efficiency with and without punishment in a way that would allow direct prediction of efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that costly punishment is used in a two-player prisoner's dilemma, and that the opportunity to punish is strongly influenced by cooperation rates, which in turn are affected by gender dynamics. However, it does not report group efficiency or total payoffs, nor does it provide a control (no-punishment) condition for direct comparison. The findings suggest that punishment can increase cooperation when the threat of being punished exists, but the lack of efficiency or group payoff data limits its direct utility for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games or their variants. The design is adjacent to PGGs, and the behavioral findings may inform models of punishment and cooperation, but not efficiency prediction per se.

- source: Güth, W., Kliemt, H., Levati, M. V., & von Wangenheim, G. (2007). On the coevolution of retribution and trustworthiness:: An (indirect) evolutionary and experimental analysis. *Journal of Institutional and Theoretical Economics-zeitschrift Fur Die Gesamte Staatswissenschaft*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that most participants behave trustworthily (exert effort) and that a small but significant share of punishment (revenge/suing) persists, even among those without monetary inducement. The share of trustworthiness and punishment does not depend on the number of monetarily induced 'moral' players or the order of treatments. There is a weak positive correlation between observed untrustworthiness and subsequent punishment. Monetary inducement increases the likelihood of trustworthiness and punishment among targeted individuals, but not to the level predicted by rational choice. The main outcomes are behavioral; the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a repeated trust game with costly punishment, the availability of punishment leads to persistent (though low) rates of punishment and high rates of trustworthiness, even without material incentives. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games. The behavioral findings suggest that punishment can support trustworthiness, but the lack of payoff analysis limits its direct utility for efficiency prediction tasks.

- source: Vollan, B., Blanco, E., Steimanis, I., Petutschnig, F., & Prediger, S. (2020). Procedural fairness and nepotism among local traditional and democratic leaders in rural Namibia. *Science Advances*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that both traditional chiefs and democratically elected leaders in rural Namibia are similarly likely to implement democratic decision-making procedures and are similarly unlikely to engage in nepotistic punishment. Chiefs may be slightly more likely to choose democratic procedures and less likely to be lenient toward relatives in punishment, but these differences are not statistically robust. The main outcomes are behavioral (procedural choice, nepotism in punishment), not efficiency or group payoff. The punishment task is a trust game with third-party punishment, not a public goods game. The study suggests that, in this context of legal pluralism, traditional leaders are not more autocratic or nepotistic than elected leaders.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence on punishment behavior (altruistic punishment) and procedural fairness in a trust game with third-party punishment, not in a public goods game. It does not report efficiency or group payoff as a main outcome, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it does show that, in a real-world setting, leaders' willingness to punish norm violations is not strongly affected by whether they are democratically elected or traditional chiefs, and that nepotism in punishment is low for both. The design and context differ from standard PGGs, so the transfer value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGGs is limited.

- source: Takahashi, R., & Tanaka, K. (2021). Social punishment for breaching restrictions during the COVID-19 pandemic. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that individuals are willing to engage in costly punishment against stores that breach social norms (COVID-19 restrictions), as evidenced by a significant reduction in donations to such stores at personal cost. The average reduction for norm-breakers was about 35 yen (11% of the average donation). However, when participants were told that norm-breaking stores adhered to safety guidelines or faced bankruptcy risk, the level of costly punishment was reduced to be statistically indistinguishable from the control (complier) group. The results suggest that punishment for norm-breaking is sensitive to the presence of competing or contrasting norms, but the outcome measured is not group efficiency or total payoff, but rather the willingness to punish at a cost.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the psychological and behavioral drivers of costly punishment in a norm-violation context, but does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare as in public goods games. The findings are relevant for understanding when and why individuals engage in costly punishment, and how this can be moderated by information about other norms. However, because the experiment is not a public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it is only indirectly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments. The main value is in showing that punishment can be reduced by introducing competing normative considerations.

- source: Tang, Z. X., Qu, C., Hu, Y., Benistant, J., Moisan, F., Derrington, E., & Dreher, J. C. (2023). Strengths of social ties modulate brain computations for third-party punishment. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that third-party punishment increases as the social distance between the punisher and the norm violator increases. The costliness of punishment reduces the amount of punishment assigned, but the effect of social distance remains robust. Computational modeling shows that participants integrate social distance, inequity aversion, and cost to punish when making punishment decisions. fMRI results identify brain regions involved in these computations. However, the study does not report on group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, and thus does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the willingness to punish norm violators depends on the social distance between the punisher and the violator, and that costliness of punishment reduces punishment levels. However, because the experiment is a third-party punishment variant of the Dictator Game (not a public goods game), and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings may be useful for understanding moderators of punishment behavior, especially the role of social distance and cost, but cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes.

- source: Bartos, V., & Levely, I. (2021). Sanctioning and trustworthiness across ethnic groups: Experimental evidence from Afghanistan. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that during periods of scarcity (lean season), participants are significantly less willing to engage in costly third-party punishment to enforce sharing norms, as measured by a lower minimum acceptable offer (MAO) in the TPPG. However, the amount shared by dictators (Player A) in both the DG and TPPG remains stable across seasons, indicating that sharing behavior is robust to scarcity even as enforcement weakens. The results are robust to various controls and alternative explanations. No direct analysis of group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare is provided.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the willingness to punish norm violations (i.e., enforce sharing) is sensitive to resource scarcity, but actual sharing behavior is not. However, because the design is a one-shot dictator/TPPG (not a repeated public goods game), and because no group efficiency or total payoff outcomes are reported, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated PGG-like environments. The main implication is that punishment may become less frequent or intense under scarcity, but this does not necessarily reduce sharing in the short run. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency may be context-dependent and potentially weaker under scarcity, but does not provide direct quantitative evidence for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Pan, X. F., & Houser, D. (2019). Why trust out-groups? The role of punishment under uncertainty. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds an asymmetric effect of punishment on trust: punishment opportunities increase non-social group investors' trust towards out-group trustees, but have no effect for social group investors or for in-group trust. Social group members are less likely to punish, and their trust in out-group members is higher even without punishment. The effect of punishment on trust is mediated by beliefs about trustee reciprocity. No direct measures of efficiency, group payoff, or welfare are reported, though the discussion suggests that social group formation may reduce the need for costly punishment and thus could improve efficiency in principle.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about how group identity formation and punishment opportunities interact to affect trust and punishment behavior in a trust game with hidden action. However, it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings are most useful for understanding behavioral mechanisms (trust, punishment, beliefs) rather than payoff-based outcomes. The design is adjacent to PGGs, but not a direct PGG or linear public goods game. Use this paper for qualitative insight into how punishment and group identity interact, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency effects.

- source: Li, Z., Hu, G. D., & Li, Q. Q. (2023). Impact of Social Identity Complexity in Unfair Events on Intergroup Bias in Third-Party Fairness Maintenance. *Behavioral Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that when third parties are presented with multiple social identities (vs. a single group identity) for the parties involved in unfair dictator game allocations, they show less punitive behavior toward outgroup violators and more compensatory behavior toward outgroup victims. However, the treatment does not affect behavior toward ingroup members. The reduction in intergroup bias is thus achieved by reducing outgroup hate, not by reducing ingroup love. The study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes related to punishment and compensation choices.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that social identity complexity can moderate the behavioral expression of punishment and compensation in third-party fairness maintenance, specifically by reducing outgroup hate. However, because the study does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. Its main relevance is in suggesting that features which increase perceived social identity complexity may reduce punitive behavior toward outgroup members, which could, in principle, affect efficiency if such behaviors are costly, but this is not tested or quantified in terms of group payoff.

- source: Lönnqvist, J. E., Rilke, R. M., & Walkowitz, G. (2015). On why hypocrisy thrives: Reasonable doubt created by moral posturing can deter punishment. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in ultimatum games, proposers can reduce the likelihood of being punished for unfair offers by claiming that a random process (coin flip) determined the outcome. This effect holds even when responders do not believe the claim and is observed for both direct victims (responders) and third-party observers (judges). The reduction in punishment is not explained by responders' self-interest or gratitude. The studies do not report on group efficiency or total earnings, but the findings suggest that moral posturing can deter punishment and thus may allow unfair behavior to persist.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of plausible deniability (moral posturing) can reduce the frequency of punishment in bargaining games, even when punishment is available and even if the claim is not believed. However, the study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The findings are most relevant for understanding moderators of punishment behavior, especially the role of uncertainty or doubt about intent, rather than the direct effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Roberts, S. C., Vakirtzis, A., Kristjánsdóttir, L., & Havlícek, J. (2013). Who Punishes? Personality Traits Predict Individual Variation in Punitive Sentiment. *Evolutionary Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=other_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that individual differences in willingness to punish a transgressor in a public-goods-like scenario are predicted by personality traits, especially lower Agreeableness. Other traits (Conscientiousness, Extraversion) have weaker or more limited associations. The study uses hypothetical scenarios where participants indicate their anger, desire to punish, and willingness to pay to punish a free-rider, but no actual economic game is played and no payoff or efficiency data are collected. The findings are relevant for understanding who is likely to engage in costly punishment, but do not inform the effect of punishment on group efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or variants. It does not manipulate or measure any game design dimensions relevant to efficiency, nor does it report control or treatment efficiency. Its findings are about individual differences in punitive sentiment, not about the consequences of punishment for group outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used to predict efficiency effects of punishment based on game design dimensions.

- source: Dhaliwal, N. A., Skarlicki, D. P., Hoegg, J., & Daniels, M. A. (2022). Consequentialist Motives for Punishment Signal Trustworthiness. *Journal of Business Ethics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that individuals who express or are perceived to have consequentialist motives for punishing norm violators are rated as more trustworthy, are entrusted with more money in Trust Game scenarios, and are chosen more often as cooperation partners compared to those with deontological motives. This effect is mediated by lower perceived psychopathy. The findings are robust across different phrasings of motives, experimental vignettes, and real-world recall. However, the studies do not examine actual group cooperation rates, efficiency, or payoff-based outcomes in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on group efficiency or payoff in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are relevant for understanding how the rationale for punishment affects the punisher's reputation and trustworthiness, which may indirectly influence cooperation, but it does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare. Therefore, it should not be used as primary evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions or control efficiency. It may be useful for understanding psychological mechanisms or for modeling how punishment motives affect trust and partner selection.

- source: Li, Z., Hu, G. D., Xu, L., & Li, Q. Q. (2021). Third-Party Punishment or Compensation? It Depends on the Reputational Benefits. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that when third parties are not free to choose between punishment and compensation, there is no reputational difference between the two methods, and both are rated better than non-intervention. When third parties are free to choose, compensation is rated as more reputable than punishment. In behavioral experiments, third parties are more likely to punish under secret (private) conditions and more likely to compensate under open (public) conditions. The amount of intervention (punishment/compensation) is higher in open contexts. However, the study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. It does, however, provide informative evidence about how reputational context and the availability of alternative interventions (compensation) affect the likelihood and type of third-party intervention. If the prediction task involves settings where reputation is salient or where third parties can choose between punishment and compensation, this paper suggests that compensation may be preferred in public contexts, and punishment in private contexts. However, since efficiency or payoff outcomes are not reported, the paper cannot be used to calibrate the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Duersch, P., & Müller, J. (2015). Taking punishment into your own hands: An experiment. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that a substantial minority of subjects are willing to pay to personally punish an unfair allocator, even when punishment is guaranteed to occur regardless of who enacts it. Bids for personal punishment are significantly higher than for a dummy (non-punishment) auction, indicating a specific demand for personal retribution. Winning the right to punish personally increases self-reported happiness. The study does not report on group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, and the game structure is not a public goods game or close variant.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their close variants. Its findings are relevant for understanding the psychological motives behind punishment, especially the value placed on personal retribution, but do not inform predictions about efficiency changes when punishment is enabled in PGG-like environments. The design is a one-shot real-effort allocation game with a punishment stage, not a repeated or multi-player public goods game. Therefore, it should not be used to predict efficiency outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: Salcedo, J. C., & Jimenez-Leal, W. (2024). Severity and deservedness determine signalled trustworthiness in third party punishment. *British Journal of Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across four experiments, the paper finds that (1) third-party punishment signals trustworthiness to observers, but only when the punishment is deserved and appropriately severe; (2) the trustworthiness signal increases with punishment severity, but only when higher severity entails higher cost to the punisher; (3) when punishment severity is increased without increasing cost, the trustworthiness signal does not increase; (4) refraining from punishing when punishment is undeserved can also signal trustworthiness, sometimes more than punishing. The main outcome is the amount entrusted to the punisher in a subsequent trust game, not group efficiency or total payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on group efficiency or payoff in public goods games or their close variants. Its findings are relevant for understanding how punishment is perceived and how it affects trust in subsequent interactions, but not for predicting changes in group efficiency when punishment is enabled. The experimental design is adjacent to PGGs (using TPP and trust games), and the main outcomes are behavioral (trust, partner choice) rather than payoff-based. Therefore, this paper should not be used as primary evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments, but may be informative for understanding reputational and signaling mechanisms.

- source: Baumgartner, T., Hausfeld, J., dos Santos, M., & Knoch, D. (2021). Who initiates punishment, who joins punishment? Disentangling types of third-party punishers by neural traits. *Human Brain Mapping*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study demonstrates the existence of three distinct types of third-party punishers (independent, conditional, nonpunishers) in a laboratory setting. These types are characterized by different neural traits (gray matter volume in right TPJ, right DLPFC, and bilateral caudate) and by different self-reported motives. The paradigm allows for conditional punishment, where individuals can choose to join punishment only if someone else initiates it. The main findings are about the classification and neural/psychological correlates of these types, not about the impact of punishment on group efficiency or payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. It is informative about individual heterogeneity in punishment behavior and the existence of conditional punishment, which may be relevant for modeling or interpreting punishment dynamics. However, it does not report efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes, nor does it manipulate or analyze game design dimensions in relation to efficiency. Thus, its value for the downstream prediction task is limited to contextual or mechanistic insight about punishment types.

- source: Konishi, N., Oe, T., Shimizu, H., Tanaka, K., & Ohtsubo, Y. (2017). Perceived Shared Condemnation Intensifies Punitive Moral Emotions. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that perceived shared condemnation increases moral outrage and, to a lesser extent, moral disgust, while reducing empathy for norm violators. These emotional shifts are associated with greater willingness to punish, both in hypothetical scenarios and in self-reported real-life interventions. The experimental manipulation in Study 2 shows that providing information about others' condemnation causally increases moral outrage and willingness to impose a fine. However, the studies do not use economic games, do not measure actual punishment behavior with real stakes, and do not report any efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar economic games. It does, however, suggest that social cues about shared condemnation can increase willingness to punish, which may be relevant for understanding psychological drivers of punishment in group settings. For the prediction task, this paper is only indirectly informative: it supports the idea that punishment is more likely when people believe others will also punish, but it does not address how this affects group efficiency, payoff, or cooperation rates in economic games.

- source: Turpie, J., & Letley, G. (2021). Would community conservation initiatives benefit from external financial oversight? A framed field experiment in Namibia's communal conservancies. *World Development*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that individuals' willingness to impose social sanctions (disapproval, social exclusion) for norm violations is conditional on their beliefs about the frequency of such violations: the more common a violation is believed to be, the less likely people are to sanction it. This conditionality is strongest for weaker norms (e.g., tax evasion, fare dodging) and among more educated respondents. For strong norms (e.g., drunk driving), sanctioning is high regardless of beliefs. The study uses survey data from Austria and applies instrumental variable analysis to establish causality. No efficiency, payoff, or group welfare outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence on the determinants of willingness to sanction norm violations in real-world, non-experimental settings, showing that sanctioning is conditional on perceived prevalence for weaker norms. However, it does not study public goods games or variants directly, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Thus, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its main relevance is conceptual: it supports the idea that the effectiveness of punishment as a deterrent may depend on beliefs about norm prevalence, which could inform mechanism design or interpretation of experimental results, but it does not provide quantitative or design-specific guidance for efficiency prediction.

- source: van Dijk, E., De Dreu, C. K. W., & Gross, J. (2020). Power in economic games. *Current Opinion in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds strong evidence for conditional norm enforcement: individuals' stated willingness to sanction norm violations (e.g., tax evasion, fare dodging) is lower when they believe such violations are more common. This effect is most pronounced for weaker norms and among more educated respondents. For strong norms (e.g., drunk driving), sanctioning is high and largely unconditional. The study establishes a causal link between beliefs about norm prevalence and willingness to sanction using an instrumental variables approach. However, all outcomes are based on self-reported intentions in a survey, not on actual behavior or payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games or their variants. Its main contribution is to show that willingness to sanction norm violations is conditional on beliefs about others' compliance, especially for weaker norms. For the downstream prediction task, this suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing cooperation or efficiency may depend on participants' beliefs about the prevalence of norm violations, but the paper does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency or group payoff. The findings are most relevant for understanding psychological and social moderators of punishment behavior, not for predicting efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions.

- source: Rai, T. S. (2022). Material Benefits Crowd Out Moralistic Punishment. *Psychological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The main finding is that paying people to engage in third-party punishment of unfair or immoral behavior reduces their willingness to do so, compared to when no payment is offered. This effect is robust across both real-stakes economic games and hypothetical vignettes. The reduction in willingness is attributed to the corruption of the moral signal that punishment provides; payment makes the act appear less morally motivated. Willingness to punish can be restored either by offering a sufficiently large payment (which compensates for the lost moral signal) or by providing moral assurances that payment does not undermine the punisher's virtue. The study does not address efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and does not use a repeated public goods game structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. Its main contribution is to show that introducing material incentives for punishment can reduce individuals' willingness to punish, unless the incentives are large or are reframed to preserve moral signaling. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff, this paper is only indirectly relevant: it suggests that the design of punishment incentives (especially the presence and framing of material rewards for punishers) could affect the frequency of punishment, which in turn could affect cooperation and efficiency in other settings. However, no direct evidence is provided about efficiency or payoff outcomes.

- source: Ferguson, E., Quigley, E., Powell, G., Stewart, L., Harrison, F., & Tallentire, H. (2019). To help or punish in the face of unfairness: men and women prefer mutually-beneficial strategies over punishment in a sexual selection context. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Across three studies using a one-shot 3PPC game, men primed with a romantic context are more likely to choose compensation or both compensation and punishment, rather than punishment alone or doing nothing. Women rate men who compensate (or both compensate and punish) as more attractive, compassionate, fair, and strong, regardless of whether they are considering short-term or long-term relationships. Trait empathy and left-wing political ideology are associated with a preference for compensation. The studies do not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or any direct monetary outcomes for the group; all outcomes are behavioral preferences or social evaluations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-like games. The design is a one-shot third-party punishment and compensation game, and the outcomes are social preferences and evaluations, not efficiency or payoff. Therefore, it does not directly inform prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency. It may be relevant for understanding social or reputational motivations for punishment and compensation, but not for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Li, J. B., Li, S. Q., Wang, P. C., Liu, X. L., Zhu, C. K., Niu, X. F., Wang, G. R., & Yin, X. L. (2018). Fourth-Party Evaluation of Third-Party Pro-social Help and Punishment: An ERP Study. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that fourth-party bystanders rate third-party help more positively than third-party punishment, especially when the third party can restore fairness between the dictator and the receiver. However, neural responses (FRN) indicate that bystanders initially expect punishment more than help, particularly immediately after witnessing norm violations. The difference in evaluation between help and punishment is modulated by whether the third party can achieve fairness. The study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. Its findings are limited to social evaluations and neural responses to third-party help and punishment in a modified dictator game. While it shows that punishment is expected in the immediate aftermath of norm violations, and help is ultimately rated more positively, it does not address how punishment affects efficiency or payoff outcomes. Thus, it offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Bigoni, M., Spagnolo, G., & Valbonesi, P. (2014). Sticks and Carrots in Procurement: An Experimental Exploration. *B E Journal of Economic Analysis & Policy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that explicit incentives—whether framed as penalties (punishment) or bonuses (reward)—strongly increase total surplus (efficiency) in a repeated buyer-seller game with multitasking and endogenous matching. In the absence of explicit incentives, surplus and effort are low. When explicit incentives are available and used, effort in the contractible dimension rises sharply, while effort in the non-contractible dimension falls only slightly, resulting in a net efficiency gain. There is no significant difference in surplus between bonus and penalty framing, though buyers prefer penalties because they can extract a larger share of the surplus, sometimes through exploitative contracts. The effect of punishment (penalties) on efficiency is robust and positive.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment (penalty contracts) in a repeated, competitive, multitask environment with endogenous matching and incomplete contracts leads to a substantial increase in efficiency (surplus) compared to a control without explicit incentives. The effect is similar for both punishment and reward, but buyers prefer punishment due to distributional advantages. The design is not a standard PGG but is adjacent: it is a repeated, group-based, real-effort game with endogenous matching and both contractible and non-contractible tasks. The findings support the prediction that, in similar environments, enabling punishment will increase efficiency relative to control. The effect is robust to the presence of repeated interaction, endogenous matching, and multitasking.

- source: Romaniuc, R., Dubois, D., Dimant, E., Lupusor, A., & Prohnitchi, V. (2022). Understanding cross-cultural differences in peer reporting practices: evidence from tax evasion games in Moldova and France. *Public Choice*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that enabling costly, anonymous peer reporting (whistleblowing) in a repeated tax evasion game leads to significantly higher tax compliance (i.e., a higher percentage of income declared) compared to a control condition where only social observation is possible. This effect is observed in both Moldova and France, even though Moldovan participants view whistleblowing as less socially acceptable and engage in it less frequently than French participants. The increase in compliance is attributed to the deterrent effect of the possibility of being reported, not just the actual frequency of reporting. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff directly, but the increase in compliance is a strong proxy for improved group outcomes in a public-goods-like context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a costly, anonymous peer reporting mechanism in a repeated tax evasion game (a close variant of a public goods game) increases compliance rates, which is closely related to group efficiency. The effect is robust to large cross-cultural differences in the acceptability and frequency of reporting. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment (here, in the form of peer reporting to a central authority) will increase efficiency or group payoff relative to a control with no such mechanism, even if actual punishment is rare. The effect is not strongly moderated by cultural differences in the willingness to punish, at least in the settings studied. However, the outcome measured is tax compliance, not direct group payoff or efficiency, so the mapping is indirect.

- source: Fabbri, M., Barbieri, P. N., & Bigoni, M. (2019). Ride Your Luck! A Field Experiment on Lottery-Based Incentives for Compliance. *Management Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: A field experiment on city buses in Italy tested a lottery-based reward for buying tickets, layered on top of existing probabilistic sanctions for fare-dodging. Treated buses saw a significant increase in on-board ticket sales (18–30% increase per hour), with no overall reduction in off-board ticket sales, indicating a net increase in compliance and revenue. The increased revenue covered the cost of the prizes actually claimed, but not the total value of all prizes offered, so cost-effectiveness depended on unclaimed prizes. The effect was not persistent after the lottery ended. Suburban routes showed a substitution from off-board to on-board ticket purchases. The study provides evidence that combining probabilistic sanctions with lottery-based rewards can increase compliance and revenue in a real-world public good context, but the net efficiency gain is limited by the cost of rewards and the sustainability of the effect.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides field evidence that adding a lottery-based reward to an environment with probabilistic sanctions increases compliance and revenue (a proxy for efficiency), but the net efficiency gain depends on the cost of the reward and whether all prizes are claimed. The effect is not persistent after removal. For prediction, the presence of both probabilistic sanctions and a reward mechanism (lottery) increases efficiency relative to sanctions alone, but the magnitude of the gain may be modest and sensitive to the cost structure and salience of the reward. The design is not a standard PGG, but the compliance/public good context and outcome measures are closely related. The findings are most informative for settings where compliance is individually observable and can be incentivized with both sanctions and probabilistic rewards.

- source: Ansink, E., Tesfaye, A., Bouma, J., & Brouwer, R. (2017). Cooperation in watershed management: A field experiment on location, trust, and enforcement. *Resource and Energy Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The enforcement (punishment) treatment, which penalizes trustees for not returning at least the amount sent by trustors, increases the amount returned but does not increase trust (investment by trustors). There is no significant effect of enforcement on average payoffs. Framing the game in terms of soil conservation reduces trust. Trust (as measured in the game) is a strong predictor of actual soil conservation investments in the field. The study finds no evidence that enforcement increases cooperation or efficiency in this context, and suggests that enforcement may crowd out trust. The trust game is not a public goods game but is related as a social dilemma with externalities.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that introducing a punishment/enforcement mechanism in a trust game does not increase trust or average payoffs, and may crowd out intrinsic motivation for cooperation. For prediction tasks, this suggests that in trust-game-like environments with high baseline trust, adding punishment may not improve efficiency and could even reduce voluntary cooperation. However, the game is not a standard public goods game, and efficiency is not directly measured as a share of maximum possible group payoff. The enforcement mechanism is probabilistic and only applies to the trustee role. The findings are most relevant for settings where trust and reciprocity are central, and less directly transferable to standard PGGs.

- source: Pan, Q. H., Liu, X. S., Bao, H. L., Su, Y., & He, M. F. (2018). Evolution of cooperation through adaptive interaction in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing and increasing the probability of disconnection (β) after defection leads to a significant increase in the fraction of cooperators in both simulation and human lab experiments. The effect is nonlinear: at low β, the effect is modest, but at higher β, cooperation increases sharply. The mechanism is interpreted as a punishment for defection, as defectors are excluded from interactions and thus lose opportunities for payoff. The system can transition between states of all-defector, all-cooperator, or coexistence depending on the parameters (temptation to defect, cost, and β). While payoffs are discussed and collected, the main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or total group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that a punishment-like mechanism (disconnection after defection) can substantially increase cooperation rates in a spatial prisoner's dilemma environment. However, it does not directly report efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome, so its value for predicting efficiency is indirect. The findings suggest that enabling a punishment mechanism that reduces defectors' ability to interact (and thus earn payoff) can increase cooperation, which is likely to improve efficiency in similar environments, especially when the punishment is strong (high β). The design is adjacent to public goods games, so transfer to PGG-like settings should be cautious. The main predictive signal is that punishment mechanisms that exclude defectors can be effective, especially when the exclusion probability is high.

- source: Molina, M., Nee, V., & Holm, H. (2023). Cooperation with Strangers: Spillover of Community Norms. *Organization Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that among Chinese CEOs, stronger belief that norm violations are met with retaliatory sanctions in their business community is associated with a higher probability of cooperating with a stranger in a one-shot PD. This association is especially strong in some cities (Nanjing, Wenzhou). An online experiment with managers and nonmanagers shows that priming reciprocity norms increases cooperation with strangers in some subgroups and cities. However, the outcome is always the individual choice to cooperate, not group payoff or efficiency. There is no direct evidence on how punishment or norm enforcement affects efficiency or total group earnings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. Its main contribution is showing that belief in norm enforcement (especially retaliatory sanctions) is associated with higher cooperation rates in a one-shot PD. While this suggests that punishment norms can promote cooperation, the lack of payoff or efficiency outcomes, and the use of a PD rather than a PGG, limits its direct applicability to the efficiency prediction task. The findings may be useful for understanding the role of punishment norms in promoting cooperation, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: DAVIS, D. D., & HOLT, C. A. (1994). EQUILIBRIUM COOPERATION IN 3-PERSON, CHOICE-OF-PARTNER GAMES. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that the ability to punish by switching partners does not increase cooperation in two-stage games compared to single-stage controls, but does lead to much higher cooperation rates in ten-stage games. The increase in cooperation in longer games is attributed to both the opportunity for reputation building and the higher cost of being punished (switched away from) early in a long sequence. The punishment mechanism is salient and does not require coordination. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly determined from the reported results.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that salient, unilateral punishment mechanisms (switching partners) can increase cooperation rates in repeated games, but only when the game is sufficiently long to allow for reputation effects and to make the cost of being punished significant. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that the number of rounds (num_rounds) is a critical moderator of punishment effectiveness on cooperation. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment.
