The results are most informative for predicting behavioral (cooperation) outcomes rather than efficiency, and the game is not a standard public goods game but an adjacent partner-selection game with similar strategic features.

- source: Julle-Danière, E., Whitehouse, J., Vrij, A., Gustafsson, E., & Waller, B. M. (2020). The social function of the feeling and expression of guilt. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that individuals who feel more guilt after being blamed for a cooperative failure allocate more of a shared reward to their partner, regardless of friendship strength. When the victim of a transgression (Player 2) perceives their partner as guilty, they are more likely to 'punish' (i.e., take more for themselves) if the partner is a close friend, though this effect is sensitive to outliers. The expression of guilt thus has a complex social function, sometimes leading to harsher punishment from close friends. The main behavioral outcome is the reward split in a dictator game, not efficiency or group payoff. The study does not directly address the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides limited direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The experimental design is adjacent to PGGs, using a one-shot dictator game after a cooperative task with induced guilt, but does not involve repeated contribution decisions, group efficiency, or explicit punishment mechanisms as in standard PGGs. The main outcome is the allocation of a fixed reward, interpreted as relationship repair or punishment, but not as efficiency. The findings suggest that social context (friendship, perceived guilt) can modulate punitive or reparative behavior, but do not provide quantitative evidence on how punishment affects efficiency in PGG-like games. Thus, the paper is more informative about the social and emotional moderators of punishment-like behavior than about efficiency outcomes relevant to the prediction task.

- source: Chambers, C. R. (2024). Nonmonetary Reward Systems, Counterproductive Behavior, and Responses to Sanctions in Open Collaboration Environments. *Organization Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that proximity to nonmonetary reward thresholds is associated with a higher likelihood of counterproductive behavior (suspension), suggesting that reward systems can create performance pressures leading to negative behaviors. However, individuals who are suspended tend to increase their productive contributions just before suspension, and their contribution rates drop after suspension. The paper does not measure efficiency or group payoff, but suggests that formal sanctions may have unintended negative consequences for sustaining contributions in open collaboration environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used to quantitatively predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. However, it offers indirect evidence that formal sanctions (suspensions) may reduce subsequent contribution rates, which could imply a negative effect on group output or efficiency if mapped to a PGG context. The findings suggest that while sanctions may deter counterproductive behavior, they may also discourage future productive contributions, highlighting a potential tradeoff. For prediction tasks, this paper signals caution in assuming that punishment will always improve group outcomes, especially in voluntary, open collaboration settings.

- source: Bshary, A., & Bshary, R. (2010). Interactions Between Sabre-Tooth Blennies and Their Reef Fish Victims: Effects Of Enforced Repeated Game Structure and Local Abundance on Victim Aggression. *Ethology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that resident reef fish species, which cannot avoid repeated interactions with parasitic blennies, are significantly more likely to show aggressive responses (interpreted as punishment) than visitor species, even after controlling for size and territoriality. Aggression is negatively correlated with local abundance (group size), both across species and within the most abundant species, suggesting that the benefits of punishment are diluted in larger groups. The authors interpret these patterns as consistent with theoretical predictions from repeated game and public goods models, but do not directly measure efficiency, group payoff, or the downstream effects of punishment on blenny behavior or group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, ecological evidence relevant to the prediction of punishment effects in public-goods-like settings. It supports the idea that repeated interactions (enforced by residency) increase the likelihood of punishment, and that larger group size dilutes the incentive to punish. However, because the study does not measure efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. Its main value is in supporting the importance of repeated interactions and group size as moderators of punishment behavior, which may inform priors or mechanistic expectations in related experimental PGG studies.

- source: Baumann, F., Benndorf, V., & Friese, M. (2019). Loss-induced emotions and criminal behavior: An experimental analysis. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that loss framing (compared to gain framing) increases negative emotions (anger, frustration) and decreases positive emotions (satisfaction, happiness) for both men and women. However, only men are more likely to take money from a charity (commit a norm violation) in the loss frame, and this behavior is positively correlated with their reported negative emotions. The punishment for norm violation is probabilistic: if caught, the subject pays a fine equal to the amount taken. The study does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, focusing instead on individual norm-violating behavior and emotional states.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. While it includes a punishment mechanism (probabilistic fine for norm violation), the design is not a public goods game and does not report group payoff or efficiency outcomes. The findings are relevant for understanding how emotional states and loss framing affect norm violations under the threat of punishment, but do not inform the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency. The punishment mechanism is similar to those in economic crime experiments, so the paper may provide indirect context for how punishment and emotional states interact, especially with respect to gender differences, but it does not support direct prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Landeo, C. M., & Spier, K. E. (2015). Incentive contracts for teams: Experimental evidence. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that long-term team interaction (infinitely repeated games) increases both the likelihood of team cooperation and the principal's payoff, especially in the absence of communication. Communication between agents also increases cooperation and the principal's payoff in short-term (one-shot) settings, but is a less effective substitute for ongoing interaction. Long-term teams enable the principal to induce cooperation with lower sharing rules, reducing the cost of cooperation and increasing efficiency. However, if the principal chooses the lowest possible sharing rule, agents may engage in costly punishment (shirking) as negative reciprocity, which can reduce efficiency. The effects are robust across endogenous (human principal) and exogenous (computer) environments, but negative reciprocity is stronger when the principal is a human.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling ongoing interaction (repeated games) among team members increases efficiency and allows for cooperation at a lower cost to the principal, especially when communication is not available. Communication can substitute for repeated interaction to some extent in one-shot games, but does not further increase efficiency when repeated interaction is already present. The findings suggest that, in environments structurally similar to this experiment (team production with effort complementarity and binary effort choices), introducing repeated interaction or communication will increase efficiency, but the effect of communication is limited when repetition is already present. The results also highlight that punitive responses (shirking as punishment) can reduce efficiency if the incentive structure is perceived as unfair, especially with human principals. For prediction, the presence of repeated interaction (long-term teams) is a strong positive moderator of punishment's effect on efficiency, while communication is a weaker, context-dependent moderator.

- source: Gallo, E., Riyanto, Y. E., Roy, N., & Teh, T. H. (2022). Cooperation and punishment mechanisms in uncertain and dynamic social networks. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated networked Prisoner's Dilemma with 12 players per group and endogenous link formation/removal, the presence of reputational uncertainty (15% chance that intended actions are flipped) leads to a substantial decrease in both cooperation rate and welfare (group payoff), regardless of whether the network is updated slowly or quickly. Fast network updating (more frequent opportunities to change links) has only a marginally positive effect on welfare and cooperation, and does not offset the negative impact of uncertainty. The mechanism is that uncertainty makes players more lenient and forgiving in network punishment (less likely to remove links from defectors), which disrupts the formation of cooperative clusters. Action punishment (retaliatory defection) is also weakened under uncertainty. The study provides direct evidence that punishment mechanisms relying on accurate reputation signals are less effective in promoting efficiency when monitoring is noisy.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in dynamic networked repeated games with endogenous partner selection, the effectiveness of punishment mechanisms (here, network punishment via link removal) in increasing efficiency is highly sensitive to the accuracy of reputation signals. When there is reputational uncertainty, punishment is less effective and group efficiency (welfare) is significantly reduced. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of noise or uncertainty in monitoring should be treated as a major moderator of punishment's effect on efficiency. The results also indicate that simply increasing the frequency of punishment opportunities (e.g., faster network updating) does not robustly increase efficiency, especially when uncertainty is present. The findings are most directly relevant to networked PD games, but the mechanisms may transfer to PGG-like environments with endogenous partner choice and noisy monitoring.

- source: Przepiorka, W., & Diekmann, A. (2020). Binding Contracts, Non-Binding Promises and Social Feedback in the Intertemporal Common-Pool Resource Game. *Games*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: In a repeated intertemporal common-pool resource game with five players and ten rounds, the study compares the effects of (1) binding contracts, (2) non-binding promises, (3) private social feedback, and (4) public social feedback, against a no-regulation control. Binding contracts, when unanimously signed, lead to the highest group efficiency (average profit per round close to the cooperative optimum). Non-binding promises increase sustainable investment and efficiency initially, but the effect decays over time. Private feedback (where only the recipient sees ratings) does not improve efficiency over the control. Public feedback (where feedback scores are visible to all) significantly increases sustainable investment and group efficiency, with the effect being more stable over time than promises. The study provides direct evidence that public, but not private, social feedback can substitute for formal sanctions in promoting efficiency in CPR dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated CPR games with continuous contribution choices, public social feedback (where feedback is visible to all) can substantially increase group efficiency, even in the absence of formal punishment or monetary sanctions. Private feedback is ineffective. Binding contracts are most effective but require unanimous agreement. For prediction tasks, the presence of public feedback (show_other_summaries = yes, show_punishment_id = yes) should be expected to increase efficiency relative to control, while private feedback (show_other_summaries = yes, show_punishment_id = no) should not. The absence of monetary punishment or reward mechanisms means that the findings are most relevant for settings where social (non-monetary) sanctions are possible. The game design dimensions (player_count = 5, num_rounds = 10, continuous contributions, no chat, known rounds, full feedback on others' choices and payoffs) are well-specified and should be used as reference points for prediction.

- source: Romano, A., Gross, J., & De Dreu, C. K. W. (2024). The nasty neighbor effect in humans. *Science Advances*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper demonstrates that, contrary to standard expectations from in-group favoritism, people are more likely to waste resources in costly competition (attack and defense) with in-group members than with out-group members or strangers. This 'nasty neighbor effect' is observed in attacker-defender contest games and in a nested social dilemma with an attack option. The effect is robust across 51 countries, a field experiment in Kenya, and representative UK samples. The increased conflict investment with in-group members directly reduces efficiency and group earnings. The effect is not explained by a lack of in-group favoritism in cooperation, as trust and trustworthiness are still higher toward in-group members. Instead, the two phenomena coexist and are uncorrelated at the individual and country level. The nasty neighbor effect is stronger when individuals perceive greater resource scarcity or lower status within their group. In a nested public goods game with an attack option, participants assigned more costly deductions to lower-status in-group members, further reducing group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in contest or public-goods-like environments with an option for costly attack or punishment, the availability and use of such options can reduce group efficiency, especially when directed at in-group members. The findings suggest that enabling costly punishment or attack (even when it is not norm-enforcing but status-seeking or competitive) can decrease total group earnings and joint welfare, particularly under conditions of perceived resource scarcity or status competition. For prediction tasks, this paper indicates that the presence of a punishment/attack option, especially with high punishment effectiveness (e.g., 1:3 cost-impact ratio), is likely to reduce efficiency relative to a control without such options, even in the absence of explicit norm violations. The effect is robust across cultures and group compositions. However, the contest/attack framing is not a standard PGG, so direct quantitative transfer to classic PGGs with norm-enforcing punishment should be cautious.

- source: Agranov, M., Fréchette, G., Palfrey, T., & Vespa, E. (2016). Static and dynamic underinvestment: An experimental investigation. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment finds that underprovision of the public good is substantial in both static and dynamic settings, with dynamic inefficiency (the additional inefficiency due to intertemporal linkage) increasing as the survival rate of the public good rises. When the public good is highly durable, dynamic inefficiency accounts for a large share of total inefficiency. Subjects use both minimum winning coalition (MWC) strategies and strategic cooperation, including informal punishment (exclusion from coalitions) to reward or punish past behavior. However, the game does not include a formal punishment technology (e.g., costly punishment as in standard PGGs). Welfare losses are decomposed into static and dynamic components, and the results show that dynamic inefficiency is empirically significant and increases with the strength of the dynamic link. The findings are robust across different strategy types and are consistent with theoretical predictions, though the magnitude of inefficiency is somewhat less than predicted by theory.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding how dynamic linkages and coalition bargaining affect efficiency in public good provision, especially in legislative or committee settings. However, it does not manipulate or include a formal punishment mechanism as in standard PGGs. Instead, it studies endogenous, informal punishment via coalition exclusion. Therefore, while the results are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in dynamic, coalition-based public goods environments, they are only indirectly relevant for predicting the effect of adding formal punishment to a standard PGG. The main lesson is that efficiency is sensitive to dynamic linkages and coalition rules, and that informal punishment can partially substitute for formal mechanisms, but the quantitative effect of formal punishment cannot be inferred from this study.

- source: Ahn, T. K., Loukas, B., Batsaikhan, M., Campos-Ortiz, F., Putterman, L., & Sutter, M. (2018). Trust and communication in a property rights dilemma. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing opportunities for free-form chat communication in a repeated property rights dilemma (where players can allocate tokens to production, theft, private or collective protection) nearly doubles the share of maximum efficiency gain achieved, compared to a no-communication baseline. Communication reduces theft and protection expenditures, and increases production and group earnings. The effect is robust to whether or not players can easily identify thieves, suggesting that punishment threats are not the main driver. Instead, trust, agreement on cooperative plans, and references to efficient outcomes in chat are strongly associated with higher efficiency. The effect of communication is much larger than that of institutionalized collective protection or voting. There is substantial cross-country variation, with higher trust societies benefiting more from communication.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling communication in social dilemma environments with appropriation (theft) and protection options can substantially increase efficiency, even when punishment is not easily implemented or monitored. For prediction, the presence of chat/communication is a major moderator of the efficiency effect, with communication nearly doubling the realized efficiency relative to control. The effect is robust to whether punishment is easy or hard to coordinate, suggesting that communication's main value is in building trust and agreement, not in facilitating punishment. The game is not a standard PGG, but the efficiency results are directly relevant for settings where appropriation and protection are possible. Control efficiency (no chat) is about 36.6% of the maximum possible gain; with chat, it rises to 77.2% (full info) and 70.5% (low info). Thus, for similar games, enabling chat should be expected to produce large positive effects on efficiency, especially in higher-trust populations.

- source: Ahn, T. K., Balafoutas, L., Batsaikhan, M., Campos-Ortiz, F., Putterman, L., & Sutter, M. (2016). Securing property rights: A dilemma experiment in Austria, Mexico, Mongolia, South Korea and the United States. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: This paper studies a property rights dilemma game in which subjects allocate effort among production, theft, and protection. Three institutional treatments are compared: (1) no collective protection (NCP, baseline), (2) voluntary collective protection (VCP), and (3) collective protection by majority vote (VOTE, mandatory if chosen). Efficiency, measured as group earnings relative to the social optimum, is lowest in NCP (24.6%), higher in VCP (36.6%), and highest in VOTE (48.0%). The VOTE treatment achieves the highest efficiency when the mandatory scheme is adopted, but this occurs in only 64% of opportunities. The efficiency gains from collective protection are robust across five countries, but the magnitude varies with national trust levels. The results show that institutional mechanisms for collective protection (analogous to public enforcement or taxation) can substantially increase efficiency in social dilemmas involving appropriation, but their effectiveness depends on both institutional design and cultural context.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks involving the effect of introducing a collective protection or sanctioning institution in a social dilemma with appropriation (theft), this paper provides direct evidence that such institutions increase efficiency, with mandatory (voted) collective protection yielding the largest gains. The efficiency increase from baseline to voluntary collective protection is modest but significant, while the increase to mandatory collective protection is larger but not maximal due to incomplete adoption and suboptimal implementation. The design dimensions most relevant are the presence and type of collective protection, the method of funding (voluntary vs. mandatory), and the cultural context (trust). While the game is not a standard PGG, the efficiency results are closely analogous and informative for settings where punishment or public enforcement is introduced. The findings suggest that enabling effective, enforceable collective protection (akin to punishment) is likely to increase efficiency, but the realized gains may be less than theoretical predictions due to behavioral and cultural factors.

- source: Rietz, T. A., Schniter, E., Sheremeta, R. M., & Shields, T. W. (2018). TRUST, RECIPROCITY, AND RULES. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: This paper experimentally studies the effect of exogenously imposed minimum return rules in a one-shot trust game. Four treatments are compared: no rule (0%), and minimum return rules of 10%, 20%, and 30% of the tripled investment. The main findings are: (1) Low minimum return rules (10%) reduce trust (investment), reciprocity (voluntary returns), and economic efficiency (ROI, group payoff) compared to no rule. (2) As the rule becomes more restrictive (20%, 30%), investment and efficiency recover, but trust and reciprocity do not. (3) Only the most restrictive rule (30%) restores or improves efficiency, but this is due to mechanical risk reduction, not increased trust. (4) The relationship between rule restrictiveness and efficiency is U-shaped: gentle rules are worse than no rule, but strong rules can restore efficiency. (5) The presence of rules crowds out voluntary cooperation and learning about partner trustworthiness.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong evidence that introducing weak or moderate minimum return rules in trust-based games can reduce efficiency compared to no rule, due to crowding out of trust and reciprocity. Only highly restrictive rules (that nearly eliminate risk) can restore or improve efficiency, but at the cost of voluntary cooperation. The effect is non-monotonic: efficiency is lowest at low rule levels, recovers at high rule levels. This suggests that, in adjacent trust-game-like environments, the effect of punishment-like minimum standards on efficiency depends critically on the restrictiveness of the rule. The findings are most relevant for settings where the control (no-punishment) game already achieves moderate efficiency; in such cases, adding weak punishment or minimum standards may reduce efficiency.

- source: Espín, A. M., Correa, M., & Ruiz-Villaverde, A. (2019). Patience predicts cooperative synergy: The roles of ingroup bias and reciprocity. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the mean patience of group members predicts higher group cooperative synergy in a problem-solving task with intergroup competition. Both short-term and long-term patience measures are positively associated with group efficiency. Patient individuals are more likely to make efficiency-maximizing and less likely to make competitive/spiteful choices in social preference tasks. The effect of patience on group synergy is not due to ingroup bias but is linked to reciprocal fairness. The design does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, but patient individuals are more likely to reward cooperative and punish uncooperative group members in allocation tasks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that individual differences in patience (delay discounting) are a strong moderator of group efficiency in intergroup competition settings structurally similar to repeated social dilemmas. While not a standard PGG or punishment experiment, the findings suggest that group composition (patience) can be as important as institutional design for predicting efficiency. The absence of explicit punishment/reward means the results are most relevant for baseline or control conditions, or for understanding individual-level moderators. The study does not provide direct evidence on the effect of enabling punishment, but it does show that patient individuals are more likely to engage in reciprocal (reward/punish) behaviors in allocation tasks, which may inform expectations about punishment effectiveness in similar environments.

- source: Sabin, N., & Reed-Tsochas, F. (2020). Able but Unwilling to Enforce: Cooperative Dilemmas in Group Lending. *American Journal of Sociology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The study finds that group repayment (a direct measure of economic cooperation/efficiency) is highest in groups with moderate social cohesion and no disconnected subgroups. Both very high and very low cohesion are associated with worse repayment. Disconnected subgroups further reduce repayment, and the negative effect is mitigated by higher overall cohesion. The mechanisms are traced to a trade-off between ability and willingness to punish: high cohesion increases ability but reduces willingness to enforce, while disconnected subgroups increase willingness to punish out-group members but reduce ability to do so effectively. The net effect on efficiency is determined by the lesser of ability or willingness. These results are robust to controls for financial ability, group composition, and alternative outcome measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of informal punishment mechanisms on efficiency in public-goods-like settings is highly sensitive to group social structure. In environments where punishment is informal and depends on social ties, both the ability and willingness to punish must be considered. Efficiency is maximized at moderate levels of cohesion; too much cohesion reduces willingness to punish, undermining enforcement. Disconnected subgroups are detrimental to efficiency, even if willingness to punish out-group members is high, because ability is low. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not monotonic in group connectedness and is context-dependent. The findings are most relevant for settings with informal, endogenous punishment and may not generalize to formal, externally imposed punishment mechanisms.

- source: Kas, J., Corten, R., & van de Rijt, A. (2020). Reputations in mixed-role markets: A theory and an experimental test. *Social Science Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment shows that in a repeated dyadic trust/lending game, providing information about a participant's past behavior in both roles (mixed-role reputation) increases the rate at which trust is placed and honored in the Lending Game (free exchange, where lending is costly and not compensated), but not in the Trust Game (paid exchange, where lending is compensated). Specifically, in the Lending Game, the trust rate and the 'success rate' (trust placed and honored) are significantly higher under mixed-role reputation than under single-role reputation. In the Trust Game, there is no significant difference between reputation conditions. The paper interprets these results as evidence that mixed-role reputation systems facilitate reciprocal lending and increase overall cooperation and welfare in free exchange settings.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks involving public-goods-like environments where the main intervention is a change in the information structure (e.g., adding mixed-role reputation), this paper provides evidence that such interventions can increase efficiency-related outcomes (success rate) in free exchange settings, but not in paid exchange settings. The results are most relevant for environments where cooperation is costly and not directly compensated, and where reputation can be built across multiple roles. The findings suggest that enabling richer reputation signals (beyond single-role) can improve group outcomes in these contexts. However, the paper does not directly study classic public goods games or explicit punishment mechanisms, so the transfer to PGGs with punishment should be made with caution.

- source: Smith, K. B. (2006). Representational altruism: The wary cooperator as authoritative decision maker. *American Journal of Political Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiments show that individuals acting as allocators in one-shot, anonymous dictator/ultimatum-like games often divide resources evenly or even disadvantage themselves to appear fair, supporting the 'wary cooperator' hypothesis. The presence or absence of a veto (ultimatum vs. dictator) does not significantly affect allocations; rather, information asymmetry (who values the resource more) and the framing of the allocator's role (merit vs. trust) are key. When allocators are told they are entrusted with responsibility, they are more likely to allocate less to themselves than when told they earned the role by merit. The findings suggest that internalized social preferences, not external punishment or veto threats, drive representational altruism and fairness in allocation decisions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting efficiency in public-goods-like environments with and without punishment. While it does not use a standard PGG or explicit punishment mechanism, it shows that internalized social preferences can lead to fair or altruistic outcomes even in the absence of external sanctions. The findings suggest that, in some contexts, adding external punishment or veto power may have limited additional effect on efficiency if internalized fairness norms are strong. However, context (information asymmetry, framing of authority) can shift behavior toward more or less self-interested allocations. For prediction, this implies that game design features affecting perceived fairness and social expectations may be as important as formal punishment mechanisms.

- source: Wang, X. L., Han, J. H., Li, F. H., & Cao, B. H. (2018). Both Rewards and Moral Praise Can Increase the Prosocial Decisions: Revealed in a Modified Ultimatum Game Task. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that participants are less likely to reject unfair/self-benefiting offers (i.e., more likely to act selfishly) when doing so would result in a material loss (risk condition). When economic or moral rewards are provided for norm compliance, participants are more likely to reject unfair offers and act prosocially. The effect is stronger for economic rewards than for moral rewards, and is most pronounced for extremely unfair divisions. The study does not report on group efficiency or total payoffs, focusing instead on behavioral compliance with fairness norms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. While it does not use a public goods game or analyze efficiency/payoff outcomes, it shows that the threat of material loss for norm compliance reduces prosocial behavior, and that rewards (especially economic) can restore norm compliance. The findings suggest that in environments where norm compliance is costly, punishment alone may not sustain cooperation, but rewards can help. However, because the outcome is not efficiency or group payoff, the paper's direct value for predicting efficiency under punishment is limited.

- source: Miraghaie, A. M., Pouretemad, H., Villa, A. E. P., Mazaheri, M. A., Khosrowabadi, R., & Lintas, A. (2022). Electrophysiological Markers of Fairness and Selfishness Revealed by a Combination of Dictator and Ultimatum Games. *Frontiers in Systems Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that distinct neural markers (N1, P2, MFN, LPC) are associated with behavioral profiles of fairness and selfishness in economic games. Selfish participants show neural signatures consistent with spiteful punishment (e.g., rejecting low offers in UG, resulting in zero payoffs for both), while fair participants show markers consistent with costly punishment (e.g., rejecting selfish allocations in DG). However, the paper does not report or analyze group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, and does not manipulate or compare the presence/absence of punishment as a treatment. The findings are relevant for understanding the neural basis of punishment and fairness, but do not provide direct evidence on the efficiency effects of punishment in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The games studied (DG and UG) are adjacent to PGGs but lack the group structure, repeated interaction, and explicit public goods provision. Punishment in UG is endogenous (rejection of unfair offers) and always results in zero payoff for both, but the study does not compare efficiency with and without punishment, nor does it report group-level payoff outcomes. The main value for prediction is in the mechanistic and neural understanding of individual differences in punishment behavior, not in quantitative efficiency effects.

- source: Camera, G., Casari, M., & Bigoni, M. (2013). EXPERIMENTAL MARKETS WITH FRICTIONS. *Journal of Economic Surveys*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, show_n_rounds
  findings: The new experiment shows that allowing free-form chat among randomly matched strangers in an indefinitely repeated prisoner's dilemma leads to much higher cooperation rates than either no communication or structured, anonymous public messages. Structured communication does not significantly improve cooperation or coordination relative to no communication, likely due to the prevalence of deceptive use of messages. The study finds that public messages are informative about intentions, but deception dilutes their effectiveness. The experiment does not include a punishment treatment, but the literature review summarizes prior findings that peer punishment can increase cooperation and sometimes efficiency, especially in small groups. The main outcomes reported are behavioral (cooperation rates, communication patterns), not payoff-based (efficiency or group earnings).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. The experimental design is an indefinitely repeated, random-matching prisoner's dilemma among strangers, which is adjacent to public goods games. The main manipulated dimension is communication: free-form chat vs. structured messages vs. no communication. The findings suggest that free-form communication can strongly increase cooperation, but structured, anonymous communication does not. The experiment does not manipulate or report on punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The literature review section summarizes prior work showing that peer punishment can increase cooperation and sometimes efficiency, but these are not tested in the new experiment. Thus, this paper is most informative about the effect of communication (chat) and monitoring (public/anonymous) on cooperation, and only indirectly about punishment or efficiency. It should be used as contextual or indirect evidence for prediction, especially regarding the role of communication and monitoring, but not as direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Vandermeer, J., Hosey, C., Epley, N., & Keysar, B. (2019). Escalation of negative social exchange: Reflexive punishment or deliberative deterrence?. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that negative escalation in repeated taking/giving games is driven by reflexive punishment rather than strategic deterrence. Deliberation reduces escalation, while reflexive or high cognitive load conditions increase it. Escalation occurs only in response to perceived negative actions (taking), not positive ones (giving). Explicitly instructing participants to focus on future consequences or to deter does not increase escalation; only a goal to punish does. The findings are robust across one-shot and repeated interactions, and across different manipulations of deliberation and cognitive resources. However, the study does not use public goods games, does not implement a punishment stage, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The experimental paradigm is a repeated dictator/taking game, not a public goods game, and the main outcomes are escalation and behavioral responses to harm, not group efficiency or payoff. There is no punishment stage where players can pay to reduce others' payoffs, and no analysis of group welfare or efficiency. The findings may be relevant for understanding the psychological drivers of punitive escalation, but do not inform the quantitative effect of punishment mechanisms on efficiency in PGG-like games.

- source: Wang, W. J., Xu, Z. H., Hua, S. J., Cui, L. Q., Zhang, J. L., & Meng, F. Y. (2024). Threshold-initiated spatial public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that intergroup competition significantly increases the rate of volunteering in the volunteer's dilemma, even when there is no additional monetary incentive or change in the payoff structure. Risk (a 50% chance of public good failure) reduces volunteering rates, but intergroup competition mitigates this negative effect. Gender and risk aversion moderate these effects: females volunteer more in no-risk settings, males increase volunteering more under risky intergroup competition. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, but notes in the discussion that over-volunteering (stimulated by intergroup competition) is socially inefficient.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. Its findings are about volunteering rates in a volunteer's dilemma, which is structurally adjacent to but not the same as a standard public goods game. The results suggest that intergroup competition increases pro-social action rates, especially under risk, but also that this can lead to inefficiency through over-volunteering. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the main takeaway is that mechanisms increasing pro-social action (like competition) may not always improve efficiency, especially in anti-coordination settings like the volunteer's dilemma. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the paper's predictive value for the target task.

- source: Mill, W., & Morgan, J. (2022). The cost of a divided America: an experimental study into destructive behavior. *Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that both Clinton and Trump voters are willing to destroy the payoff of another participant, but the probability of doing so is significantly higher when the other is an outgroup member (opposing voter). This effect is mainly driven by Clinton voters, who increase their probability of spiteful behavior by 34% when facing an outgroup member. Trump voters are generally more spiteful overall, but do not differentiate between ingroup and outgroup. Attitudinal measures (social distance, feeling thermometer) are strongly correlated with spiteful behavior. The study does not use a public goods game or measure group efficiency, but rather focuses on direct, antisocial destruction of another’s payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The design is a one-shot, two-player allocation task with the option to destroy the other’s payoff at no benefit to self (pure spite), not a repeated public goods game with punishment. There is no group production, no opportunity for cooperation, and no efficiency or welfare outcome reported. The findings are relevant for understanding the prevalence and moderators of destructive behavior (spite) in polarized contexts, but do not inform predictions about the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The closest relevance is to the literature on antisocial punishment or joy-of-destruction, but the mapping to PGG-like efficiency outcomes is weak.

- source: Müller-Leinss, J. M., Enzi, B., Flasbeck, V., & Brüne, M. (2018). Retaliation or selfishness? An rTMS investigation of the role of the dorsolateral prefrontal cortex in prosocial motives. *Social Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: Inhibiting the right DLPFC via rTMS increases both retaliation against previously unfair opponents and selfish behavior (less fairness) towards previously fair opponents in a Dictator Game following an Ultimatum Game. The manipulation did not affect rejection rates in the UG. The findings suggest the right DLPFC is involved in controlling selfish motives, but the design does not distinguish between retaliation and pure selfishness. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and is not a public goods game or direct punishment paradigm.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides limited direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The experimental design is adjacent: it uses sequential UG and DG with role reversal and rTMS manipulation, focusing on individual-level retaliation/selfishness rather than group-level cooperation or efficiency. There is no manipulation of punishment institutions, costs, or group payoff structure as in PGGs. The main outcomes are behavioral (fair/unfair offers), not efficiency or payoff. Thus, the paper is not directly informative for the prediction task, except as context for the neural basis of retaliation/selfishness in economic games.

- source: Hilbig, B. E., & Thielmann, I. (2025). Toward a (more) parsimonious account of the link between 'dark' personality and social decision-making in economic games. *Judgment and Decision Making*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Across 10 large-sample, hypothetical studies using eight different economic games (including the Prisoner's Dilemma, Dictator Game, Ultimatum Game, Faith Game, Chicken Game, Stag Hunt, and Uncostly Retaliation Game), the common core of dark personality traits (D) consistently predicts antisocial choices (i.e., lower prosociality). Single dark tetrad traits also predict antisocial choices, but these effects are almost entirely accounted for by D. The study does not manipulate or analyze punishment, reward, or efficiency outcomes, and does not include public goods games with or without punishment. The main contribution is to clarify the role of personality in prosocial/antisocial decision-making, not to inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. It does not include any games with a punishment or sanctioning phase, nor does it report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare outcomes. The findings are about individual differences in prosocial choice, not about institutional or design features such as punishment, reward, or their impact on efficiency. Therefore, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Tanaka, H., Ohtsuki, H., & Ohtsubo, Y. (2016). The price of being seen to be just: an intention signalling strategy for indirect reciprocity. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that participants in a dyadic donation game use a costly signalling option (abandoning a resource) to manage their reputation, especially after justified defection or implementation error. Others interpret this signal as a sign of cooperative intent and are more likely to cooperate with signallers. The signalling option allows players to maintain a good reputation without requiring second-order information. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, but suggests that such signalling can stabilize cooperation in indirect reciprocity settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is only indirectly relevant to predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The experimental design is a dyadic donation game, not a multi-player PGG, and the 'punishment' is a costly self-imposed signal (abandoning a resource) rather than a punishment directed at others. The main outcomes are behavioral (signalling and cooperation rates), not efficiency or payoff. However, the findings suggest that providing players with a costly signalling option can help maintain cooperation in the absence of second-order information, which may be relevant for understanding mechanisms that stabilize cooperation in related environments. The results do not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games.

- source: Ma, L. K., Tunney, R. J., & Ferguson, E. (2014). Gratefully Received, Gratefully Repaid: The Role of Perceived Fairness in Cooperative Interactions. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that recipients of unconditional (free) help perceive the donor as more genuinely helpful, feel more gratitude, and report a higher tendency to reciprocate in the future compared to recipients of conditional (repayment-required) help. Among those who received conditional offers and accepted, 38% defaulted (either partially or fully) on repayment, suggesting a behavioral response to perceived unfairness. The effect of offer conditionality on willingness to reciprocate is serially mediated by perceived helpfulness and gratitude. The study does not report on group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, and the game is a one-shot, dyadic trust game rather than a repeated public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how conditionality (akin to punishment or sanctions) affects willingness to reciprocate and actual repayment behavior in a one-shot, dyadic trust game. It does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The findings suggest that perceived fairness and the framing of help (conditional vs. unconditional) can influence cooperation and defaulting, which may be relevant for understanding psychological mechanisms in cooperation, but the results cannot be directly used to predict efficiency changes in public goods games with punishment enabled or disabled.

- source: Kandori, M., & Obayashi, S. (2014). Labor union members play an OLG repeated game. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that cooperation (helping behavior) among union members is sustained not by explicit punishment, perfect monitoring, or reputation, but by a focal-point equilibrium where members help others in expectation of reciprocal help when they need it. Most members leave the union after their own disputes are resolved, indicating that helping is conditional and self-interested rather than purely altruistic. The equilibrium is supported by direct observation and mutual monitoring during joint activities, but not by formal sanctions or payoff-based incentives. The paper provides qualitative evidence and a theoretical model, but does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar laboratory environments. It provides qualitative, contextual evidence about how cooperation can be sustained in a real-world OLG setting with limited observability, but does not report or manipulate efficiency, payoff, or formal punishment mechanisms. The findings may be useful for understanding the range of mechanisms that can support cooperation in loosely knit groups, but do not provide quantitative or experimental evidence relevant to the prediction task.

- source: Lyle, H. F. (2017). Volver a Nuestras Raices: The Reemergence and Adaptation of Traditional Forms of Andean Reciprocity. *Journal of Latin American and Caribbean Anthropology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in the absence of formal or institutionalized punishment, collective action in the community is maintained through a combination of symbolic capital (reputation for generosity, skill, and reliability) and indirect social exclusion (withholding support from free riders). Households that invest more in collective action have larger and more robust support networks, suggesting positive assortment among prosocial individuals. Free riding is tolerated to some extent, and there is no evidence of direct punishment or sanctions. The system is sustained by the interplay of modified traditional reciprocity practices (ayni and minga), which have adapted to local needs. There is no direct measurement of efficiency or group payoff, but the system appears stable and resilient.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments, as there is no experimental manipulation of punishment, no formal sanctions, and no measurement of efficiency or group payoff. However, it does suggest that in real-world settings, cooperation can be maintained without formal punishment through social mechanisms like reputation and indirect exclusion. For prediction tasks, this paper is most useful as contextual evidence that alternative mechanisms (symbolic capital, social networks) can substitute for formal punishment, but it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on the efficiency effects of punishment interventions.

- source: Eisenbarth, S., Graham, L., & Rigterink, A. S. (2021). Can Reminders of Rules Induce Compliance? Experimental Evidence from a Common Pool Resource Setting. *Environmental & Resource Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The SMS reminder intervention increased self-reported knowledge of forest use rules and the perceived probability of sanctions for rule-breaking, but did not increase actual scrutiny or sanctioning of others. There was no significant effect on compliance with forest use rules or on forest use (measured both by self-report and satellite data). The intervention did not systematically reduce resource extraction or improve compliance, and thus did not provide evidence that reminders of rules (and increased perceived sanctions) improve efficiency or group welfare in this common pool resource context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment mechanisms on efficiency in public goods or common pool resource games, as it does not manipulate or directly measure actual punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The intervention only increased perceived probability of sanctions, not actual sanctioning or compliance, and did not reduce resource use. Thus, the findings suggest that increasing perceived sanctions via reminders alone may not be sufficient to improve efficiency or reduce overuse in common pool resource settings. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that behavioral nudges increasing perceived sanctions (without changing actual punishment mechanisms or costs) are unlikely to have strong effects on efficiency or group payoff.

- source: Montoya, L. A., Montoya, I., & González, O. D. S. (2015). Lessons from collaborative governance and sociobiology theories for reinforcing sustained cooperation: a government food security case study. *Public Health*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that sustained interagency cooperation in public policy (here, food security in Bogotá) depends on (1) strong sponsorship (e.g., from central government or consensus), (2) stability of staff in the coordination system, and (3) mechanisms to reward or punish collaboration levels. The absence of these factors leads to decreased collaboration. The paper draws analogies from sociobiology (e.g., reciprocal altruism, punishment, reward) and collaborative governance theory, suggesting that similar mechanisms are relevant in human institutional settings. However, the findings are qualitative and do not include quantitative measures of efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It is adjacent to the prediction task, as it discusses the importance of reward and punishment mechanisms for sustaining cooperation in real-world, multi-agent institutional settings, and draws on sociobiological theory. However, it does not manipulate or measure game design dimensions, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its main value is in supporting the general idea that mechanisms for reward and punishment can help sustain cooperation, especially when other supports (like sponsorship or staff stability) are present. It does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence relevant to the prediction task.

- source: Eriksson, K., Simpson, B., & Vartanova, I. (2021). The Power of Tolerance vs. Unselfishness as a Cultural Determinant of Cooperation. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: Societies that place a higher value on tolerance (as measured by survey responses) exhibit higher average cooperation in a continuous PD, stronger norms against non-cooperation in a CPR scenario, and metanorms favoring gossip over physical confrontation. Societal valuation of unselfishness does not predict these outcomes. The effect of tolerance is robust to controlling for individual-level values. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment mechanisms in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its main contribution is to show that societal-level values (especially tolerance) predict cross-societal variation in cooperation and related norms/metanorms. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is only weakly informative, as it does not address how game design dimensions or punishment affect efficiency.

- source: Kümmerli, R. (2011). A Test of Evolutionary Policing Theory with Data from Human Societies. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that (1) higher similarity among citizens is associated with lower crime rates (interpreted as higher cooperation), (2) lower similarity is associated with higher policing effort, and (3) increases in policing effort over time are associated with reductions in crime rates. These findings support evolutionary policing theory in a real-world context. However, the outcomes are not directly efficiency or payoff-based, but rather use crime rate as a behavioral proxy for defection/cooperation. The study does not use a public goods game or experimental design, and does not report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence that increased punishment (policing) can reduce defection (crime) in real-world settings, especially in less homogeneous populations. However, because it does not use a public goods game, does not manipulate punishment experimentally, and does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, it is of limited direct use for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. Its main value is in supporting the general plausibility of punishment reducing defection, but it does not inform the quantitative relationship between game design dimensions and efficiency.

- source: Johansson, L. O., Gustafsson, M., Falkemark, G., Gärling, T., & Johansson-Stenman, O. (2003). Goal conflicts in political decisionmaking:: a survey of municipality politicians' views of road pricing. *Environment and Planning C-government and Policy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The main finding is that municipality politicians experience significant goal conflicts when considering road pricing: while environmental benefits are recognized, fairness (especially for low-income citizens) and financial goals often take precedence. This is especially true for Social Democrats, Conservatives, Liberals, and Christian Democrats, who represent the majority. Only Left and Green Party politicians consistently prioritize environmental goals. The paper discusses how these conflicts may prevent road pricing from achieving its intended environmental effects, and suggests that policy packages or compensatory measures may be needed to increase acceptability. However, no behavioral or payoff-based outcomes are measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It is relevant in that it discusses the use of economic disincentives (road pricing) as a structural solution to social dilemmas, and highlights the importance of fairness and acceptability in the implementation of such measures. However, it does not report any experimental or observational data on efficiency, group payoff, or the behavioral effects of punishment. Its main value is in contextualizing the political and social obstacles to implementing sanction-based solutions in real-world collective action problems.

- source: Kamei, K., & Putterman, L. (2018). REPUTATION TRANSMISSION WITHOUT BENEFIT TO THE REPORTER: A BEHAVIORAL UNDERPINNING OF MARKETS IN EXPERIMENTAL FOCUS. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that a substantial fraction of cooperators are willing to incur a cost to report defectors, even when there is no direct material benefit. Reporting is much more common when it is free, but remains significant at positive costs. Reporting is overwhelmingly targeted at defectors by cooperators, with little reporting in other pairings. The frequency of cooperation is relatively stable across treatments (about 50-60%), and beliefs about others' cooperation and reporting are correlated with own behavior. The results are consistent with models of social preferences (inequity aversion) and with emotional motivations (anger, obligation). However, the study does not measure or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the motivational underpinnings of costly punishment-like behaviors (here, reporting) in social dilemma settings, but does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their close variants. The design is adjacent to PGGs with punishment, as it studies a two-player prisoners' dilemma with a costly reporting option that can affect a partner's reputation and future interactions. The findings suggest that costly punishment/reporting is more likely when costs are low and when social/emotional motivations are present, but do not allow direct prediction of efficiency changes from enabling punishment in a PGG. The results may inform priors about the likelihood of costly punishment being used, but not about its effect on group efficiency.

- source: Ma, N., Li, N., He, X. S., Sun, D. L., Zhang, X. C., & Zhang, D. R. (2012). Rejection of Unfair Offers Can Be Driven by Negative Emotions, Evidence from Modified Ultimatum Games with Anonymity. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that rejection rates of unfair offers are significantly above zero even when anonymity is strictly enforced, suggesting that negative emotions alone can drive costly punishment-like behavior. Rejection rates are higher in public (experimenter-observed) or 'informed' conditions, indicating that reputational concerns and cognitive processing of consequences further increase rejection. However, the study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and the game is a modified ultimatum game rather than a public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its main contribution is to clarify the psychological motivations for costly punishment (rejection) in ultimatum-like games, distinguishing between emotional and reputational drivers. It does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so it cannot be used to calibrate the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like settings. It may be useful for understanding the psychological underpinnings of punishment behavior, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Testori, M., Cruz, T. D. D., & Beersma, B. (2024). Punishing or praising gossipers: How people interpret the motives driving negative gossip shapes its consequences. *Social and Personality Psychology Compass*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
