  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that receivers' interpretation of gossip motives (pro-social vs. pro-self) significantly shapes their trust in both the sender and the target of gossip. Correctly interpreting pro-social motives leads to higher trust in the sender, while pro-self motives reduce trust. The ability to overcome intuition (measured by CRT) increases the likelihood of correctly identifying pro-social motives. However, the study does not use a public goods game or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare. The behavioral outcome is the amount invested in a trust game vignette, which is not linked to real payoffs or group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The experimental design is adjacent to PGGs, using a trust game vignette, and focuses on trust and motive interpretation rather than payoff or efficiency. While the findings may inform understanding of how social information and its interpretation affect trust and possibly cooperation, they do not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence about efficiency or payoff outcomes under punishment or sanction conditions. Thus, this paper should not be weighted heavily for the downstream prediction task focused on efficiency.

- source: Ohnuma, S., Hirose, Y., Karasawa, K., Yorifuji, K., & Sugiura, J. (2005). Why do residents accept a demanding rule?: Fairness and social benefit as determinants of approval of a recycling system. *Japanese Psychological Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that citizens' approval of a demanding, sanction-free recycling system is primarily determined by their perception of social benefit and procedural fairness. Preference for introducing strict rules (penalties, surveillance) is mainly driven by perceived outcome unfairness (i.e., the sense that some people are not complying and are not penalized). The system succeeded in reducing waste, but the paper does not analyze efficiency or group payoff in a way directly comparable to public goods games. The findings suggest that emphasizing social benefit and procedural fairness can increase approval and cooperation even in the absence of incentives or sanctions, and that perceived unfairness can drive support for introducing punitive measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence for the prediction task. It suggests that in real-world social dilemma settings, approval and willingness to cooperate with demanding rules can be high even without punishment, if social benefit and procedural fairness are perceived as high. Preference for punishment or sanctions arises mainly from perceived unfairness in outcomes. However, because the study does not measure efficiency or group payoff, and does not manipulate or observe actual punishment regimes, it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its main value is in highlighting the psychological determinants of support for punishment and cooperation in adjacent, real-world collective action problems.

- source: Hart, E., Mellers, B. A., & Bicchieri, C. (2019). Bad luck or bad intentions: When do third parties reveal offenders' intentions to victims?. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Third party observers are more likely to inform victims when harm is accidental than when it is intentional. Moral anger increases the likelihood of informing about intentional harm, while empathy increases the likelihood of informing in both cases. Observers are more likely to inform when they believe the victim's beliefs about the offender's intent are inaccurate, and when the information has consequences for future interactions (e.g., potential for punishment). However, the paper does not study or report on group efficiency, total payoff, or the effects of punishment mechanisms as in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods game environments. The design is adjacent in that it uses economic games and discusses punishment and fairness, but the dependent variables are about information sharing, not payoffs or efficiency. Thus, it should not be used to inform quantitative predictions about efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like settings.

- source: Kim, M., & Sivan, E. W. Z. (2017). Faking It Is Hard to Do: Entrepreneurial Norm Enforcement and Suspicions of Deviance. *Sociological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that when norm enforcement is mandated (i.e., actors are required to respond to deviance), enforcers are perceived as more sincere and less likely to be suspected of ulterior motives, even in high-accusability contexts. In contrast, when enforcement is entrepreneurial (i.e., voluntary and not mandated) and the context is high accusability (where actors have reason to fear being accused of deviance), enforcers are viewed with greater suspicion and are more likely to be seen as having ulterior motives. In low-accusability contexts, entrepreneurial enforcement does not arouse suspicion. The study does not measure or report on efficiency, group payoff, or any economic outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or similar economic games. Its findings are about social perceptions of norm enforcers, not about behavioral or payoff consequences of punishment. The design is adjacent to PGG-like environments in that it involves norm enforcement and public goods logic, but it is not an economic game and does not manipulate or measure efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGGs, but may be useful for understanding psychological or reputational moderators of norm enforcement.

- source: Le Maux, B., & Necker, S. (2023). Honesty nudges: Effect varies with content but not with timing. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Honesty nudges, especially those that mention the possibility of monitoring (deterrence), significantly reduce cheating in a repeated individual reporting task. The effect of nudges is similar whether implemented before or after some decisions have been made. Adding individualized feedback does not further reduce cheating beyond the deterrence message. The reduction in dishonesty is larger among those who were previously dishonest. However, the study does not analyze group efficiency, welfare, or total payoff, and the game is not interactive or public-goods-like.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. Its findings are only indirectly relevant: deterrence messages (even without real sanctions) can reduce dishonest behavior in individual settings, and the effect is robust to timing. However, because the design is not a public goods game, there is no group interaction, and no efficiency or group payoff is measured, the results should be used with caution for downstream prediction tasks focused on efficiency in PGG-like environments.

- source: Lin, C. A., & Bates, T. C. (2022). Free to choose: Mutualist motives for partner choice, proportional division, punishment, and help. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that mutualism is a reliable, stable, and discriminant psychological construct, with four components: partner choice, proportional sharing, proportional punishment, and proportional helping. Higher mutualism scores predict attitudinal opposition to equal sharing and redistribution, and preference for proportional division in hypothetical scenarios. The mutualism scale is largely independent of compassion, envy, self-interest, and utilitarianism. The findings are robust across two large samples. However, all outcomes are attitudinal or psychometric; there is no evidence on actual behavior in public goods games, punishment games, or on efficiency/welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It may be useful for understanding individual differences in motives that could influence behavior in such games, but it does not report any behavioral or payoff-based outcomes, nor does it manipulate or observe game design dimensions. Thus, it should not be used as primary evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment interventions. Its main value is in providing a validated measure of mutualist motives, which could be used in future studies that do measure relevant outcomes.

- source: Bravo, G. (2010). Voluntary contribution to public goods in mutual-help forums: reciprocity or group attachment?. *Socio-economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that the success of the mutual-help forum depends on the complementary roles of a small core of altruistic contributors and a larger group of reciprocators. Reciprocity is a significant motivator for most users, while the core group is driven by intrinsic motivation and group attachment. The forum achieves high performance in terms of questions answered and response speed, but this is not analyzed in terms of efficiency or group payoff. There is no evidence of formal or informal punishment mechanisms affecting contribution or efficiency. The findings suggest that, in the absence of sanctioning, a privileged core group can sustain cooperation and public good provision in online communities.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides limited direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it studies a real-world mutual-help forum without any explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanism. The findings are relevant for understanding how cooperation can be sustained without punishment, especially through reciprocity and intrinsic motivation. However, the absence of efficiency or payoff outcomes, and the lack of any punishment treatment, mean that this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency. It may be useful as contextual evidence for baseline cooperation in the absence of sanctions.

  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The main findings are that male sociable weavers contribute significantly more to cooperative nest construction than females, and that this difference is not explained by age or nest movement. Individual cooperative behavior is consistent over time. The study does not test or report on punishment, sanctions, or group efficiency/welfare. The discussion considers possible evolutionary mechanisms (kin selection, group augmentation, sexual selection, punishment) but provides no empirical test of these mechanisms.

While it discusses public goods and mentions punishment as a theoretical mechanism, it does not analyze or report on punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes. Thus, it offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task, except as a contextual example of natural public goods maintenance and the possible irrelevance of punishment in some real-world systems.

- source: Bernhard, R. M., & Cushman, F. (2022). Extortion, intuition, and the dark side of reciprocity. *Cognition*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment finds that both communication and subsidies for NCH can shift groups toward more cooperative, higher-efficiency outcomes, but also that some groups coordinate on Nash equilibria (heavy spraying) that are also efficient in the game’s payoff structure. Group-level characteristics are more predictive of cooperative behavior than individual traits. The game does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, so no evidence is provided about the effects of punishment on efficiency or cooperation. The findings are most relevant for understanding the effects of communication and positive incentives (subsidies) on coordination and efficiency in public-goods-like environments, but not for punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the experimental design. It does provide informative evidence about the effects of communication and subsidies, and about the importance of group-level characteristics for coordination and efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly informative. For tasks involving communication or positive incentives, the findings are more relevant.

- source: Liu, G. F., Lin, C. D., & Xin, Z. Q. (2014). The Effects of Within- and Between-Group Competition on Trust and Trustworthiness among Acquaintances. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that both within-group and between-group competition increase trust (investment) among acquaintances in an investment game. However, within-group competition reduces trustworthiness (return ratio) and leads to lower payoffs for trustors but higher payoffs for trustees. Between-group competition increases trustee payoffs but does not significantly affect trustworthiness or trustor payoffs compared to the non-competition condition. The results suggest that competition type differentially affects efficiency for trustors and trustees, and that within-group competition can undermine overall group efficiency by harming trustor payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in the absence of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, introducing within-group competition can increase trust but reduce trustworthiness, leading to lower payoffs for trustors and higher payoffs for trustees. Between-group competition increases trust and trustee payoffs but does not harm trustor payoffs. Since the study does not include punishment, it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does show that competitive structures can have asymmetric effects on efficiency depending on the role. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that the presence or absence of competition (and its type) can moderate efficiency outcomes, but the absence of punishment means the results are not directly transferable to punishment-enabled PGGs.

- source: Andreoni, J., Kuhn, M. A., & Samuelson, L. (2019). Building rational cooperation on their own: Learning to start small. *Journal of Public Economic Theory*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that in a twice-played prisoners' dilemma, allowing players to endogenously choose the allocation of stakes between rounds leads to higher joint payoffs, especially as players learn to 'start small' (allocate more to the second round). The payoff-maximizing allocation is similar whether chosen endogenously or exogenously, but endogenous choice yields even higher payoffs, likely due to signaling effects. There is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the design, and the results are not directly about public goods games but are adjacent in their relevance to cooperation and efficiency in social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as no punishment or sanctioning is present. However, it is highly informative about how endogenous structuring of game parameters (here, stake allocation) can enhance efficiency and cooperation, especially via signaling. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly applicable, but it does suggest that endogenous institution formation can yield higher efficiency than exogenous imposition, a principle that may generalize to other game design features.

- source: DeAngelo, G., Ferrell, P., & McCannon, B. (2017). Sources of deviant behavior: Contrasting alternative explanations in the laboratory. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that individuals who experience deteriorating social interactions (declining trust) in a repeated Trust Game are more likely to extract more from a common resource in a subsequent game, reducing group efficiency. Similarly, individuals' expectations about the typical behavior of others (empirical social norms) strongly predict their own extraction behavior. The study does not include any punishment or sanction mechanism, and thus does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or related outcomes. Instead, it suggests that interventions targeting social history or social norms may be more effective than sanctions in reducing deviant behavior and improving group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment, as no punishment or sanction mechanism is present in the experimental design. However, it provides strong evidence that social history and social norms are important determinants of efficiency in common resource dilemmas. For prediction tasks focused on punishment effects, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may be useful for understanding baseline efficiency in the absence of punishment and for considering alternative interventions.

- source: Fornwagner, H., & Hauser, O. P. (2022). Climate Action for (My) Children. *Environmental & Resource Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that parents invest, on average, over 80% of their endowment in VCA (planting trees), with the highest investment when observed by their own child. The effect of being observed by one's own child is significant among parents with a high school diploma and those with strong climate change beliefs. No significant effect is found for being observed by a stranger adult or child. The study does not include any punishment, sanction, or reward mechanism. The outcome is a direct measure of efficiency/welfare (how much of the endowment is invested in the public good). The design is adjacent to a public goods game but lacks group interaction, punishment, or reward features.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions in public goods games or their variants. It is relevant for understanding how observability and social context (especially intergenerational observability) affect voluntary contributions to a public good, but it does not inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is adjacent to a PGG (single-player, no group interaction, no punishment/reward). Thus, it should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments.

- source: Salazar, M., Shaw, D. J., Czekóová, K., Stanek, R., & Brázdil, M. (2022). The role of generalised reciprocity and reciprocal tendencies in the emergence of cooperative group norms*. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that, in a repeated anonymous bargaining game with no punishment or reputation, cooperative (equal) demands become more common over time in many groups, indicating the emergence of cooperative-like group norms via generalised reciprocity. However, there is substantial heterogeneity between groups, driven by the mix of individual reciprocal tendencies. Some groups converge to egalitarian outcomes, while others remain uncooperative. The presence of more 'Constant' or 'Conditional' cooperators in a group leads to more cooperative outcomes. The paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and does not directly analyze group efficiency as a ratio of actual to maximum possible payoff, though it does report mean payoffs for different demand types.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games, as there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the design. However, it provides informative context about how group composition and reciprocal tendencies affect the emergence of cooperation in repeated anonymous interactions. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly informative, but it does suggest that, even without punishment, some groups can achieve high cooperation depending on their composition. The findings may be useful for understanding baseline cooperation in the absence of punishment, but not for predicting the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Ibbotson, P., Jimenez-Romero, C., & Page, K. M. (2022). Dying to cooperate: the role of environmental harshness in human collaboration. *Behavioral Ecology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that environmental harshness is a key moderator of cooperative behavior in coordination games like the Stag Hunt. In both models and human experiments, individuals are more likely to choose cooperative, high-risk strategies when the environment is harsh and survival is threatened. Communication between agents (or players) allows for better coordination and increases the viability of cooperation. The study does not include punishment, reward, or sanction mechanisms, and does not use a public goods game structure, but rather a coordination game with binary choices and pairwise matching. The main outcomes are behavioral (strategy choice and switching), with survival probability and energy reserves as related but not directly group efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It does, however, provide informative context about how environmental harshness and communication affect the viability of cooperation in coordination games. Since punishment and reward are not present, and the game is not a PGG or close variant, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments. It may be useful for understanding the role of environmental context and communication in cooperation, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment.

- source: Nockur, L., & Pfattheicher, S. (2021). The Beautiful Complexity of Human Prosociality: On the Interplay of Honesty-Humility, Intuition, and a Reward System. *Social Psychological and Personality Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that introducing a reward system (the possibility of being rewarded by a third party) increases the likelihood and amount of giving in a dictator game. Honesty-Humility is also positively associated with giving. Intuitive decision making does not have a main effect, but among individuals low in Honesty-Humility, intuition reduces giving when no reward system is present. The reward system effect is robust across personality and decision-making style. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not include punishment or sanction mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. It does provide evidence that a reward system (not punishment) increases prosocial behavior in a dictator game, but the outcome is individual giving, not group efficiency. The design is not a PGG or close variant, and there is no analysis of efficiency or related payoff outcomes. Thus, its value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments is very limited.

- source: Horiuchi, S. (2015). Emergence and Collapse of the Norm of Resource Sharing Around Locally Abundant Resources. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that workers' effort (reciprocity) increases with their own wage (direct reciprocity) and, when their own wage is unobservable, also with the coworker's wage (indirect reciprocity via signaling). When both wages are observable, the coworker's wage has no effect on effort, indicating that indirect reciprocity is driven by signaling about one's own treatment, not by altruism toward the coworker. There is no evidence that inequality aversion explains the observed patterns. The main outcome is effort, not efficiency or group payoff. No punishment or sanctioning is present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It is adjacent in that it studies reciprocity and information in a gift-exchange game, but the main outcome is effort, not efficiency, and there is no punishment mechanism. The findings may inform predictions about the role of information and signaling in reciprocity, but not about punishment or efficiency. Thus, its value for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency is limited.

- source: Haucap, J., Heldman, C., & Rau, H. A. (2024). Gender and cooperation in the presence of negative externalities. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that the presence of a negative externality (mutual cooperation harms a passive third party) reduces cooperation rates, especially among women. Women are less likely to cooperate when their cooperation harms others, and this is linked to higher guilt/shame aversion. Social information about the partner (gender, age, semesters studied) does not affect women's cooperation rates but reduces cooperation among men, especially when matched with a woman. There is no evidence of punishment, reward, or sanction mechanisms. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, focusing instead on cooperation rates and psychological correlates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. It is adjacent to the target because it studies a prisoner's dilemma with a negative externality, but there is no punishment or sanction mechanism, and no efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported. The findings may inform expectations about gender differences in cooperation when externalities are present, but do not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is all-or-nothing, one-shot, with no communication, and no feedback beyond social information. Thus, its value for the downstream prediction task is limited to contextual insights about cooperation under negative externalities and gender, not about punishment or efficiency.

- source: Ma, S. M., Zhang, B. Y., Cao, S. N., Liu, J. S., & Wang, W. X. (2021). Limited memory optimizes cooperation in social dilemma experiments. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that providing players with memory of their own and their partner's actions from previous rounds (memory length L) in a repeated, randomized-pair Prisoner's Dilemma leads to a non-monotonic effect on cooperation: cooperation rates are lowest with no memory (L=0), increase sharply with minimal memory (L=1), and peak at intermediate memory (L=2), then decline with longer memory (L=3,4). The behavioral mechanism is memory-based conditional cooperation, where most subjects cooperate if their partner's recent cooperation rate is high. Theoretical models (replicator dynamics and Markov processes) fit the observed cooperation rates well. No punishment, reward, or efficiency outcomes are studied.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. It is relevant for understanding how memory and information about past actions affect cooperation rates in repeated dyadic social dilemmas, but it does not address group payoff, efficiency, or the impact of punishment or reward mechanisms. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that memory length (information about past actions) can have a non-monotonic effect on cooperation rates, but provides no evidence about efficiency or about punishment effects. It is not directly informative for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in public-goods-like environments.

- source: Schitter, C., Fleiss, J., & Palan, S. (2019). To claim or not to claim: Anonymity, symmetric externalities and honesty. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment finds that removing anonymity (making claims public to other group members) significantly reduces dishonest overclaiming, while adding symmetric externalities (where claims reduce the pool for others) does not significantly affect honesty. There is no evidence that the combination of both mechanisms has a stronger effect than removing anonymity alone. The presence of symmetric externalities does not increase honesty, possibly because fear of being exploited by others crowds out pro-social motives. The study does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The findings are robust to controls and alternative model specifications.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. It does, however, provide evidence that increasing transparency (removing anonymity) can reduce dishonest behavior in environments where individuals can make unverifiable claims that affect others. The presence of symmetric externalities alone does not increase honesty. For prediction tasks focused on punishment effects or efficiency, this paper is only weakly relevant, as it does not manipulate or measure those variables.

- source: Lamba, S., & Mace, R. (2013). The evolution of fairness: explaining variation in bargaining behaviour. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in one-shot, anonymous ultimatum games, proposer offers vary significantly across villages, but responder behavior is highly uniform: almost all offers are accepted, and responders' self-reported minimum acceptable offers are very low. There is no evidence for cultural conformity or coordinated expectations at the ethno-linguistic group level. The findings suggest that local environmental factors, rather than group-level fairness norms, may drive variation in bargaining behavior. The study does not include punishment or analyze efficiency, welfare, or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not include punishment or sanctions, and does not analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings about variation in bargaining behavior and the lack of coordinated expectations in the ultimatum game may be contextually informative for understanding heterogeneity in social preferences, but do not inform the prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Powers, S. T., Ekárt, A., & Lewis, P. R. (2018). Modelling enduring institutions: The complementarity of evolutionary and agent-based approaches. *Cognitive Systems Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that participants' choices in one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma games are influenced by information about the other player's action and by individual personality traits. The disjunction effect (greater cooperation under uncertainty) is present but weaker than in previous studies and is not explained by a general bias to cooperate. Higher potential payoffs (high variability matrices) reduce the disjunction effect, suggesting more optimal responding when incentives are larger. Reward Responsiveness predicts more optimal (defecting) choices when the optimal strategy is to defect and the other player's action is known. Agreeableness predicts more cooperative choices when the optimal strategy is to cooperate. No evidence is found for a general bias toward cooperation, and the results are similar whether the task is framed as a social dilemma or a neutral card game. The study does not include punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not include punishment or sanctions, nor does it analyze efficiency, group payoff, or related payoff-based outcomes. Its findings on the influence of payoff variability and personality on cooperation/defection may be contextually informative for understanding individual decision-making in social dilemmas, but it does not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects or efficiency.

- source: Du, J., Thomas, M. G., Bårdsen, B. J., Mace, R., & Næss, M. W. (2019). Comparison of social complexity in two independent pastoralist societies. *Behavioral Ecology and Sociobiology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that both kinship and herding group membership predict gift-giving among pastoralists, with some cross-cultural differences: Saami herders in Norway emphasize reciprocity and group membership, while Tibetan herders in China emphasize kinship and giving to poorer herders. Gifts are more likely to be given within herding groups and to close kin, but the relative importance of these factors varies by site. There is no evidence of punishment, sanctions, or explicit efficiency/payoff outcomes. The association between gifts received and herd size (wealth) is negative in Tibetan sites (gifts go to poorer herders) and absent in Norwegian sites.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report group efficiency or payoff outcomes. The design is adjacent to PGGs in that it involves allocation of resources in a social context, but the absence of punishment and efficiency outcomes means it offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task. - source: Quan, J., Nie, J. C., Chen, W. M., & Wang, X. J. (2022). Keeping or reversing social norms promote cooperation by enhancing indirect reciprocity. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Children aged 6-9 are more likely to cooperate with in-group members than with out-group members in an iterated face-to-face Prisoner's Dilemma Game. Girls generally cooperate more than boys, and the sensitivity to group membership varies by age and gender. Cooperation is also more likely following a partner's cooperative choice. The study does not include punishment, reward, or analyze efficiency or group payoff; all outcomes are behavioral (cooperation/defection).
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public-goods-game-like environments with or without punishment. It is relevant for understanding how group identity, gender, and age affect cooperation rates in dyadic social dilemmas, but does not address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency. It may be useful for contextualizing baseline cooperation rates in iterated dyadic games among children, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Zhang, M., Wang, S. Y., Hu, X. T., & Alfaro-Bittner, K. (2021). Local and global information affect cooperation in networked Prisoner's dilemma games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that providing players with global information about their opponents' strategies, or allowing them to choose between local and global information, increases the frequency of cooperation compared to providing only local information. In the local information condition, cooperation declines steadily over time. In the global and discretionary conditions, some sessions show a reversal with increasing cooperation in later rounds. Players strongly prefer global information when given a choice. The experiment does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it studies a networked Prisoner's Dilemma without punishment or reward. Its main relevance is indirect: it suggests that the type and scope of information available to players can influence cooperation rates, which may in turn affect efficiency in related games. However, since efficiency or payoff outcomes are not analyzed, and punishment is not present, this paper offers only weak, contextual support for the prediction task.

- source: Alberti, F., & Güth, W. (2013). Studying deception without deceiving participants: An experiment of deception experiments. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that strong incentives lead participants in the experimenter role to deceive others, but that this deception does not significantly affect the behavior of allocators or recipients in subsequent rounds, even after debriefing. There is no evidence that deception changes generosity or acceptance thresholds. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not involve punishment or sanction mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not involve punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes, and the game is a dictator game variant rather than a public goods game. The findings may be marginally informative for understanding the robustness of participant behavior to negative experiences (such as deception), but do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment or game design dimensions in PGGs.

- source: House, B., Silk, J. B., & McAuliffe, K. (2023). No strong evidence for universal gender differences in the development of cooperative behaviour across societies. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that gender has little impact on the development of prosociality and fairness in children aged 4-15 across a wide range of societies. In both the Dictator Game (measuring prosocial choice) and the Inequity Games (measuring aversion to advantageous and disadvantageous inequity), males and females behave similarly, with only small and unreliable differences observed. There is also little evidence for substantial societal variation in gender differences. The findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (choices, rejections) and do not address efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. No punishment or sanctioning mechanisms are present in the experimental designs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The games studied (Dictator Game, Inequity Games) are adjacent to PGGs but do not include group contributions, punishment, or efficiency/welfare outcomes. The findings are about gender and behavioral choices, not about game design dimensions or payoff-based outcomes. Therefore, this paper should not be used to inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGGs or similar environments.

- source: Miranda-Rodríguez, R. A., Leenen, I., Han, H., Palafox-Palafox, G., & García-Rodríguez, G. (2023). Moral reasoning and moral competence as predictors of cooperative behavior in a social dilemma. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in an iterated stranger PDG, the probability of cooperation is strongly influenced by the outcome of previous rounds: negative experiences (sucker, punishment, temptation) reduce cooperation, while positive experiences (reward) increase it. However, individuals with higher levels of moral reasoning (DIT-2 N2-index) and moral competence (MCT C-score) are less likely to reduce cooperation after negative experiences, particularly after sucker outcomes. There is no main effect of moral reasoning or competence on overall cooperation rates, but both moderate the effect of negative experiences. The study does not examine efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanction mechanism, nor does it analyze efficiency or group payoff. Its findings are limited to individual-level predictors of cooperation in a repeated PDG without punishment or reward. The study is only indirectly relevant for understanding baseline cooperation dynamics in the absence of punishment, and does not inform how punishment would affect efficiency or payoff outcomes under different game design dimensions.

- source: Candia, C., Oyarzún, M., Landaeta, V., Yaikin, T., Monge, C., Hidalgo, C., & Rodriguez-Sickert, C. (2022). Reciprocity heightens academic performance in elementary school students. *Heliyon*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that students who engage in more reciprocated cooperative relationships (as measured by a non-anonymous, continuous-token Prisoner's Dilemma game) tend to have higher subsequent academic performance (GPA). This association is robust to controls for prior GPA, attendance, social status, and other confounders, and is confirmed using a difference-in-differences approach. The effect is stronger for students in the top 20% of reciprocated cooperation. The experiment does not include punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes from the game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The experimental design lacks any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and the outcomes are not efficiency or payoff-based but rather focus on mapping social relationships and predicting academic performance. The game is a non-anonymous, dyadic, continuous-token social dilemma, which is adjacent to PGGs but not a direct variant. Thus, this paper should not be used to inform predictions about punishment effects on efficiency in PGGs or similar games.

- source: Smith, K. M. M., Mabulla, I. A. A., & Apicella, C. L. L. (2022). Hadza hunter-gatherers with greater exposure to other cultures share more with generous campmates. *Biology Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that Hadza participants are more likely to share with campmates they rank higher on generosity and, to a lesser extent, foraging ability. Importantly, the preference for generosity is significantly stronger among Hadza with greater exposure to other cultures. The effect of exposure is specific to generosity and does not extend to foraging ability. The results are interpreted as evidence for the flexibility of partner choice psychology, with socioecological context (exposure to other cultures) shaping the traits valued in social partners. The study does not involve punishment, sanctions, or analyze efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not include punishment or sanction mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. Its design (anonymous sharing game with individual allocation decisions) is adjacent to public goods games but lacks the core features of contribution to a group project, punishment, or efficiency analysis. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about punishment effects on efficiency in PGG-like settings.

- source: Garfield, Z. H., Ringen, E. J., Buckner, W., Medupe, D., Wrangham, R. W., & Glowacki, L. (2023). Norm violations and punishments across human societies. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=weak | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that evidence for punishment of norm violations is common across societies, with physical, material, and execution punishments each present in about a third of societies, and reputational punishments somewhat less common. The presence of reputational punishment is associated with egalitarianism and the absence of food storage, while material punishment is associated with food storage and animal husbandry. Execution punishment is moderately associated with social stratification. There is little evidence for strong phylogenetic (cultural history) effects, suggesting that punishment systems adapt quickly to local socioecology. The study does not analyze or report any efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, nor does it use experimental economic games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It is relevant for understanding the cross-cultural diversity of punishment systems and their socioecological correlates, but does not report on payoff-based outcomes or experimental manipulations of punishment. Its findings may be contextually informative for understanding the types of punishment that might arise in different socioecological settings, but it does not inform the quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in controlled game environments.

These effects are more likely when organizations set clear goals, establish trust, and manage cooperation dynamically. The research is based on surveys of Slovak enterprises and case studies of global business alliances. While the paper discusses outcomes such as increased economic value and competitiveness, it does not measure efficiency or group payoff in a game-theoretic or experimental sense, nor does it involve punishment or sanction mechanisms.

- source: Xiao, E., & Bicchieri, C. (2012). Words or deeds? Choosing what to know about others. *Synthese*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=weak | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that a majority of dictators (75%) choose to view free norm-revealing information (either empirical or normative) before making their allocation decision, but very few are willing to pay even a small cost (15 cents) to obtain such information. There is no significant difference in the demand for empirical versus normative information when presented separately. The experiment is a one-shot dictator game with no punishment or reward mechanisms, and the main focus is on information-seeking behavior, not on efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency-related outcomes. Its findings are only weakly relevant to the prediction task, as it focuses on information-seeking in a dictator game without any sanctioning mechanism. The only possible indirect relevance is the discussion of context-dependence of norm compliance, but this is not tied to efficiency or punishment.

- source: Przepiorka, W., & Aksoy, O. (2021). Does Herding Undermine the Trust Enhancing Effect of Reputation? An Empirical Investigation with Online-Auction Data. *Social Forces*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: show_other_summaries
  findings: Using a large dataset of eBay auctions, the study shows that sellers with more positive reputations are more likely to sell their items and at higher prices, while negative reputations reduce these outcomes. Buyers exhibit herding behavior, being more likely to bid on items that already have bids, but this effect peaks and then declines as the number of bidders increases. Importantly, even when herding, buyers still consider seller reputation, and the effect of reputation on sales and price remains significant. There is no evidence that herding undermines the efficiency or trust-enhancing effect of reputation systems in this market context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that reputation mechanisms (a form of informal sanctioning) increase efficiency in online market environments by raising both the probability of sale and the price sellers can obtain. While not a public goods game or direct punishment setting, the findings are highly relevant for predicting the effect of reputation-based sanctions on efficiency in environments where trust and cooperation are important. The results suggest that, even in the presence of herding (social influence), reputation systems continue to enhance efficiency. However, the market context differs from standard PGGs, so direct quantitative transfer should be cautious.

- source: Clavien, C., Tanner, C. J., Clément, F., & Chapuisat, M. (2012). Choosy Moral Punishers. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that the propensity to punish a norm violator (by voting against her) varies by socio-professional category: future teachers are significantly more likely to punish than future police officers or high school students, despite all groups recognizing the immoral behavior. The punishment is costless and anonymous, and the context is a simulated music competition, not an economic or public goods game. The findings challenge the idea of a universal, context-independent motivation to punish norm violators. No efficiency, payoff, or group welfare outcomes are measured or discussed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or similar economic games. Its findings are about context-specific willingness to punish in a non-economic, real-world scenario, and do not inform the relationship between punishment and efficiency or payoff. It may be relevant for understanding moderators of punishment motivation, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on efficiency in PGG-like environments.

- source: Galeotti, F., Maggian, V., & Villeval, M. C. (2021). FRAUD DETERRENCE INSTITUTIONS REDUCE INTRINSIC HONESTY. *Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: Exposure to centralized deterrence institutions (ticket inspections and sanctions) increases subsequent unethical behavior (dishonesty) in a different context, for both previous rule-breakers and compliers. The effect is robust to controls and is stronger with larger inspection teams, suggesting a possible normative or signaling mechanism. The study does not use a public goods game or report any efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The findings are about behavioral spillovers of punishment institutions, not about their effect on efficiency or group payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not use a PGG or variant, does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, and does not manipulate or report on any of the key PGG design dimensions. Its findings are about behavioral spillovers of deterrence institutions on individual honesty in unrelated contexts. Thus, it should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task, though it may be of conceptual interest for understanding broader effects of punishment institutions.
